\title{\bfseries The Fabius Function in Lean\\[0.35em]
\large A Guided Walkthrough of the \texttt{ProveIt} Formalization}
\author{Proof architecture, exact computation, asymptotics, and effective approximation\\
\small Human-readable guide to Vladimir Reshetnikov's \texttt{ProveIt} repository}
\date{Repository source snapshot: 25 August 2026\\
\small base commit \nolinkurl{eaea1b9afe2b7ff0b7dd880bd1710e303dec6d80};
Volterra addenda \nolinkurl{3da579387}, \nolinkurl{e109088ed};\\
\small activation-series and finite-Taylor addendum
\nolinkurl{a345425d21d90e680bf15e34093af42c69c08a83};\\

fixed-nome q-Pochhammer entire addendum, 1 September 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
The Fabius function is a classical example of a smooth function with an elementary-looking self-similarity law and unexpectedly rich arithmetic, Fourier-analytic, probabilistic, and asymptotic structure.  Vladimir Reshetnikov's \texttt{ProveIt} repository contains a large Lean~4 development that formalizes both of Juan Arias de Reyna's 2017 papers on the function, proves the existence and uniqueness of a canonical bounded solution, constructs its signed global extension, verifies exact evaluation algorithms at dyadic rationals, develops moment and denominator theory, reconstructs Rvachev's Fourier characterization, and goes substantially beyond the papers through q-binomial identities, discrete-limit theorems, sharp shape and inverse theory, finite activation Taylor jets, square-summable activation asymptotics, computability, non-elementarity results, corrected saddle-point asymptotics, and Legendre expansions.

This document is a proof-oriented walkthrough of that codebase.  Its aim is not to restate every declaration in source order.  Instead, it explains why the development is split into its present modules, which dependencies are mathematically essential, which are merely packaging dependencies, and how the principal proofs are made acceptable to Lean.  Particular attention is paid to the two parallel foundations of the project---exact rational arithmetic and analysis under an abstract \nolinkurl{IsFabius} specification---and to the bridge that identifies them.  The discussion also records source corrections that become unavoidable in a formal proof, recurring proof-engineering idioms, recommended reading paths, and practical advice for extending the library.
\end{abstract}

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Reader's map.}\space
A mathematically oriented reader should begin with Sections~\ref{ch:objects}, \ref{ch:existence}, \ref{ch:regularity}, \ref{ch:dyadic-bridge}, \ref{ch:fourier}, and \ref{ch:asymptotics}.  A Lean developer should read Sections~\ref{ch:build}, \ref{ch:architecture}, \ref{ch:arithmetic}, \ref{ch:engineering}, and \ref{ch:extending}.  Readers interested in the two source papers can use the theorem map in Appendix~\ref{app:paper-map}; readers trying to locate a particular implementation should use the annotated module guide in Appendix~\ref{app:modules}; and readers reproducing trust checks should use Appendix~\ref{app:reproduction}.
\end{boxedremark}

\section*{Scope and conventions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Scope and conventions}
The repository is active source code, and module boundaries can evolve.  Names and dependencies described here refer to commit \nolinkurl{eaea1b9afe2b7ff0b7dd880bd1710e303dec6d80} on the \texttt{codex/fabius-both-papers} branch, inspected on 25 August 2026.  The walkthrough quotes identifiers and very short fragments where useful, but it paraphrases proof bodies rather than reproducing them.  The exact Lean source remains the authoritative statement of hypotheses, namespaces, coercions, and imported lemmas.  Claims described as questions, conjectures, conditional results, audits, or counterexamples are not silently promoted here to proved positive theorems.
The only exceptions are passages explicitly labelled as post-snapshot and the
corresponding rows in Appendix~\ref{app:modules}; their individual source
checkpoints or current-tree inspection dates are recorded with each addendum.

Mathematically, two functions must be kept distinct throughout:
\begin{itemize}
  \item the bounded cumulative-distribution form \(F\colon\RealNumbers\to[0,1]\), equal to \(0\) on the left of the unit interval and to \(1\) on the right; and
  \item the compactly supported even Rvachev up-function \(\RvachevUp\), obtained by folding or differentiating the bounded form and naturally living near \([-1,1]\).
\end{itemize}
The code also defines a signed global extension of the bounded function.  It is this extension, rather than the clamped bounded map, that satisfies certain translation and dyadic identities for arbitrary real arguments.  Several apparent contradictions in informal accounts disappear once these three objects are not conflated.

Lean names are displayed in a monospaced face, for example \nolinkurl{Fabius.IsFabius}, \nolinkurl{Fabius.fabiusDyadicValue}, and \nolinkurl{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}.  Mathematical equations are written in conventional notation even when the formal theorem uses a more elaborated type, a subtype coercion, or a filter formulation.

Unless stated otherwise, empty finite sums are \(0\), empty finite products
are \(1\), and Lean's natural-power convention makes \(0^0=1\).  The Fourier
transform convention used in this walkthrough is
\[
  \FourierTwoPi{f}(\xi)=\int_{\RealNumbers}f(x)e^{-2\pi\ImaginaryUnit x\xi}\,\Differential x.
\]
These conventions matter at base indices and in every Poisson- or
Fourier-normalization check.

\section{Building and entering the development}\label{ch:build}

\subsection{Repository location and toolchain}
The relevant source tree is
\begin{center}
\nolinkurl{Analysis/FabiusFunction/Lean/}
\end{center}
and the umbrella import is \nolinkurl{FabiusFunction.lean}.  The Lake package declares a Lean library named \nolinkurl{FabiusFunction} whose source directory is the path above.  At the inspected snapshot, \nolinkurl{lean-toolchain} selects \nolinkurl{leanprover/lean4:v4.32.0}; \nolinkurl{lake-manifest.json} pins a corresponding mathlib revision and the transitive packages used by mathlib, including Aesop, Batteries, Qq, proof widgets, and the import-graph tooling.

The normal build command from the project root is:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
LAKE_JOBS=1 lake build +FabiusFunction
\end{lstlisting}
The source claims in this walkthrough are pinned to commit
\nolinkurl{eaea1b9afe2b7ff0b7dd880bd1710e303dec6d80}.  Before reproducing a
declaration or module count, check the checkout with \texttt{git rev-parse
HEAD}; a moving \texttt{main} branch is not a reproducible specification.
After dependencies have been installed, a focused edit loop can build one
module at a time with a Lake target such as
\texttt{LAKE\_JOBS=1 lake build
+FabiusFunction.DyadicAnalytic}.  The root-module build
remains the release check.

A useful first test file is:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
import FabiusFunction

open Fabius

#check IsFabius
#check fabius_spec
#check rvachevUp
#check fabiusDyadicValue
#check extendedFabius
\end{lstlisting}
Importing the umbrella module is convenient for exploration.  During development, importing a narrow leaf module is preferable: it shortens rebuilds, makes accidental dependencies visible, and helps preserve the intended layering.

\subsection{What the umbrella module exposes}
The top-level \nolinkurl{FabiusFunction.lean} does not contain the development itself.  It is an import façade.  Its principal imports collect six broad products:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item the formalizations of the two 2017 papers, through \nolinkurl{Paper05442.lean} and \nolinkurl{Paper06487.lean};
  \item the independent asymptotic project and the k-fold Thue--Morse audit, through \nolinkurl{PaperFabiusAsymptotic.lean} and \nolinkurl{PaperKFoldThueMorse.lean};
  \item negative-Laplace, Mellin, periodic-correction, and probability-transform interfaces;
  \item translated Legendre expansions, least-squares optimality, q-binomial binary reduction, and uniform spline approximation;
  \item the sharp shape API---monotonicity, convexity, optimal Lipschitz and derivative bounds, exact analytic loci, and endpoint flatness; and
  \item the totalized inverse, non-elementarity and inverse-branch obstructions, Grzegorczyk-style computability, closed saddle jets, and the explicit second saddle correction.
\end{enumerate}
This is a deliberate public surface.  The umbrella import answers the question ``what has the repository established?''  It is not the best answer to ``where is this theorem proved?''  For the latter, one should descend into the paper aggregators or the subject-specific module families.

\subsection{Four kinds of source module}
A useful architectural distinction is that a source file usually has one of
four roles:
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=2.8em]
  \item[Reusable infrastructure.] Files such as \nolinkurl{LegendrePolynomial.lean},
  \nolinkurl{LegendreSeriesConvergence.lean}, \nolinkurl{SaddleAllOrders.lean}, and the
  Gaussian-polynomial family develop machinery not tied to one canonical
  Fabius term.
  \item[Fabius-specific bridges.] Files such as \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean},
  \nolinkurl{AnalyticMoments.lean}, \nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean}, and
  \nolinkurl{EndpointLaplaceComparison.lean} identify two representations of the
  same object.  These are often the most informative files to read.
  \item[Paper-facing façades.] The four \nolinkurl{Paper*.lean} aggregators expose
  source claims in source vocabulary while delegating proofs to stronger
  internal declarations.
  \item[Audit and correction modules.] Files such as
  \nolinkurl{DraftCounterexamples.lean}, \nolinkurl{PoissonSummation.lean}, and
  \nolinkurl{FabiusWikipediaObstruction.lean} isolate a false printed claim or a
  missing hypothesis and expose the checked correction or obstruction.
\end{description}

\subsection{Naming conventions that encode intent}
Several recurring name patterns help locate the right abstraction:
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=2.8em]
  \item[Generic versus canonical.] A theorem parameterized by
  \nolinkurl{(F : BoundedFabius) (hF : IsFabius F)} applies to every implementation;
  a \nolinkurl{canonical_} or bare \nolinkurl{fabius_} wrapper specializes it to
  \nolinkurl{fabius} and \nolinkurl{fabius_spec}.
  \item[Exact-to-analytic casts.] Names ending in \nolinkurl{_cast} are the
  load-bearing bridges from rational computation to real analysis.
  \item[Convergence forms.] A \nolinkurl{HasSum} theorem records a convergence
  witness; a companion theorem phrased with \nolinkurl{tsum} usually provides the
  convenient equality.  Filter and asymptotic APIs use a similar strong-core,
  wrapper pattern.
  \item[Integer scales.] Names containing \nolinkurl{_zpow} use an integer exponent,
  which matters when a dyadic or asymptotic scale can cross \(1\).
  \item[Specifications.] Predicates such as \nolinkurl{IsFabius} and
  \nolinkurl{IsOriginalFabius} separate a characterization from a construction and
  permit independent existence and uniqueness routes.
\end{description}

\subsection{A practical exploration workflow}
A productive workflow in an editor with the Lean language server is:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item open the theorem used by a paper aggregator;
  \item inspect its type before its proof;
  \item use ``go to definition'' on the first nontrivial helper;
  \item note whether the helper belongs to the exact-arithmetic or analytic side of the project;
  \item follow imports upward only until the mathematical mechanism becomes clear; and
  \item return to the wrapper theorem to see how casts, side conditions, and notation are discharged.
\end{enumerate}
The wrapper theorem is often intentionally short.  The real content may be a sequence of modules: one establishing a recurrence over \(\NaturalNumbers\) or \(\RationalNumbers\), one proving an analytic recurrence for an arbitrary object satisfying \nolinkurl{IsFabius}, and one proving that both recurrences share the same initial values.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Architectural point.}\space
The development is easier to understand as a directed acyclic graph than as a directory listing.  Exact arithmetic starts without the canonical analytic function.  Generic analysis starts from an abstract specification rather than from a constructed term.  Only after both sides are mature does the code identify exact rational expressions with analytic values and package a canonical function.
\end{boxedremark}

\section{The mathematical objects and their Lean interfaces}\label{ch:objects}

\subsection{The bounded function}
The foundational module \nolinkurl{Basic.lean} introduces the closed unit interval as a subtype and uses it as the codomain of a bounded map.  Schematically, the first layer has the shape
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
abbrev UnitInterval := Set.Icc (0 : Real) 1
abbrev BoundedFabius := Real -> UnitInterval
\end{lstlisting}
The subtype codomain records the range bound in the type itself.  The projection to \(\RealNumbers\), which the code exposes through a convenience definition such as \nolinkurl{fabiusReal}, is used for calculus and integration.  This division of labor avoids reproving \(0\leq F(x)\leq1\) every time a measure-theoretic bound is required, while still giving the differentiable real-valued function expected by mathlib's analysis APIs.

The abstract predicate \nolinkurl{IsFabius} packages the characteristic properties of a candidate.  Its fields express, in essence:
\begin{align*}
 F(x)&=0 &&(x\leq0),\\
 F(x)&=1 &&(1\leq x),\\
 F(x)+F(1-x)&=1 &&(0\leq x\leq1),\\
 F'(x)&=2F(2x) &&(0\leq x\leq\tfrac12),
\end{align*}
together with global smoothness.  The precise formal structure uses pointwise derivative predicates and interval membership.  Later lemmas extend the symmetry law from the core interval to a statement valid for all real \(x\), with tail cases handled separately.

\subsubsection{Why specify before constructing?}
Most analytic theorems are stated for a variable \(F\) with hypothesis \nolinkurl{hF : IsFabius F}.  This is an important architectural choice.  It allows derivative identities, Taylor formulas, moment recurrences, and dyadic uniqueness to be proved without unfolding the fixed-point construction.  The existence module can then instantiate this generic API, while uniqueness is proved once at the level of the specification.

This organization resembles an interface-and-implementation pattern:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \nolinkurl{Basic.lean} supplies the interface;
  \item \nolinkurl{Differential.lean}, \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean}, and related modules prove generic consequences of the interface;
  \item \nolinkurl{Existence.lean} constructs \nolinkurl{Existence.boundedCandidate} and proves unique existence through \nolinkurl{existsUnique_fabius} and \nolinkurl{IsFabius.eq}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{PaperStatements.lean} selects the canonical term \nolinkurl{fabius} from that witness and exports \nolinkurl{fabius_spec}, letting later files forget the implementation again.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The Rvachev up-function}
The compactly supported function \(\RvachevUp\) is the symmetric ``twin'' of the bounded cumulative form.  The code defines \nolinkurl{rvachevUp} by folding the bounded function around the center of the unit interval.  Up to the chosen normalization and translation, the relation is the familiar one
\[
   \RvachevUp(x)=F(x+1) \quad (-1\leq x\leq0),
   \qquad
   \RvachevUp(x)=F(1-x) \quad (0\leq x\leq1),
\]
with zero outside the support.  Symmetry gives the equivalent positive-branch expression \(1-F(x)\) on the unit interval.  The exact Lean definition is arranged to make evenness and support proofs tractable and to match the Fourier normalization used in the original paper.

The up-function is the more natural object for Fourier analysis because it is even, smooth, compactly supported, and normalized to have integral one.  The bounded form is the more natural object for dyadic arithmetic because its derivative equation and boundary values generate finite Taylor recurrences.

\subsection{The signed global extension}
The bounded function cannot satisfy an unrestricted translation law: it is constant on both tails.  The repository therefore defines \nolinkurl{extendedFabius}, a signed global extension assembled as a locally finite sum of odd translates of \(\RvachevUp\), weighted by the Thue--Morse sign.  In the repository's normalization,
\[
  \FabiusGlobal(x)=\sum_{n\geq0}(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}\,\RvachevUp(x-2n-1).
\]
Only finitely many summands are nonzero at any given \(x\).  Lean nevertheless treats the expression through a \nolinkurl{tsum}, so the library must prove local finiteness, summability, and the reduction of the infinite sum to a finite set before elementary rearrangements are legal.

On the unit interval the extension agrees with the bounded function.  Away from that interval it carries the Thue--Morse block signs needed for global dyadic formulas.  This distinction is used pervasively in \nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean}, \nolinkurl{GlobalDyadic.lean}, and the q-binomial modules.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Formalization-sensitive point.}\space
When an informal statement writes \(F(x)\) for all real \(x\), inspect the theorem's target.  It may refer to the bounded map, the real projection of that map, the compactly supported up-function, or the signed extension.  Several corrected statements in the repository amount precisely to choosing the right one.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Auxiliary analytic objects}
\nolinkurl{Basic.lean} also reserves names for the analytic transforms that later modules identify:
\begin{itemize}
  \item a complex sinc function with a removable value at zero;
  \item the Fourier transform and infinite sinc product associated with \(\RvachevUp\);
  \item moment and half-moment generating series;
  \item real and complex versions of \((\exp z-1)/z\), again with the singularity removed; and
  \item integral definitions of moments over the support.
\end{itemize}
Defining these objects early stabilizes names and normalizations.  Proofs of convergence, equality, and differentiability are deferred to modules with the necessary imports.  This keeps \nolinkurl{Basic.lean} conceptually small even though it determines the vocabulary of the whole project.

\section{The dependency architecture}\label{ch:architecture}

\subsection{A layered view}
The following table suppresses many helper modules but captures the principal flow.  Arrows point from prerequisites to consumers.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash\bfseries}p{0.17\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{0.05\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
Layer & Principal inputs & Flow & Principal consumers \\
\midrule
Exact arithmetic & Arithmetic; parity; normalized moments; denominator bounds & $\longrightarrow$ & dyadic closed forms and the exact evaluator \\
Generic analysis & Basic and \nolinkurl{IsFabius}; differential, scale, and translation laws & $\longrightarrow$ & \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic}, the exact--analytic bridge \\
Canonical object & the bridge plus contraction-based existence and uniqueness & $\longrightarrow$ & analytic moments, the signed global extension, Fourier analysis, and probability \\
Advanced layers & exact evaluation, moments, and the global extension & $\longrightarrow$ & q-binomial limits, negative-Laplace asymptotics, paper façades, and Legendre theory \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Conceptual dependency flow.  The source contains finer-grained helper modules between nearly every displayed pair.}
\end{table}

\subsection{Why existence is not the first analytic file}
A newcomer might expect \nolinkurl{Existence.lean} to precede every theorem about the function.  Instead, the code first proves a great deal about an arbitrary \nolinkurl{IsFabius} object.  This is not circular.  The direction is:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item assume a candidate satisfies the abstract laws;
  \item derive its dyadic values and prove that any two candidates agree on a dense set;
  \item construct one candidate by a contraction mapping; and
  \item invoke the already-proved generic uniqueness theorem.
\end{enumerate}
This order isolates the hard analytic construction from the algebraic uniqueness mechanism.  It also means that the dyadic evaluator is not merely a computational afterthought: it participates in the proof that the canonical object is unique.

\subsection{Two independent uniqueness arguments}
The repository contains two conceptually different uniqueness proofs.

The first is adapted to the bounded specification.  The derivative and symmetry equations determine exact values at every dyadic rational.  Dyadics are dense, and the candidates are continuous, so agreement on dyadics implies agreement everywhere.

The second is adapted to Rvachev's original compactly supported characterization.  The derivative/refinement law is transformed into a Fourier-domain sinc equation.  Iteration forces the entire Fourier transform to equal an infinite sinc product.  Fourier injectivity on an appropriate smooth rapidly decreasing class then forces the original functions to coincide.

Keeping both arguments is valuable.  The dyadic proof is computational and feeds exact arithmetic; the Fourier proof mirrors the historical characterization and explains the infinite product.

\subsection{Paper aggregators versus theorem-producing modules}
Files named \nolinkurl{Paper05442.lean}, \nolinkurl{Paper06487.lean}, and \nolinkurl{PaperFabiusAsymptotic.lean} are indexes and audit records.  They import theorem-producing modules and expose a source-facing collection of results.  For example, \nolinkurl{PaperStatements.lean} gives declarations whose numbering and prose correspond to the arithmetic paper, but the actual proof of a denominator theorem may be distributed across \nolinkurl{NormalizedMoments.lean}, \nolinkurl{Parity.lean}, \nolinkurl{TwoAdic.lean}, and \nolinkurl{DenominatorBound.lean}.

This separation has three advantages:
\begin{itemize}
  \item source numbering does not dictate internal dependency boundaries;
  \item corrected hypotheses can be documented at the aggregator without contaminating low-level lemmas; and
  \item later developments can reuse strong internal theorems rather than wrappers tailored to a paper's notation.
\end{itemize}

\section{The exact-arithmetic foundation}\label{ch:arithmetic}

\subsection{Why the development begins over \texorpdfstring{\(\NaturalNumbers\) and \(\RationalNumbers\)}{N and Q}}
The module \nolinkurl{Arithmetic.lean} is deliberately independent of the canonical analytic function.  It defines the integer and rational sequences that later turn out to be moments, dyadic values, normalizing factors, and denominator bounds.  This has a profound practical advantage: exact identities can be proved using induction, finite sums, divisibility, and polynomial normalization without carrying measurability, differentiability, or convergence hypotheses.

The exact side is not just a transcription of closed formulas.  It supplies executable definitions.  One can evaluate a dyadic rational by normalization and recursion, and Lean's kernel can reduce the resulting rational expression.  The same definitions then serve as specifications for correctness theorems.

\subsection{Binary weight and the Thue--Morse sign}
The smallest combinatorial ingredients are the binary digit sum and its sign:
\[
  \BinaryDigitSum(n)=\text{number of ones in the binary expansion of }n,
  \qquad
  \varepsilon(n)=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}.
\]
In the code these appear under names such as \nolinkurl{binaryWeight} and \nolinkurl{thueMorseSign}.  Elementary identities under doubling and successor are proved early because they drive both the dyadic evaluator and the q-binomial development:
\[
  \BinaryDigitSum(2n)=\BinaryDigitSum(n),
  \qquad
  \BinaryDigitSum(2n+1)=\BinaryDigitSum(n)+1,
\]
so that \(\varepsilon(2n)=\varepsilon(n)\) and \(\varepsilon(2n+1)=-\varepsilon(n)\).

Lean's natural-number division and remainder operations require explicit control of positivity.  Proofs that are a one-line observation on binary expansions often become a short cluster of lemmas about \nolinkurl{Nat.div}, \nolinkurl{Nat.mod}, powers of two, and bit constructors.  Establishing this API once prevents the higher-level files from repeatedly descending to bit arithmetic.

\subsection{Prime-power Pascal-row valuations}
The exhaustive public surface of \nolinkurl{PrimePowerBinomialValuation.lean}
consists of three theorems.  For a prime \(p\), a natural exponent \(m\), and
\(0<j\leq p^m\),
\[
  \nu_p\!\binom{p^m}{j}+\nu_p(j)=m,
  \qquad
  \nu_p\!\binom{p^m}{j}=m-\nu_p(j).
\]
These are \nolinkurl{primePowerChoose_padicValNat_add} and
\nolinkurl{primePowerChoose_padicValNat}; they include \(j=p^m\) and the
boundary \(m=0\).  The strict dyadic specialization \(0<j<2^m\) is
\nolinkurl{twoPowChoose_padicValNat}.  No endpoint-flat
\(\binom{2^m-2}{j-1}\) valuation or valuation-histogram theorem is claimed.

\subsection{Product normalizations}
Several finite products recur in moment denominators.  The arithmetic module names them rather than expanding them at every use.  Principal examples include variants of
\[
  \prod_{j=1}^{n}(2^j-1),
  \qquad
  \prod_{j=1}^{n}(2^{2j}-1),
  \qquad
  (2n-1)!!.
\]
The source uses names such as \nolinkurl{mersenneProduct}, \nolinkurl{evenMersenneProduct}, and \nolinkurl{oddDoubleFactorial}.  Their basic positivity, nonvanishing, splitting, and divisibility properties are the denominator-management toolkit for later proofs.

A formal proof benefits from keeping two versions of a formula:
\begin{itemize}
  \item a mathematically transparent quotient in \(\RationalNumbers\); and
  \item a division-free equality in \(\NaturalNumbers\), obtained after multiplying by a common product.
\end{itemize}
The quotient is convenient for stating a moment recurrence.  The division-free equality is the robust object for induction, parity, and divisibility, because it avoids repeated side goals asserting that denominators are nonzero.

\subsection{Moment and half-moment recurrences}
The exact sequences \nolinkurl{moment} and \nolinkurl{halfMoment} are defined recursively as rational numbers.  The first models the even moments \(\int_{\RealNumbers}x^{2n}\RvachevUp(x)\,\Differential x\).  The second has normalized base value \(d_0=1\) and, for \(n\geq0\), models
\[
  d_{n+1}=(n+1)\int_0^1 t^n\RvachevUp(t)\,\Differential t.
\]
Its connection to the bounded function's inverse-power values is derived afterward rather than built into the definition.  Both exact definitions mirror recurrences obtained analytically by integration by parts and scaling.

Alongside them, \nolinkurl{momentNumerator} and \nolinkurl{halfMomentNumerator} are natural-number recurrences with the common denominators cleared.  Their role is easy to underestimate.  They provide:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item a finite, executable representation of exact values;
  \item a target for strong-induction proofs of closed forms;
  \item a clean setting for parity arguments; and
  \item a bridge to reduced rational numerators and denominators.
\end{enumerate}

A recurring proof pattern is to start from the rational recurrence, multiply by the anticipated global normalizing product, distribute that product through a finite sum, and use factorial or Mersenne product splitting to identify every term with an earlier natural numerator.  Once the normalizations are designed correctly, the final goal is a polynomial identity in natural numbers and powers of two.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Lean pattern.}\space
For exact rational identities, the development usually proves all denominator factors positive or nonzero near the beginning of the proof, rewrites divisions into a common denominator, and invokes \nolinkurl{field_simp} only after the relevant nonzero facts are available.  The remaining equality is discharged by a combination of finite-sum rewrites, \nolinkurl{ring_nf}, and induction hypotheses.  Calling \nolinkurl{field_simp} too early tends to produce enormous and poorly shaped goals.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Closed dyadic formulas}
The exact values at inverse powers of two are represented by a sequence such as \nolinkurl{halfMomentFabiusValue}.  The source-facing closed formula \nolinkurl{fabiusDyadic} is total on natural numerators and is a finite sum built from Thue--Morse signs, even binomial coefficients, and the exact moment sequence.  After casting, it equals the bounded Fabius value at \(a/2^n\) when \(a\leq2^n\), and the signed global extension at that argument for arbitrary natural \(a\).  This closed formula is distinct from the inverse-power table used by the faster Horner evaluator.

The formalization maintains a distinction between:
\begin{itemize}
  \item a closed formula suited to mathematical theorems;
  \item a Horner form suited to evaluation;
  \item a unit-interval evaluator that clamps and removes highest set bits; and
  \item a global evaluator that applies triangular reduction on two-unit cells and a Thue--Morse block sign.
\end{itemize}
This separation makes correctness modular.  One first proves that the Horner program computes the finite polynomial, then that recursive bit reduction computes the closed formula, then that the closed formula equals the analytic function.

\subsection{The inverse-power table}
The function \nolinkurl{fabiusInversePowTwoTable} computes a prefix of exact values
\[
  F(1),F(2^{-1}),F(2^{-2}),\ldots,F(2^{-n}),
\]
so the array has size \(n+1\).  A table-based implementation avoids recomputing the same recurrence at every recursive step.  Correctness is expressed through prefix stability: extending a table preserves all previously computed entries.  This is essential because the evaluator may request values in an order dictated by the bits of the numerator rather than by a single monotone loop.

Formal prefix stability is expressed through array size and optional
\nolinkurl{Array.getElem?} lookup, together with compatibility of
\nolinkurl{Array.push} and the defining recurrence.
Such lemmas look implementation-specific, but they prevent computational code from leaking into the mathematical correctness theorem.

\subsection{Horner evaluation}
The local Taylor polynomial is evaluated by \nolinkurl{fabiusTaylorHorner}.  Horner's rule is not only computationally efficient; it is proof-friendly.  The correctness theorem is an induction over the coefficient list, and the induction invariant is a suffix polynomial.  In contrast, a direct power-sum implementation forces repeated reasoning about exponents, list indices, and truncated ranges.

The coefficients are rational and involve powers of two coming from iterated differentiation.  The proof therefore includes cast-normalization lemmas ensuring that a natural exponent or binomial coefficient has the same interpretation in \(\NaturalNumbers\), \(\RationalNumbers\), and \(\RealNumbers\).  The code frequently uses explicit type annotations at precisely these interfaces; their apparent verbosity documents where coercion inference would otherwise be ambiguous.

\subsection{Recursive removal of the highest bit}
At the center of the evaluator is a structurally terminating function such as \nolinkurl{fabiusDyadicUnitAux}.  Given a dyadic numerator \(a\) and exponent \(n\), it removes the highest set bit of \(a\).  Write
\[
  a=2^b+r,
  \qquad 0\leq r<2^b.
\]
A Taylor identity expresses the value at \(a/2^n\) using the value and inverse-power data associated with the smaller remainder \(r\).  Since \(r<a\) whenever \(a>0\), the recursive call decreases in a well-founded order.

This is the computational counterpart of traversing the ones in the binary expansion of \(a\).  The number of recursive Taylor reductions is therefore controlled by \(\BinaryDigitSum(a)\), not by the magnitude of \(a\) itself.  Together with precomputation of the inverse-power table, the design yields the approximate complexity described in the repository documentation: a quadratic table phase in the exponent and a bit-weight-dependent evaluation phase.

Lean does not accept ``the highest bit was removed'' as a termination argument.  The source proves a chain of inequalities connecting the bit logarithm, the largest power of two not exceeding \(a\), and the remainder.  This chain is packaged so the recursive definition can use a measure.  The correctness proof later reuses the very same decomposition, an example of an implementation lemma doing double duty as a mathematical induction lemma.

\subsection{Clamping, reflection, and the global evaluator}
The unit-interval wrapper \nolinkurl{fabiusDyadicUnit} clamps natural numerators at the right endpoint and delegates interior points to highest-bit recursion; it does not perform reflection.  The rational-valued \nolinkurl{fabiusDyadicValue} accepts an integer numerator and power-of-two denominator, returning \(0\) for nonpositive inputs and \(1\) at or beyond the right endpoint.  Separate refinement theorems prove that equivalent unreduced representations give the same result.

For the signed extension, \nolinkurl{extendedFabiusDyadicValue} reduces the numerator modulo a two-unit grid length, reflects the residue into the unit cell, and applies the Thue--Morse sign of the block index.  This is triangular cell reduction, not global periodicity.  Multiplying numerator and denominator by a common power of two leaves the output unchanged.  Refinement invariance is proved explicitly rather than delegated to quotient types, because the evaluator's input is computational data, not an abstract element of a dyadic localization.

\begin{proofidea}
There are three distinct notions of normalization:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item arithmetic normalization of a fraction by canceling powers of two;
  \item bounded normalization into \([0,1]\) using the constant tails; and
  \item global triangular cell reduction, with a Thue--Morse block sign.
\end{enumerate}
The code proves each normalization preserves the intended mathematical value before composing them.
\end{proofidea}

\subsection{Recognizing dyadic rationals}
The definitions \nolinkurl{IsDyadicRational} and \nolinkurl{dyadicExponent?} support a user-facing exact evaluator for rational inputs.  A rational is dyadic when its reduced denominator is a power of two.  The decision procedure must connect:
\begin{itemize}
  \item the internal normalized numerator and positive denominator of \(\RationalNumbers\);
  \item a Boolean or optional power-of-two test; and
  \item a proposition asserting the existence of an integer numerator and natural exponent.
\end{itemize}
The proof that recognition is sound is separate from the proof that the returned exponent and numerator evaluate correctly.  This makes failure meaningful: a non-dyadic rational is rejected because no representation with a power-of-two denominator exists, not merely because a search limit was reached.

\subsection{Arithmetic API design lessons}
The exact core illustrates several design principles that recur throughout the repository.

First, executable functions and theorem-friendly formulas need not be identical definitions.  They are connected by named correctness lemmas.  Second, normalization data should be made explicit, especially when a mathematical quotient hides representation choices.  Third, the strongest useful invariant is often not the final equality but a stability statement about prefixes, refinements, or common denominators.  Finally, natural-number recurrences are frequently the right interface for arithmetic theorems even when the motivating quantities are rational.

\section{Constructing the canonical bounded function}\label{ch:existence}

\subsection{The ambient complete metric space}
The construction in \nolinkurl{Existence.lean} uses Banach's fixed-point theorem.  Let
\[
  I=[0,1],
  \qquad
  C=C(I,\RealNumbers)
\]
with the uniform metric.  Since the codomain is complete and \(I\) is compact, the continuous-map space is complete.  The code then cuts out a closed subset of \(C\) consisting of functions satisfying two elementary conditions:
\begin{align*}
  0&\leq f(x)\leq1,\\
  f(x)+f(1-x)&=1.
\end{align*}
The source calls the reflection operator and the admissibility predicate by local names and proves that the admissible set is closed.  A linear function, essentially \(x\mapsto x\), witnesses nonemptiness.

Using a closed subspace rather than the entire continuous-function space is decisive.  Symmetry is not recovered after the fixed point; it is an invariant of the contraction.  This makes the integral normalization automatic and improves the contraction constant from the naive value one to one half.

\subsection{Extending an interval function}
An element of \(C(I,\RealNumbers)\) accepts a subtype argument.  To integrate it over an ordinary real interval, the code defines an extension by projecting an arbitrary real number to \(I\).  This projection clamps values below zero to zero and values above one to one.  Continuity follows from continuity of the metric projection to a closed convex interval.

For an admissible \(f\), define the cumulative integral
\[
  A_f(y)=\int_0^y \ClampedExtensionOf{f}(t)\,\Differential t,
\]
where \(\ClampedExtensionOf{f}\) is the clamped extension.  Symmetry implies
\[
  \int_0^1 f(t)\,\Differential t=\frac12.
\]
The Lean proof reflects the integral by the substitution \(t\mapsto1-t\), adds the two copies, and uses the pointwise symmetry equation.  The subtype coercions and interval maps are handled in helper lemmas so the final normalization proof resembles the paper calculation.

\subsection{The nonlinear-looking transform that is actually affine}
The fixed-point transform is defined piecewise at the midpoint:
\[
  (Tf)(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    A_f(2x),&0\leq x\leq\tfrac12,\\[2mm]
    1-A_f(2-2x),&\tfrac12\leq x\leq1.
  \end{cases}
\]
The two branches agree at \(x=1/2\) because \(A_f(1)=1/2\).  Each branch is continuous, and the gluing lemma supplies continuity of \(Tf\) on the whole interval.  Bounds follow because \(f\) is bounded between zero and one; symmetry follows by exchanging the two branches.

The transform is affine on the admissible set.  The constants in the right branch cancel when two transformed functions are subtracted.  This is why the contraction estimate can be proved through integrals of \(f-g\).

\subsection{The contraction estimate}
Let \(h=f-g\) for two admissible functions.  Symmetry gives
\[
  \int_0^1 h(t)\,\Differential t=0.
\]
For \(0\leq y\leq1\), the prefix integral may be estimated in two ways:
\[
  \left|\int_0^y h\right|\leq y\lVert h\rVert_\infty,
  \qquad
  \left|\int_0^y h\right|
   =\left|\int_y^1 h\right|
   \leq(1-y)\lVert h\rVert_\infty.
\]
Therefore
\[
  \left|\int_0^y h\right|
  \leq \min(y,1-y)\lVert h\rVert_\infty
  \leq\frac12\lVert h\rVert_\infty.
\]
This is the key insight of the construction.  A crude estimate would give constant one and no contraction.  The zero-total-integral identity lets the proof select the shorter of the prefix and complementary suffix.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Lean pattern.}\space
The formal estimate is assembled from interval-integral norm bounds rather than from an informal ``length times supremum'' principle.  The code proves integrability of the extended difference, obtains a pointwise uniform bound from the distance in the continuous-map space, applies the interval integral inequality on both subintervals, and rewrites the total integral as zero.  A final case split on \(y\leq1/2\) selects the useful bound.
\end{boxedremark}

Banach's theorem now yields a unique fixed point in the admissible subspace.  The source extracts its underlying continuous map and extends it to a bounded real function with constant tails.

\subsection{From an integral fixed point to the differential law}
A fixed point satisfies an integral equation.  On the left half,
\[
  F(x)=\int_0^{2x}F(t)\,\Differential t.
\]
For the probability CDF this identity is actually valid for every
$x\leq1/2$, not only for $x\in[0,1/2]$: below zero both the CDF and the
oriented integral vanish.  Lean exposes this stronger form as
\nolinkurl{weightedSumCDF_eq_intervalIntegral_of_le_half} and retains
\nolinkurl{weightedSumCDF_left_formula} as the interval-shaped compatibility
wrapper.
The fundamental theorem of calculus and the chain rule give
\[
  F'(x)=2F(2x).
\]
On the right half, differentiation of the reflected branch yields the corresponding equation through symmetry.  The proof handles the midpoint and endpoints separately because standard derivative rules apply most directly on open neighborhoods, while the desired structure requests derivatives at boundary points as well.

The source uses \nolinkurl{HasDerivAt} statements whenever possible.  Such statements compose cleanly through affine maps and integral primitives; a theorem about \nolinkurl{deriv} alone would require differentiability side conditions each time it is rewritten.  Endpoint derivatives are obtained by showing that the formulas from adjacent pieces agree, using the tail values and symmetry.

\subsection{Bootstrapping smoothness}
The fixed-point theorem initially gives only continuity.  The integral equation gives one derivative.  The derivative is a scaled composition of the original continuous function, so it is continuous.  Iterating this observation upgrades the function to every finite differentiability order.

The formal proof is organized around reusable scale-and-derivative lemmas rather than a single enormous induction on \nolinkurl{ContDiff}.  At stage \(n+1\), the derivative formula expresses the new derivative in terms of a scaled version of the \(n\)-th derivative.  Boundary compatibility follows from vanishing on the tails and symmetry.  The final theorem packages all finite stages as \(C^\infty\).

\subsection{Uniqueness by dyadic density}
The contraction theorem already gives uniqueness inside the admissible continuous space.  The exported specification, however, is phrased for real functions satisfying \nolinkurl{IsFabius}; it is useful to prove uniqueness at this abstract level.  The later dyadic analysis shows that every such function has the same exact value at every dyadic point.  Since dyadics are dense in \(\RealNumbers\) and both candidates are continuous, the functions coincide.

The proof has the standard topological shape: approximate an arbitrary \(x\) by a sequence of dyadics, rewrite equality at each sequence term, and pass to the limit by continuity.  The nontrivial work lies earlier, in proving exact dyadic equality without assuming the fixed-point implementation.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Architectural point.}\space
The fixed-point construction proves that the interface is inhabited.  The dyadic theory proves that the interface is subsingleton.  This yields a canonical object whose public properties are independent of the construction details.
\end{boxedremark}

\section{Differential identities and the signed global extension}\label{ch:differential}

\subsection{The role of \texttt{Differential.lean}}
\nolinkurl{Differential.lean} is the calculus engine for an arbitrary \nolinkurl{IsFabius} candidate.  Starting from the first derivative law on the left half and the globalized symmetry law, it derives corresponding formulas on other intervals and iterates the derivative relation.

The characteristic coefficient after \(n\) differentiations is a triangular power of two.  In a typical region where all scaled arguments remain in the appropriate branch, one obtains
\[
  F^{(n)}(x)
  =2^{1+2+\cdots+n}F(2^n x)
  =2^{n(n+1)/2}F(2^n x).
\]
The exact theorem includes domain conditions ensuring that each intermediate scaled point lies in the branch where the derivative law is valid.  Reflection introduces signs and translated arguments on the other half.

These iterated formulas explain three later phenomena at once:
\begin{itemize}
  \item finite Taylor formulas at dyadic points;
  \item flatness at the support boundary; and
  \item extremely rapid decay near zero.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Domain bookkeeping}
An informal derivation often differentiates the relation \(F'(x)=2F(2x)\) without restating its domain.  Lean forces the domain to be propagated.  If a theorem is iterated \(n\) times, the proof must establish that \(2^j x\) belongs to the relevant interval for every \(j<n\).  The source packages this as inequalities involving powers of two and dyadic cells.

This bookkeeping is not bureaucratic.  One of the asymptotic draft corrections later in the repository arises because a transformed delay-differential identity is valid only beyond a threshold.  The formal derivative theorem makes the threshold impossible to omit.

\subsection{Scale and translation lemmas}
\nolinkurl{ScaleTranslation.lean} collects chain-rule identities for functions of the form
\[
  x\longmapsto c\,F(ax+b),
  \qquad
  x\longmapsto c\,\RvachevUp(ax+b).
\]
These are used by approximation arguments, global extension formulas, Fourier transforms, and splines.  The value of a dedicated module is that every later proof can invoke a theorem with all factors of \(a\), signs, and differentiability hypotheses already correct.

A common Lean mistake is to rewrite an iterated derivative of a composition as though the affine map contributed only one factor of \(a\).  The library's lemmas state the full \(a^n\) factor.  Specialized powers-of-two corollaries then combine this with the triangular coefficient from the Fabius differential law.

\subsection{Local finiteness of the translate sum}
\nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean} proves that the signed translate series defining \(\FabiusGlobal\) is harmless.  Because \(\RvachevUp\) has compact support, only finitely many natural indices \(n\) can satisfy \(x-2n-1\in[-1,1]\) for fixed \(x\).  More strongly, on a compact neighborhood of \(x\), a single finite set of odd translates contains every nonzero summand.

That stronger local statement is what permits termwise continuity and differentiation.  The proof turns support inequalities into natural-number bounds and proves every index outside a finite range contributes zero.  The \nolinkurl{tsum} can then be rewritten as a finite sum using a summability-by-finite-support lemma.

\subsection{Agreement and global identities}
Once local finiteness is available, the signed extension is shown to agree with the bounded function on \([0,1]\).  On every cell \(2b\leq x\leq2b+2\), exactly one translate survives and
\[
  \FabiusGlobal(x)=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(b)}\,\RvachevUp(x-2b-1).
\]
The case \(b=0\), together with the defining fold relation, gives unit-interval agreement.

The extension is therefore a sequence of two-unit cells, not a globally periodic function: the sign of cell \(b\) is the Thue--Morse value \((-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(b)}\).  Scale-and-translation theorems record the precise identities on their valid blocks.  \nolinkurl{GlobalDyadic.lean} combines this cell structure with exact values to remove assumptions that a numerator be reduced or lie between zero and its denominator.

\subsection{Global differential calculus}
Local finiteness also permits termwise differentiation and even--odd
reindexing.  For every \(x\in\RealNumbers\), the theorems
\nolinkurl{extendedFabius_hasDerivAt} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_extendedFabius} give
\[
  \FabiusGlobal'(x)=2\FabiusGlobal(2x).
\]
Iterating this identity yields \nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv_extendedFabius}:
for every \(k\in\NaturalNumbers\) and \(x\in\RealNumbers\),
\[
  \FabiusGlobal^{(k)}(x)
  =2^{\binom{k+1}{2}}\FabiusGlobal(2^k x).
\]
Unlike the bounded derivative formula above, these identities require no
branch-domain hypothesis.

\subsection{Exact Taylor reduction}
On its initial block, \nolinkurl{extendedFabius_add_pow_two} says that
\[
  \FabiusGlobal(x+2^r)=-\FabiusGlobal(x)
  \qquad(r\geq1,\ 0\leq x\leq2^r).
\]
A derivative version extends this sign change to an integer scale \(s\)
whenever the derivative order \(m\) is high enough that \(0\leq s+m\).
Under that condition, \nolinkurl{taylorRemainder_translate} packages the exact
cancellation of the two adjacent integral Taylor remainders.  Write \(R_m(y)\)
for \nolinkurl{fabiusReductionSum m y}.  If \(n\in\IntegerNumbers\),
\(x>0\),
\[
  2^{-n}\leq x<2^{-n+1},
  \qquad y=x-2^{-n},
\]
then \nolinkurl{extendedFabius_reduction}, exported as
\nolinkurl{proposition_ten}, states
\[
  \FabiusGlobal(x)=-\FabiusGlobal(y)+
  \begin{cases}
    R_{\operatorname{toNat}(n)}(y),& n\geq0,\\
    0,& n<0.
  \end{cases}
\]
Thus the finite correction for \(n\geq0\) is an exact Taylor polynomial,
not a truncated approximation with an estimated remainder; for \(n<0\),
the formula reduces directly to the power-of-two sign-translation law.

\subsection{Why use a \texttt{tsum} for a locally finite expression?}
A hand proof would select the unique active odd translate on each nonnegative two-unit cell.  The repository instead uses a conceptually uniform sum over all natural indices.  This pays off in translation and differentiation arguments: the same summand family can be manipulated with standard infinite-sum lemmas.  The initial cost is a local-finiteness API; the later algebra becomes much cleaner.

\section{Sharp shape, inverse branches, and effective computation}\label{ch:regularity}

This part of the public API gathers consequences that were once scattered
among paper wrappers into a reusable shape theory, then uses that theory to
construct the inverse and a certified computable evaluator.

\subsection{One global derivative equation}
The key simplification is the identity
\[
  F'(x)=2\,\RvachevUp(2x-1)\qquad(x\in\RealNumbers),
\]
proved for every \nolinkurl{IsFabius} candidate.  Since \(\RvachevUp\geq0\), the bounded
function is globally monotone.  The strict theory in
\nolinkurl{Monotonicity.lean} proves that \(F\) is strictly increasing on
\([0,1]\) and maps that interval bijectively to itself.  On the folded side,
\(\RvachevUp\) is positive exactly on \((-1,1)\), increases strictly to its unique
maximum \(1\) at \(0\), and then decreases strictly.

\nolinkurl{Convexity.lean} feeds this unimodality back through the derivative
equation.  The derivative of \(F\) increases up to \(x=1/2\) and decreases
afterward, with exact range \([0,2]\) and unique maximum \(2\) at the
midpoint.  Consequently \(F\) is convex on the left half-line and concave on
the right, strictly so on the two open halves of its transition interval.
The same module now identifies the curvature pointwise:
\[
  F''(x)=8\bigl(\RvachevUp(4x-1)-\RvachevUp(4x-3)\bigr).
\]
The declarations \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal_pos_iff},
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal_neg_iff}, and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusReal_eq_zero_iff} say respectively that this is
positive exactly on \((0,1/2)\), negative exactly on \((1/2,1)\), and zero
off \((0,1)\) or at the midpoint.

\subsection{Sharp regularity and flatness}
\nolinkurl{Regularity.lean} proves that \(2\) is not merely a convenient Lipschitz
constant: it is the least global Lipschitz constant for both \(F\) and
\(\RvachevUp\).  The scaling recurrence then yields, for every \(k\geq0\),
\[
  \bigl|F^{(k)}(x)\bigr|\leq 2^{\binom{k+1}{2}},
\]
with equality attained at \(x=2^{-k}\) for positive order.  The signed
extension and the up-function have parallel sharp bounds in
\nolinkurl{GlobalBounds.lean}; \nolinkurl{BoundedDerivatives.lean} transfers them to
the clamped function without confusing its constant right tail with the
signed extension.

The effective endpoint bounds are stronger than the qualitative assertion
that every positive derivative vanishes.  If \(x\geq0\) and \(2^n x\leq1\),
\nolinkurl{SharpFlatness.lean} proves
\[
  F(x)\leq \frac{2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{n!}\,x^n.
\]
Reflection gives the analogous estimate for \(1-F(x)\) at the right endpoint.
The scale condition is part of the theorem; the family is not a single bound
uniform in \(n\).

\subsection{The totalized inverse}
Strict monotonicity packages the restriction of \(F\) to \([0,1]\) as the
order isomorphism \nolinkurl{fabiusOrderIso}.  The definition \nolinkurl{fabiusInv}
uses the inverse order isomorphism on that interval and \nolinkurl{IccExtend} to
clamp all real inputs: it is \(0\) on the left tail and \(1\) on the right.
The module \nolinkurl{FabiusInverse.lean} proves both inverse identities on the unit
interval, global continuity and monotonicity, endpoint reflection, and exact
inversion of every value produced by the bounded dyadic evaluator.

The same module now packages the first-order interior calculus and the exact
global smoothness locus.  On \(0<y<1\),
\nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusInv_eq_inv_two_mul_rvachevUp}, together with
\nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusInv_pos}, gives
\[
  (F^{-1})'(y)
    =\frac{1}{F'(F^{-1}(y))}
    =\frac{1}{2\RvachevUp(2F^{-1}(y)-1)}>0,
\]
and \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_contDiffOn_Ioo} proves \(C^\infty\) smoothness on the
open unit interval.  Because the totalization is locally constant on both
open tails, \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_contDiffAt_infty_iff} sharpens this to an
exact global statement: the inverse is \(C^\infty\) at \(y\) if and only if
\(y\neq0\) and \(y\neq1\).  Likewise,
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_differentiableAt_iff} says that it is differentiable
exactly away from the two clamping endpoints, with
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_not_differentiableAt_zero} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_not_differentiableAt_one} providing the endpoint
obstructions.  More generally, \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_contDiffAt_iff}
classifies every order bounded by \(C^\infty\): order zero, namely
continuity, holds globally, whereas every positive finite order and
\(C^\infty\) hold exactly off \(\{0,1\}\).  Its order hypothesis deliberately
excludes the stronger analytic order.  The divergent one-sided difference
quotients quantify the endpoint failure.

The next derivative is also packaged exactly.  The declarations
\nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusInv_hasDerivAt} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv} prove
\[
  (F^{-1})''(y)
    =-\frac{F''(F^{-1}(y))}{F'(F^{-1}(y))^3}
    \qquad(0<y<1).
\]
At the midpoint the exact jet is
\[
  F^{-1}(1/2)=1/2,\qquad (F^{-1})'(1/2)=1/2,\qquad
  (F^{-1})''(1/2)=0,
\]
by \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_half}, \nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusInv_half}, and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_half}.  On \(0<y<1\), the declarations
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_neg_iff},
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_pos_iff}, and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusInv_eq_zero_iff} identify the negative,
positive, and zero loci as \((0,1/2)\), \((1/2,1)\), and \(\{1/2\}\).
The reciprocal reverses the monotonicity of \(F'\).  Consequently
\nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_fabiusInv_firstHalf} proves strict concavity on the
closed interval \([0,1/2]\), while
\nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_fabiusInv_secondHalf} proves strict convexity on
\([1/2,1]\).  These closed-interval shape theorems use differentiability only
on the interiors, so they are compatible with the singular endpoint slopes.

The downstream module \nolinkurl{InverseModulus.lean} packages the sharp
global modulus.  Its increment
\nolinkurl{fabiusIntervalMass} is reflection-invariant and globally unimodal
for every nonnegative length, by
\nolinkurl{fabiusIntervalMass_reflect},
\nolinkurl{fabiusIntervalMass_eq_zero_of_add_nonpos}, and
\nolinkurl{fabiusIntervalMass_eq_zero_of_one_le}; the strict rise/fall on the
nondegenerate support window is
\nolinkurl{strictMonoOn_fabiusIntervalMass_firstHalf} and
\nolinkurl{strictAntiOn_fabiusIntervalMass_secondHalf}.
Hence the endpoint comparison has both strict and equality classifications:
\nolinkurl{fabiusReal_lt_fabiusIntervalMass_of_mem_Ioo},
\nolinkurl{fabiusIntervalMass_eq_fabiusReal_iff},
\nolinkurl{fabiusReal_sub_lt_sub}, and
\nolinkurl{fabiusReal_sub_eq_sub_iff}; the additive equality form is
\nolinkurl{fabiusReal_add_eq_iff}.
The global and unit-interval inverse equality loci are
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_sub_eq_sub_iff_of_mem_Icc} and
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq_iff_of_mem_Icc}, respectively.  These
refinements preserve the clamping caveat: the complete equality classification
is stated on the unit interval, while the global statements retain only the
sharp inequalities and exact attained moduli.
The same shape results also give
\nolinkurl{monotoneOn_fabiusIntervalMass_firstHalf}, and
\nolinkurl{antitoneOn_fabiusIntervalMass_secondHalf}.  Hence
\nolinkurl{fabiusReal_sub_le_sub} and \nolinkurl{fabiusReal_add_le} prove the
least endpoint mass and constrained forward superadditivity.  The three
ordered/order-free inverse-gap forms are
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub_of_mem_Icc},
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub_of_le}, and
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_sub_le_sub}; \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_add_le} gives global
inverse subadditivity.  In particular,
\[
 \lvert F^{-1}(u)-F^{-1}(v)\rvert
 \le F^{-1}(\lvert u-v\rvert)
\]
for arbitrary real inputs, exactly
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le} (with metric spelling
\nolinkurl{dist_fabiusInv_le}).  Saturation is
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_min_one}.  The attained global and unit-interval maxima
and their supremum equalities are
\nolinkurl{isGreatest_abs_fabiusInv_sub},
\nolinkurl{sSup_abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq},
\nolinkurl{isGreatest_abs_fabiusInv_sub_Icc}, and
\nolinkurl{sSup_abs_fabiusInv_sub_Icc_eq}.  Finally,
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_of_abs_sub_lt_fabiusReal},
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_of_abs_sub_le_fabiusReal},
\nolinkurl{fabiusReal_le_abs_sub_of_le_abs_fabiusInv_sub}, and
\nolinkurl{forall_abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_iff} give the strict, closed,
contrapositive, and exact-threshold effective-injectivity forms.  This module
does not supply recursive-modulus packaging or an inversion algorithm.

\nolinkurl{FabiusInverseEffectiveContinuity.lean} supplies the explicit
primitive-recursive specialization of that modulus.  Its two denominators are
\[
 D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)=2^{\binom{r+1}{2}}(r+1)!,\qquad
 D_{\Delta}(r)=2^{\binom{r+1}{2}}(r+1)^{r+1}.
\]
They are \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator}, with closed form
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_eq}, and
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator}; their primitive-recursive proofs are
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_primrec} and
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator_primrec}.  Retaining the positive
zeroth recurrence term gives
\nolinkurl{fabiusReal_inverse_two_pow_one_term_lower_bound}.  Its weakened
factorial form, the comparison \(D_{\mathrm{fac}}\le D_{\Delta}\), and the
resulting elementary lower bound are respectively
\nolinkurl{inv_inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_le_fabiusReal},
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_le_deltaDenominator}, and
\nolinkurl{inv_inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator_le_fabiusReal}.

The strict and closed inverse moduli for the factorial denominator are
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inverse_two_pow_of_lt_factorialDenominator}
and
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_inverse_two_pow_of_le_factorialDenominator};
the corresponding elementary-denominator forms are
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inverse_two_pow_of_lt_deltaDenominator} and
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_inverse_two_pow_of_le_deltaDenominator}.
Finally \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous} packages the
reciprocal-precision condition as \nolinkurl{EffectivelyUniformContinuous}.
These two definitions and twelve theorems are the module's complete public
surface.  It does not provide a tolerant-bisection realizer, sequential
computability, or a combined \nolinkurl{IsComputableRealFunction} theorem.

The downstream \nolinkurl{FabiusInverseLogarithmicModulus.lean} replaces the
coarse choice \(r=n\) by
\[
 r_{\log}(n)=\operatorname{Nat.log2}(n)+1.
\]
The definition is \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder}.  Its exact
binary-length equation, primitive-recursive certificate, least-order theorem
for \(n>0\), and comparison \(r_{\log}(n)\le n\) are
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_eq_succ_log2},
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_primrec},
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_isLeast}, and
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder_le_self}.  Thus, at positive \(n\),
it is exactly the least natural \(r\) with \(n<2^r\).

The other two definitions are
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator} and
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicDeltaDenominator}.  They take value one
at zero and, at positive \(n\), evaluate the corresponding dyadic denominator
at \(r_{\log}(n)\).  Those positive-input equations are
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator_of_pos} and
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicDeltaDenominator_of_pos}; primitive
recursiveness is proved by
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator_primrec} and
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicDeltaDenominator_primrec}; and the
pointwise factorial-to-Delta comparison is
\nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator_le_deltaDenominator}.

For either logarithmic denominator \(d\), both threshold conventions give a
strict reciprocal output estimate:
\[
 |u-v|<d(n)^{-1}\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<\frac1n,\qquad
 |u-v|\le d(n)^{-1}\Longrightarrow |I(u)-I(v)|<\frac1n
 \quad(n>0).
\]
The factorial declarations are
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_lt_logarithmicFactorialDenominator}
and
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_le_logarithmicFactorialDenominator};
the Delta declarations are
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_lt_logarithmicDeltaDenominator}
and
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inv_nat_of_le_logarithmicDeltaDenominator}.
The closed input threshold still has a strict conclusion because
\(2^{-r_{\log}(n)}<1/n\).  Finally,
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous_logarithmic} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous_logarithmicDelta}
separately package the factorial and report-exact Delta witnesses for
\nolinkurl{EffectivelyUniformContinuous}.

These three definitions and fifteen theorems are the complete public surface.
The module does not construct the exact endpoint-mass ceiling denominator or
a tolerant-bisection realizer, and it proves no
\nolinkurl{SequentiallyComputable}, combined
\nolinkurl{IsComputableRealFunction}, or input-bit asymptotic statement.

The separate smooth bridge \nolinkurl{FabiusInverseQuarterJet.lean} identifies
the complete inverse jet at the quarter anchor.  For
\(G(y)=\operatorname{fabiusInv}(F,h_F,y)\),
\[
 \left.\frac{d^n}{dt^n}
   \left(G\!\left(\frac5{72}+t\right)-\frac14\right)\right|_{t=0}
 =n!\,[X^n]\bigl(\operatorname{QuadraticInverse.inverse}(4)\bigr),
\]
and hence
\[
 G^{(m+1)}(5/72)=(m+1)!(-4)^m C_m.
\]
The exact declarations are
\nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv_centeredFabiusInv_quarter_eq_quadraticInverse} and
\nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv_fabiusInv_five_seventy_two_succ}.  They prove
equality of every derivative jet, not equality with an analytic function on
a neighborhood or a named nonzero flat-remainder decomposition.

Flatness of \(F\) becomes steepness of \(F^{-1}\).  Substituting
\(x=F^{-1}(y)\) into the effective estimate gives, on the stated shrinking
window,
\[
  y\leq 2^{\binom{n+1}{2}}\bigl(F^{-1}(y)\bigr)^n,
\]
with a factorial-strengthened version as well.  In particular
\(F^{-1}(y)/y\to+\infty\) as \(y\downarrow0\), and reflection supplies the
right-endpoint counterpart.
More sharply, the positive interior derivative itself tends to \(+\infty\)
at both ends, by
\nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_fabiusInv_atTop_at_zero_right} and
\nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_fabiusInv_atTop_at_one_left}.  These are one-sided
limits through \((0,1)\), not derivative values at the clamping points.

\subsection{Exact inverse modulus and rigidity (post-snapshot)}

The structural layer \nolinkurl{InverseModulus.lean} now has an exhaustive public
surface of one definition and thirty-one theorems.  Relative to its earlier
twenty-theorem surface, the eleven equality/rigidity declarations, in source order, are:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
fabiusIntervalMass_eq_zero_of_add_nonpos
fabiusIntervalMass_eq_zero_of_one_le
strictMonoOn_fabiusIntervalMass_firstHalf
strictAntiOn_fabiusIntervalMass_secondHalf
fabiusReal_lt_fabiusIntervalMass_of_mem_Ioo
fabiusIntervalMass_eq_fabiusReal_iff
fabiusReal_sub_lt_sub
fabiusReal_sub_eq_sub_iff
fabiusReal_add_eq_iff
fabiusInv_sub_eq_sub_iff_of_mem_Icc
abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq_iff_of_mem_Icc
\end{lstlisting}
The first two record the zero-mass tails for nonnegative interval lengths.  The
global weak shape remains valid for every nonnegative length; at a nondegenerate
length \(0<h\leq1\), the new strict branches are exactly
\([-h,(1-h)/2]\) and \([(1-h)/2,1]\), ending where the two constant tails begin.
Within \([0,1]\), an interval of length \(h\) has the endpoint mass \(F(h)\)
exactly when \(h=0\), its left endpoint is zero, or its right endpoint is one.
The strict-interior and subtraction wrappers give the same classification for the
least-mass inequality, while constrained superadditivity is an equality exactly
when one summand is zero or their sum is one.

Transport through the inverse classifies the unit-interval equality cases as well.
For \(0\leq u\leq v\leq1\), the ordered inverse-gap bound is sharp exactly when
\(u=v\), \(u=0\), or \(v=1\).  In the order-free absolute bound, equality for
\(u,v\in[0,1]\) means that the inputs coincide or at least one is an endpoint.
Both restrictions to the unit interval are essential: the totalized inverse has
constant tails, which create additional equality configurations on all of \(\RealNumbers\).
The preceding twenty declarations still supply the weak shape, sharp inequalities,
attained exact moduli, and effective-injectivity implications; the next module turns
the structural modulus into explicit recursive dyadic denominators.

\subsection{Effective dyadic continuity of the noncomputable inverse}

\nolinkurl{FabiusInverseEffectiveContinuity.lean} supplies an explicit
primitive-recursive modulus for the totalized inverse.  Its two
natural-valued denominators are
\[
 D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)=2^{\binom{r+1}{2}}(r+1)!,
 \qquad
 D_{\Delta}(r)=2^{\binom{r+1}{2}}(r+1)^{r+1}.
\]
The first is defined recursively and then identified with the displayed
factorial form; the second is the elementary box-event denominator from the
inverse-computability report.  Both functions are primitive recursive and
\(D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)\le D_{\Delta}(r)\) for every \(r\).

Fix \(F\) of type \nolinkurl{BoundedFabius} and let \(h_F\) witness
\nolinkurl{IsFabius} \(F\).  Only the sharp one-term recurrence estimate
requires \(r>0\):
\[
 \frac{1}{
   2^{\binom r2}(r+1)!(2^r-1)}
 \le F(2^{-r}).
\]
The consequences are total in \(r\), including \(r=0\):
\[
 D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)^{-1}\le F(2^{-r}),
 \qquad
 D_{\Delta}(r)^{-1}\le F(2^{-r}).
\]
For every real \(u,v\), either denominator \(D\) gives the strict and closed
inverse moduli
\[
\begin{aligned}
 |u-v|<D(r)^{-1}
   &\Longrightarrow |F^{-1}(u)-F^{-1}(v)|<2^{-r},\\
 |u-v|\le D(r)^{-1}
   &\Longrightarrow |F^{-1}(u)-F^{-1}(v)|\le2^{-r}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Here \(F^{-1}\) means the globally clamped \nolinkurl{fabiusInv}, so no
unit-interval hypothesis is placed on \(u\) or \(v\).  Taking the stronger
factorial denominator at \(r=n\), and using \(n<2^n\), proves
\nolinkurl{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous}, namely
\nolinkurl{EffectivelyUniformContinuous} for that totalized inverse, in the
repository's positive-precision reciprocal convention.

The module's exhaustive public inventory is two definitions and twelve
theorems:
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.07\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.53\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.33\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
No. & Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
No. & Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endhead
1 & \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator} & Recursive natural-valued factorial denominator. \\
2 & \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_eq} & Closed form \(D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)\). \\
3 & \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_primrec} & Primitive recursiveness of \(D_{\mathrm{fac}}\). \\
4 & \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator} & Natural-valued report denominator \(D_{\Delta}\). \\
5 & \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator_primrec} & Primitive recursiveness of \(D_{\Delta}\). \\
6 & \nolinkurl{fabiusReal_inverse_two_pow_one_term_lower_bound} & One-term endpoint bound, under \(h_F\) and \(r>0\). \\
7 & \nolinkurl{inv_inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_le_fabiusReal} & Factorial reciprocal bound for every \(r\), under \(h_F\). \\
8 & \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusFactorialDenominator_le_deltaDenominator} & \(D_{\mathrm{fac}}(r)\le D_{\Delta}(r)\), with no Fabius hypothesis. \\
9 & \nolinkurl{inv_inverseFabiusDeltaDenominator_le_fabiusReal} & Report reciprocal bound for every \(r\), under \(h_F\). \\
10 & \nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inverse_two_pow_of_lt_factorialDenominator} & Strict factorial-threshold modulus for all real inputs. \\
11 & \nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_inverse_two_pow_of_le_factorialDenominator} & Closed factorial-threshold modulus for all real inputs. \\
12 & \nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_inverse_two_pow_of_lt_deltaDenominator} & Strict report-threshold modulus for all real inputs. \\
13 & \nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le_inverse_two_pow_of_le_deltaDenominator} & Closed report-threshold modulus for all real inputs. \\
14 & \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous} & Effective uniform continuity with witness \(D_{\mathrm{fac}}(n)\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The boundary is important.  The two denominator definitions and their
primitive-recursion proofs are executable, but \nolinkurl{fabiusInv} itself
is still a \emph{noncomputable} order-isomorphism inverse.  An effective
modulus alone is not a name-producing algorithm.  This module proves neither
\nolinkurl{SequentiallyComputable} nor a combined
\nolinkurl{IsComputableRealFunction} theorem for the inverse, and it supplies
no logarithmic selector \(r(n)\), exact least/ceiling denominator, tolerant
bisection realizer, or input-bit complexity bound.

\subsection{Generic formal implicit roots versus inverse-Fabius germs}
\nolinkurl{ImplicitPowerSeries.lean} isolates a purely formal root engine.
\nolinkurl{FormalImplicitRoot.existsUnique_isRoot_sub_mem} lifts a simple
approximate root modulo an adic ideal in a complete commutative ring without a
monicity assumption.  Its
power-series specialization
\nolinkurl{PowerSeries.Implicit.existsUnique_zeroConstant_root} says that a
polynomial over \(R[[X]]\) with vanishing parameter-constant
constant coefficient and unit parameter-constant linear coefficient has a
unique zero-constant formal root.  The chosen root and its uniqueness
interface are \nolinkurl{PowerSeries.Implicit.root},
\nolinkurl{PowerSeries.Implicit.constantCoeff_root},
\nolinkurl{PowerSeries.Implicit.eval_root}, and
\nolinkurl{PowerSeries.Implicit.eq_root}.

The specialization \nolinkurl{QuarterCatalanGerm.lean} has two public
definitions and thirteen public theorems.  Besides the explicit unique
zero-constant solution of \(D+4D^2=(4/9)X\), it proves that the distinguished
\nolinkurl{dyadicGermTwo} obeys the same scaled equation.  The four bridge
declarations are \nolinkurl{dyadicGermTwo_functionalEquation},
\nolinkurl{rescale_dyadicGermTwo_eq_quadraticInverse},
\nolinkurl{dyadicGermTwo_eq_rescale_quadraticInverse}, and
\nolinkurl{coeff_dyadicGermTwo_succ}; rescaling by \(9/4\) gives
\(\operatorname{QuadraticInverse.inverse}(4)\), and the degree-\((m+1)\)
coefficient before that rescaling is
\((4/9)^{m+1}(-4)^mC_m\).

These declarations are formal algebra only.  They neither define the inverse
frontier's \(\mathcal A_r\) or \(Q_{x_0}\), nor prove convergence or analyticity
of the resulting series, identification with a local inverse, entry into a
finite-prefix plateau, a nonzero flat defect, or any Fabius quantile formula.

\subsection{Exact analytic loci and non-elementarity}
The smooth function is analytic precisely off its closed transition interval:
\[
  \operatorname{AnalyticAt}(F,x)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  x\notin[0,1].
\]
\nolinkurl{NowhereAnalytic.lean} includes both endpoints; outside the interval the
function is locally constant.  \nolinkurl{InverseNotElementary.lean} proves the
same analytic locus for the totalized inverse.  In the interior, analyticity
of the inverse would make \(F\) analytic by the inverse-function theorem; at
the endpoints, the identity theorem conflicts with the constant tails and
strict increase.

The generic modules \nolinkurl{AlgebraicBranch.lean} and
\nolinkurl{ElementaryFunction.lean} show that the formal class
\nolinkurl{IsElementary} has dense analytic locus, as do continuous algebraic
branches with elementary coefficients.  \nolinkurl{NotElementary.lean}
therefore excludes agreement with \(F\) on any subset of \([0,1]\) having
nonempty interior.  \nolinkurl{InverseBranch.lean} proves that a continuous
right-inverse branch of a densely analytic function is analytically dense on
its branch domain.  The constructor \nolinkurl{IsElementaryOrInverse.invBranch}
closes the class under such branches on an open set \(U\) only when the chosen
total extension is analytic on \(\operatorname{interior}(U^c)\); branch-domain
boundaries are deliberately excluded.  Its Lambert-\(W\) theorem is a
conditional criterion for any branch satisfying these hypotheses, not an
instantiated mathlib declaration of \(W\).  \nolinkurl{InverseNotElementary.lean}
then excludes both \(F\) and its totalized inverse from this enlarged class on
\((0,1)\).

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Formalization-sensitive point.}\space
These are theorems about the explicitly defined Lean predicates
\nolinkurl{IsElementary} and \nolinkurl{IsElementaryOrInverse}, not a claim about every
possible computer-algebra language or every informal use of the word
``closed form.''  The precise constructors and branch hypotheses in
\nolinkurl{ElementaryFunction.lean} and \nolinkurl{InverseBranch.lean} are part of the
statement.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Primitive-recursive splines and computable reals}
The approximation branch also became effective.  \nolinkurl{FabiusComputability.lean}
proves the uniform estimates
\[
  \sup_{x\in[0,1]}|S_p(x)-F(x)|\leq2^{-p},\qquad
  \sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}|S_p(x)-\FabiusGlobal(x)|\leq2^{-p},
\]
and records the primitive-recursive modulus \(d(n)=2n\) coming from the sharp
Lipschitz bound.  \nolinkurl{FabiusComputableSpline.lean} implements the centered
Thue--Morse spline using signed pairs of natural numerators, proves its pieces
primitive recursive, rounds to a dyadic name with an explicit error budget,
and supplies two evaluators with the same error \(5\cdot2^{-(p+3)}\).  The
first clamps its input to the bounded interval and packages
\nolinkurl{IsComputableRealFunction (fabiusReal F)}.  The second retains the
unrestricted signed spline code; \nolinkurl{extendedFabiusSplineApproxPR_error}
proves its global error, and
\nolinkurl{extendedFabius_isComputableRealFunction} packages the result for
the signed extension of every \(F\) satisfying \nolinkurl{IsFabius F}.  Its canonical specialization is
\nolinkurl{globalFabius_isComputableRealFunction}.  Thus both bounded and
signed-global computability theorems contain the two required clauses:
preservation of computable real sequences and effective uniform continuity.
The unrestricted evaluator is a primitive-recursive certificate rather than a
complexity claim, since its finite fold length depends on the positive input
numerator.

Uniform convergence also controls changing inputs.  The theorem
\nolinkurl{abs_fabiusUniformSpline_sub_extendedFabius_le_add} states
\[
  |S_p(x)-\FabiusGlobal(y)|\leq 2^{-p}+2|x-y|,
\]
and \nolinkurl{fabiusUniformSpline_tendsto_extendedFabius_of_tendsto} concludes
that \(S_p(x_p)\to\FabiusGlobal(x)\) whenever \(x_p\to x\).

\section{The exact--analytic dyadic bridge}\label{ch:dyadic-bridge}

\subsection{The purpose of \texttt{DyadicAnalytic.lean}}
The exact evaluator is useful only after it is tied to the analytic function.  \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean} is the central bridge.  It proves, for every \nolinkurl{IsFabius} candidate, the same finite recurrences that were defined independently in \nolinkurl{Arithmetic.lean}; then it identifies the two by induction.

The proof has four main stages:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item derive a finite Taylor identity on a dyadic cell;
  \item specialize it to inverse powers of two and obtain an endpoint recurrence;
  \item prove that the analytic inverse-power sequence equals the exact rational table; and
  \item lift this equality to arbitrary dyadic numerators through the highest-bit recursion.
\end{enumerate}
The surrounding modules \nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean} and \nolinkurl{DyadicCorrectness.lean} isolate the algebraic and algorithmic portions of this chain.

\subsection{Finite Taylor expansion on a dyadic scale}
Fix a sufficiently small dyadic base point.  The iterated derivative formula expresses every derivative up to a chosen order through a scaled value of \(F\).  At the order where the scaled argument leaves the active interval and enters the zero tail, higher derivatives vanish.  Taylor's theorem therefore terminates exactly rather than producing an infinite series with a remainder.

A representative identity, in the normalization used by the evaluator, is
\[
  F(2^{-m}+y)+F(y)
  =\sum_{k=0}^{m}
    \frac{2^{\binom{k+1}{2}}}{k!}
    F(2^{-(m-k)})\,y^k,
\]
valid for \(m\geq1\) and \(0\leq y\leq2^{-m}\).  Thus the highest-bit evaluator computes the displayed polynomial and subtracts the recursively evaluated \(F(y)\).  The domain and the subtraction are essential parts of the recurrence.

The proof does not invoke analyticity.  It uses a finite-order Taylor theorem with a remainder, then proves the next derivative vanishes on the relevant segment.  This is crucial: the Fabius function is smooth but nowhere analytic in the appropriate sense, so an argument based on convergence of an infinite Taylor series would be false.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Formalization-sensitive point.}\space
Finite Taylor identities are local consequences of compact support and the differential equation.  They do not imply that the function equals its infinite Taylor series in a neighborhood.  The formalization later turns the same exact truncation phenomenon into a proof of nonanalyticity.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Inverse-power values}
For a candidate \(F\), define informally
\[
  A_n(F)=F(2^{-n}).
\]
The dyadic Taylor identity at a suitable endpoint gives a recurrence for \(A_{n+1}(F)\) in terms of earlier \(A_j(F)\).  Boundary compatibility and symmetry supply the initial value \(A_1(F)=1/2\).

Independently, \nolinkurl{Arithmetic.lean} defines a rational sequence satisfying the same recurrence.  Strong induction proves
\[
  A_n(F)=A_n^{\mathrm{exact}}
\]
for every \(n\).  The induction step is predominantly algebraic, but the statement lives in \(\RealNumbers\); the proof must cast exact rational sums and products into reals.  The source localizes these casts in lemmas asserting that the rational recurrence is preserved by the canonical embedding \(\RationalNumbers\hookrightarrow\RealNumbers\).

\subsection{From closed polynomial to Horner program}
Once the analytic value is a finite polynomial in exact inverse-power values, \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean} invokes the algebraic theorem that the closed polynomial equals \nolinkurl{fabiusTaylorHorner}.  This is a clean interface point:
\begin{itemize}
  \item analysis proves the value is the mathematical polynomial;
  \item arithmetic proves the polynomial has exact rational coefficients; and
  \item algorithmics proves Horner evaluation computes that polynomial.
\end{itemize}
