\[
  \left|g(t)-\frac{\log2}{2}t^2\right|
  \leq C t\log t
\]
for all sufficiently large \(t\).  This is weaker than the eventual sharp formula but strong enough to establish flatness and compare \(F\) with elementary scales.

Such a concrete inequality is more useful in Lean than a bare \(o(t^2)\) statement.  It can be inserted into exponential comparisons with explicit thresholds, and it supports later proofs that every power of \(x\) dominates \(F(x)\) while every \(e^{-c/x}\) is dominated by \(F(x)\).

\subsection{Filter formulations}
The repository states asymptotics both in the \(t\to\infty\) coordinate and as \(x\to0^+\).  Conversion lemmas relate the filters under \(x=2^{-t}\).  A typical proof establishes a limit along \nolinkurl{atTop}, composes with the continuous map \(t\mapsto2^{-t}\), and then identifies the image filter with \nolinkurl{nhdsWithin 0 (Set.Ioi 0)}.

Explicit conversion lemmas are preferable to ad hoc substitutions in each theorem.  They ensure that positivity domains and logarithm definitions are carried through uniformly.

\section{Negative Laplace analysis and the periodic correction}\label{ch:negative-laplace}

\subsection{The negative moment generating function}
The repository's entire generating function is totalized as
\[
  G(z)=1+z\int_0^1\RvachevUp(t)e^{zt}\,\Differential t.
\]
For \(s>0\), \nolinkurl{NegativeLaplace.lean} identifies its negative-axis value with the exact product
\[
  e^{\operatorname{negativeLaplaceLog}(s)}=G(-s)
  =\prod_{j\geq1}
    \frac{1-e^{-s/2^j}}{s/2^j}.
\]
The companion \nolinkurl{LaplaceTransform.lean} proves the equivalent bounded-function formula
\[
  \frac{G(-s)}s=\int_{(0,\infty)}F(t)e^{-st}\,\Differential t.
\]
The product's logarithm is
\[
  L(s)=\sum_{j\geq1}
   \left[\log(1-e^{-s/2^j})-
         \log(s/2^j)\right].
\]
\nolinkurl{NegativeLaplace.lean} constructs this series carefully and proves absolute convergence after grouping terms in a form adapted to small and large arguments.

\subsection{The kernel and its bounds}
The central scalar kernel is the logarithm of a normalized Bose factor.  For small \(u\), the quotient \((1-e^{-u})/u\) tends to one, so its logarithm is \(O(u)\).  For large \(u\), the exponentially small correction \(\log(1-e^{-u})\) is controlled by a geometric series.

The source proves elementary inequalities before summing over dyadic scales.  Typical estimates use
\[
  0\leq-\log(1-v)\leq\frac{v}{1-v}
  \qquad(0\leq v<1),
\]
with \(v=e^{-u}\).  The resulting majorants are summable after the dyadic split at the scale where \(s/2^j\) crosses one.

\subsection{The exact dilation equation}
The product shifts cleanly under \(s\mapsto2s\).  Removing the first factor and reindexing the rest gives an exact functional equation for \(L(s)=\log G(-s)\).  Solving the associated difference equation in the logarithmic variable \(u=\log_2s\) produces a quadratic polynomial plus a periodic remainder.

The formal decomposition is
\[
  L(s)=
  -\frac{(\log s)^2}{2\log2}
  +\frac12\log s
  +R(\log_2s)
  +E(s),
\]
where \(R(u+1)=R(u)\) and the forward-tail error satisfies an explicit exponential bound.  A representative bound proved in the module is
\[
  |E(s)|\leq\frac{e^{-s}}{(1-e^{-s})^2},
\]
and for \(s\) beyond a fixed threshold this is simplified to a constant multiple of \(e^{-s}\).

The sign information is equally exact on the positive half-line.  Every
logarithmic factor and the full logarithmic product are strictly negative,
as is the forward tail.  Consequently
\nolinkurl{negativeLaplaceTailError}, defined as the negative of that tail,
is strictly positive and its absolute value can be removed.

The reusable differentiation step is isolated in
\nolinkurl{ScalingRecurrence.lean}, a Mathlib-only module independent of the
Fabius definitions.  It differentiates multiplicative scaling recurrences
for an arbitrary dilation \(q>0\), in both vector-valued and real-scalar
forms, with the outgoing weight normalized by the caller.  Its inert-parameter
version acquires no factor of \(q\), because differentiation is in the other
variable; dyadic wrappers feed these results into the ordinary and vertical
negative-Laplace derivative layers.

\subsection{How the periodic function is constructed}
The periodic term is not guessed from numerics.  The code builds it by comparing the exact logarithm with a particular quadratic solution of the dilation equation.  The difference is invariant under doubling up to a forward tail.  Iterating the dilation toward infinity makes that tail summable; adding its convergent correction yields an exactly invariant function of \(\log_2s\).

Concretely, one introduces a defect
\[
  D(s)=L(s)+\frac{(\log s)^2}{2\log2}-\frac12\log s.
\]
The dilation identity shows that \(D(2s)-D(s)\) is an exponentially small kernel.  A forward sum of these defects corrects \(D\) into a function satisfying \(P(2s)=P(s)\).  Writing \(R(u)=P(2^u)\) gives period one.

\begin{proofidea}
The periodic correction is the homogeneous solution of a dyadic difference equation.  The quadratic logarithmic terms form a particular solution.  Exponentially small forward tails determine which periodic solution corresponds to the actual infinite product.
\end{proofidea}

\subsection{Continuity and regularity}
\nolinkurl{PeriodicCorrection.lean} proves continuity of \(R\).  The defining correction is a locally uniformly convergent series on positive compact intervals.  The proof covers a compact interval \([a,b]\subset(0,\infty)\), bounds every tail term uniformly by a summable geometric sequence depending on \(a\), and applies a Weierstrass-type theorem.

Later modules strengthen regularity.  \nolinkurl{PeriodicRegularity.lean} differentiates the correction series term by term after proving uniform convergence of derivative tails.  \nolinkurl{PeriodicMean.lean} computes or normalizes its mean.  \nolinkurl{PeriodicFourier.lean} identifies Fourier coefficients through Mellin-transform data.

\subsection{Mellin--Bose analysis and finite parts}
\nolinkurl{MellinBose.lean} proves, first for real \(a>0\),
\[
 \int_0^\infty x^{a-1}\log(1-e^{-x})\,\Differential x
 =-\Gamma(a)\zeta(a+1),
\]
by expanding the logarithm into an absolutely integrable exponential series
and integrating term by term.  \nolinkurl{MellinFinitePart.lean} regularizes the
double pole of \(\Gamma(s)\zeta(1+s)\) at \(s=0\), while
\nolinkurl{BoseFinitePartIntegral.lean} realizes the finite part as convergent
split integrals after subtracting the small-\(x\) singular term.

The local constants are normalized explicitly.  \nolinkurl{GammaSecondOrder.lean}
proves the sharp complex remainder
\[
 \Gamma(1+z)=1-\gamma z+
 \left(\frac{\gamma^2}{2}+\frac{\pi^2}{12}\right)z^2+O(z^3)
 \qquad(z\to0),
\]
while \nolinkurl{StieltjesConstant.lean} fixes \(\gamma_1\) by declaring the
derivative of \nolinkurl{zetaRegularReal} at one to be \(-\gamma_1\), recorded
in \nolinkurl{HasDerivAt} form.  Thus the sign of the linear coefficient in
\(\zeta(1+s)=s^{-1}+\gamma-\gamma_1s+O(s^2)\) is part of the checked interface.

\nolinkurl{PeriodicFourier.lean} treats the zero mode through the mean calculation
and the nonzero modes by the substitution \(x=2^t\), dyadic interval
decompositions, an identity-theorem extension to the open right half-plane,
and continuity to the punctured imaginary axis.  It obtains the Gamma--zeta
Fourier coefficients and absolute Fourier reconstruction without a residue
calculation, contour truncation, or Mellin inversion.

\subsection{Why the periodic term cannot be dropped}
The oscillatory correction \(R(\log_2s)\) is bounded but nonconstant.  Therefore it is lower order than the quadratic logarithmic term but not an error tending to zero.  Any asymptotic formula claiming an \(o(1)\) or \(O(1/\log s)\) residual after omitting \(R\) is false.

\nolinkurl{FabiusWikipediaObstruction.lean} and related audit modules formalize this obstruction.  They prove nonconstancy through a nonzero Fourier coefficient or a pair of points with unequal values.  Sampling along two dyadic phase subsequences then produces distinct limit points for the alleged residual.

\subsection{Identification with the generating function}
After constructing the product and its logarithm, \nolinkurl{NegativeLaplace.lean} proves equality with the analytic generating function defined earlier.  The route is:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item establish the product formula for finite truncations;
  \item prove convergence to the infinite product;
  \item establish the same functional equation and normalization for the generating function; and
  \item use uniqueness or direct limit passage to identify them.
\end{enumerate}
This closes the loop between exact moment series, Fourier/probability products, and the asymptotic Laplace object.

\section{Bromwich inversion and the sharp saddle point}\label{ch:saddle}

\subsection{From a transform to the endpoint density}
For every \(r>0\), \nolinkurl{FabiusBromwichInput.lean} proves the exact
Fourier--Bromwich identity
\[
 F(x)=e^{rx}\int_{\RealNumbers}
 e^{2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi x}\,
 \frac{G\!\left(-(r+2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi)\right)}
      {r+2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi}\,\Differential\xi .
\]
Thus the vertical coordinate \(z=r+2\pi\ImaginaryUnit\xi\) has positive real part,
while the generating function is sampled at the negative argument \(-z\).
The theorem comes directly from Fourier inversion of the exponentially tilted
CDF and requires no contour-shifting assumption.

\nolinkurl{BromwichSaddle.lean} supplies the generic quantitative framework and
\nolinkurl{FabiusBromwichInput.lean} discharges its Fabius-specific integrability
and decay hypotheses.  For asymptotic extraction one then chooses the
explicit lower-Lambert reference radius and splits the resulting real
integral into central and tail regions.

\subsection{The Lambert phase}
The dominant balance introduces a lower-Lambert reference coordinate.  On its natural small-positive-\(x\) domain the repository defines \(\lambda(x)\) so that
\[
  \lambda(x)\,2^{-\lambda(x)}=x.
\]
Equivalently, if \(x=2^{-t}\), then
\[
  \lambda(2^{-t})-\log_2\!\lambda(2^{-t})=t.
\]
These are exact algebraic identities for the chosen coordinate.  The formal module deliberately does not claim that this reference coordinate is the exact critical point of every residual term in the full contour phase.

The advantage of retaining the exact Lambert phase is that all-order expansions can be stated without repeatedly composing truncated asymptotic series.  The leading behavior is
\[
  \lambda(2^{-t})=t+\log_2 t+o(1),
\]
or the corresponding expression after substituting \(t=-\log_2x\).  The exact relation captures the corrections needed in both the exponential and Gaussian normalization.

\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertPhase.lean}, \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertRates.lean}, and \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean} establish the domain, monotonicity, growth, derivative bounds, and exact lower-Lambert identities.  Separate modules for small-argument expansions later translate \(\lambda\) into elementary logarithms to any fixed order.

The reciprocal scale conversion is algebraically total:
\nolinkurl{smallArgumentLog_inv_eq_all} holds for every real input, and
\nolinkurl{smallArgumentLog_inv_isBigO} holds at an arbitrary filter.  The
small-positive filter reappears only when these identities are transferred to
an endpoint asymptotic theorem.

\subsection{Exact reduction at the saddle}
\nolinkurl{FabiusSharpExactReduction.lean} packages the inversion calculation into an exact error decomposition.  In logarithmic form, it has the conceptual shape
\[
  \log F(x)-M(x)
  =\log\bigl(\text{normalized saddle mass}\bigr)
   +\text{forward-tail correction},
\]
where \(M(x)\) contains the explicit quadratic-logarithmic, Lambert, Gaussian, and periodic pieces.

This theorem is the critical interface between contour analysis and asymptotic algebra.  All later approximations can focus on two controlled terms: a normalized central integral tending to one, and an exponentially flat tail inherited from the negative-Laplace product.

\subsection{Central and tail decomposition}
Write the vertical contour variable as \(s=\sigma+\ImaginaryUnit y\).  The integral is split at a central radius \(|y|\leq Y(\lambda)\):
\[
  I=I_{\mathrm{central}}+I_{\mathrm{tail}}.
\]
Near the saddle, a Taylor expansion of the exponent begins with a negative quadratic term.  Away from the saddle, a minor-arc estimate shows strict decay of the real part.

The module family includes \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleCentral.lean}, \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleTail.lean}, \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertMinorArc.lean}, and reference-weight modules.  The split allows different techniques:
\begin{itemize}
  \item local cumulant expansions and Gaussian comparison in the center;
  \item coarse but uniform exponential inequalities in the tail.
\end{itemize}
Trying to use the local Taylor series on the entire contour would give an unjustified remainder.

\subsection{The Gaussian reference weight}
After rescaling \(y\) by the square root of the second derivative at the saddle, the leading central factor is \(e^{-u^2/2}\).  \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleReferenceWeight.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleReferenceTail.lean} collect exact Gaussian integrals and tail bounds in the normalization used by the contour.

The normalized saddle mass is divided by the Gaussian main integral so its limit should be one.  This normalization simplifies logarithms: once the ratio is \(1+O(1/\lambda)\), its logarithm is controlled by a standard \(\log(1+u)\) estimate.

\subsection{Cumulants and derivative bounds}
The logarithm of the negative-Laplace product has derivatives expressible as dyadic sums of kernel derivatives.  \nolinkurl{LaplaceCumulantAsymptotics.lean}, \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertDerivativeBounds.lean}, and related modules prove uniform estimates for these cumulants at the saddle.

The same cumulant module packages Bell-polynomial inversion of normalized
Laplace moments through order four.  The first identity is already exact
without side conditions:
\[
 \operatorname{normalizedLaplaceMoment}_1(s)=-L'(s).
\]

The second derivative determines the Gaussian width.  Higher derivatives determine correction polynomials.  The source proves bounds of the form
\[
  \kappa_r(\lambda)=O(\lambda^{1-r/2})
\]
after the chosen normalization.  Exact exponents vary with convention, but the hierarchy guarantees that each additional Taylor order contributes another inverse power of the large phase.

Two vertical-log modules make the derivative interface explicit.  For every
\(m\), \nolinkurl{exists_norm_iteratedDeriv_negativeLaplaceVerticalLog_rpow_le_add_one}
supplies \(C\geq0\) such that the \((m+1)\)-st vertical derivative at radius
\(2^b\) is bounded by \(C(b+1)\) for \(b\geq0\) and \(|\theta|\leq1\), including
the base radius \(2^0=1\).  The legacy \(Cb\) estimate is retained for
\(b\geq1\).  At any radius \(r>0\),
\nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv_negativeLaplaceVerticalLog_at_zero_eq_ordinary_all}
identifies every zero-axis jet: order zero is normalized to zero, while order
\(n>0\) is \((r\ImaginaryUnit)^n\) times the \(n\)-th ordinary derivative of the
negative-Laplace logarithm at \(r\).

\subsection{Minor arcs and tail decay}
A complex product may have oscillatory peaks away from zero.  \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertMinorArc.lean} proves that, outside the central window, enough sinc/Bose factors lose modulus to force decay.  The proof divides the frequency axis into ranges:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item a near-central range where a quadratic cosine or exponential inequality applies;
  \item an intermediate range where a fixed block of dyadic factors supplies uniform loss; and
  \item a far range controlled by elementary transform decay and integration by parts.
\end{enumerate}
Each range has a separate numerical inequality, making the final integral estimate a finite case split rather than an opaque global bound.

\subsection{The first correction}
\nolinkurl{FabiusFirstSaddleCorrection.lean} computes the first non-Gaussian term.  Cubic and quartic derivatives of the phase contribute through Gaussian moments.  Odd terms integrate to zero against the even Gaussian; the first surviving coefficient combines the quartic term and the square of the cubic term.

The code represents the correction as a polynomial in the rescaled central variable.  Exact Gaussian moments are evaluated recursively or through double factorials.  The resulting rational coefficient is then inserted into the normalized saddle mass expansion.

\subsection{The corrected sharp formula}
\nolinkurl{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean} states the sharp logarithmic asymptotic in the repository's exact normalization.  Writing \(\Psi\) for the bounded nonconstant period-one correction and \(C_*\) for the proved normalization constant, its main term is
\[
  \log F(x)=
  -\frac{\log2}{2}\lambda(x)^2
  +\left(1+\frac{\log2}{2}\right)\lambda(x)
  -\frac12\log\lambda(x)
  +C_*+\Psi\bigl(\lambda(x)\bigr)
  +O\!\left(\frac1{\lambda(x)}\right),
\]
where \(\lambda(x)\) is the exact lower-Lambert coordinate.  In particular, the quadratic coefficient is \(-\log2/2\); changing logarithm bases without changing the definition of \(\lambda\) would give a different and incorrect coefficient.

Exponentiating gives an asymptotic equivalence for \(F(x)\) itself.  The proof first bounds the logarithmic error, then uses continuity of the exponential and the fact that \(e^{O(1/\lambda)}=1+O(1/\lambda)\).

\subsection{Failure of an uncorrected elementary formula}
A simpler formula obtained by dropping the periodic term may still reproduce the leading \((\log x)^2\) scale.  It does not have a residual of order \(1/(-\log x)\).  The repository proves this by choosing two phase subsequences along which the periodic correction approaches different values.  The residual therefore has distinct limit points and cannot tend to zero.

Similarly, a proposed quotient of elementary exponentials is shown to decay on the wrong scale.  \nolinkurl{FabiusQuotientExponentialMismatch.lean} compares logarithms and proves the quotient is not asymptotically equivalent to \(F\), even if a finite numerical range looks persuasive.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Architectural point.}\space
The sharp theorem does not emerge from manipulating the delay equation alone.  The delay equation explains the coarse quadratic scale.  The periodic term and correct constant require the exact transform product; the Gaussian factor and inverse-phase corrections require Bromwich inversion and saddle analysis.
\end{boxedremark}

\section{All-order asymptotic expansions}\label{ch:all-orders}

\subsection{Why the exact phase is retained}
An all-order expansion can be expressed either in the exact saddle variable \(\lambda(x)\) or directly in powers of \(-\log x\) and iterated logarithms.  The repository takes the exact-phase expansion as primary.  This avoids a common error: composing two finite asymptotic series and claiming an arbitrary-order remainder without proving uniform control of the composition.

The exact-phase theorem has the form
\[
  \mathcal M(x)
   \sim \sum_{j\geq0}\frac{c_j(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^j},
\]
where \(\mathcal M(x)\) is the normalized saddle mass and each \(c_j\) is a bounded period-one coefficient function.  For every \(N\), the difference from the first \(N\) terms is \(O(\lambda^{-N})\).  Replacing \(\lambda\) by a finite elementary-log approximation inside these oscillatory coefficients requires a separate composition theorem and is not part of this statement.

\subsection{Formal logarithm of the Laplace product}
\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertFormalLog.lean} extracts a formal series for the local phase.  It treats logarithms, exponentials, and reciprocal series in a truncated polynomial ring.  The goal is algebraic: compute coefficients that must occur if a sufficiently smooth analytic remainder is controlled.

Separating formal algebra from analytic remainder estimates is essential.  Formal power-series identities can be checked by ring normalization and coefficient extensionality.  Their analytic validity is supplied later by bounds on the omitted terms.

\subsection{Higher small-argument expansion}
\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertHigherExpansion.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertAllOrderSmallArgument.lean} prove the expansion of the exact Lambert phase in terms of
\[
  L=\log(1/x),
  \qquad
  \ell=\log L.
\]
For each fixed order, \(\lambda\) is represented by a polynomial in \(1/L\) whose coefficients are polynomials in \(\ell\), plus a rigorously bounded remainder.

The proof uses the implicit saddle equation.  An approximate phase is substituted into the equation; formal algebra shows cancellation through the desired order.  Monotonicity and a derivative lower bound for the implicit function turn the equation residual into a bound on the phase error.

\subsection{Gaussian polynomial contractions}
The central exponential has an expansion
\[
  e^{-u^2/2}
  \exp\!\left(
    \sum_{r\geq3}\lambda^{1-r/2}a_r(iu)^r
  \right).
\]
Truncating the second exponential produces polynomials in \(u\) and \(1/\sqrt\lambda\).  Odd monomials vanish after integration; even monomials contract to Gaussian moments.  The modules \nolinkurl{GaussianPolynomialContraction.lean}, \nolinkurl{GaussianPolynomialWholeIntegral.lean}, and the tail modules formalize this mechanism.

A polynomial is represented by finitely supported coefficients.  The contraction functional maps \(u^{2k}\) to \((2k-1)!!\) and odd powers to zero.  Proving that contraction commutes with finite sums and scalar multiplication turns the central coefficient calculation into exact algebra.

\subsection{Coefficient recurrences}
\nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleCoefficientRecurrence.lean}, \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddlePolynomialCoefficients.lean}, and \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleExpansionCoefficients.lean} organize the coefficients recursively.  Each order depends only on finitely many cumulants and lower coefficients.  This recurrence remains the executable foundation even though newer modules also solve its Fabius-specific pieces in closed form.

The recurrence is justified by differentiating or multiplying truncated formal series and comparing coefficients.  Lean's finite-support polynomial representation ensures every coefficient sum is finite.  Index constraints are handled with \nolinkurl{Finset.antidiagonal} or explicit bounded ranges.

\nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleJetClosedForm.lean} solves the differential recurrence for
every periodic saddle jet, while \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleExponentClosedForm.lean}
reduces each exponent coefficient to one jet term plus a universal jet-free
tail.  The jet weights are the coefficients of
\[
  \prod_{k=1}^{n}(X-k).
\]
With signed first-kind Stirling numbers defined by
\(X(X-1)\cdots(X-n+1)=\sum_m s(n,m)X^m\),
\nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleJetStirling.lean} identifies the weight at \(X^m\) as
\(s(n+1,m+1)\), not \(s(n,m)\).  Thus the closed form is finite and
machine-checkable, while the recurrence remains the convenient generator.
\nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleLeadingCoefficient.lean} additionally isolates the top-jet
coefficient of every saddle \emph{mass} coefficient.  Its header notes the
analogous logarithmic transfer, but that transfer is not proved in this
module.

\subsection{Universal endpoint-transfer polynomials and finite expectations}
Two explicitly post-snapshot modules isolate the finite algebra behind an
endpoint-transfer step.  \nolinkurl{EndpointTransferPolynomials.lean} defines
universal polynomials \(P_r\in\RationalNumbers[X]\) by the formal identity
\[
  \exp\!\left(-\sum_{j\geq2}\frac{X^j t^{j-1}}{j}\right)
  =\sum_{r\geq0}P_r(X)t^r.
\]
Theorems \nolinkurl{endpointTransferPolynomial_succ},
\nolinkurl{endpointTransferPolynomial_eq_partitionExpSum}, and
\nolinkurl{endpointTransferSeries_eq_exp_subst} connect the recursive,
weighted-partition, and formal-series descriptions.  The evaluation theorems
\nolinkurl{aeval_endpointTransferPolynomial} and
\nolinkurl{map_endpointTransferSeries} show that the construction is natural
in every commutative rational algebra.  These are formal-algebra statements:
they assert no analytic convergence, branch choice, or remainder estimate.

\nolinkurl{PolynomialExpectationCumulant.lean} supplies the complementary
finite integration bridge.  The theorem
\leanevaltwo{_eq_sum_moment} expands the integral of a polynomial
evaluation into coefficient-weighted raw moments, assuming integrability only
for powers in the polynomial's support.  The theorem
\leanevaltwo{_eq_sum_completeBell_momentCumulant_with_mass_correction}
rewrites those moments through the complete Bell transform of their formal
cumulants and adds the exact constant-term correction
\(\varphi(p_0)(m_0-1)\).  The normalized theorem
\leanevaltwo{_eq_sum_completeBell_momentCumulant_of_moment_zero_eq_one}
and the probability-measure specialization
\leanevaltwo{_eq_sum_completeBell_momentCumulant} remove that
correction.  Taking \(p=P_r\) composes the two finite interfaces, but neither
module proves the analytic endpoint-transfer expansion, Taylor remainders, or
tail estimates.

\subsection{Geometric principal specialization and all residual moments}
At post-snapshot checkpoint
\nolinkurl{1e761ed77583e9870ceeaeb2c74ac824094f0f3f}, the finite interpolation
algebra acquires a denominator-free geometric endpoint.  Five reusable
bridges in \nolinkurl{CompleteHomogeneous.lean} are
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_smul},
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_option_zero},
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_fin_succ},
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_range}, and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalAt_zero}.  They record homogeneity, removal
of an adjoined zero variable, the head--tail recurrence on a nonempty
\texttt{Fin} family, reindexing of a natural-number range by \texttt{Fin}, and
specialization of the distinguished target to zero.

The later \nolinkurl{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating.lean} packages these
evaluations as the formal power series
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn}.  Its coefficient and
empty-family interfaces are
\nolinkurl{coeff_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn} and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_empty}, while
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_insert} is the
adjoining-variable geometric recurrence over every commutative semiring.
Over a commutative ring,
\nolinkurl{one_sub_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_insert} clears
one linear denominator,
\nolinkurl{prod_one_sub_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn} clears the
whole finite denominator product, and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_eq_invOfUnit_prod}
identifies the coefficient series with its canonical formal reciprocal.
These are finite formal-series identities, valid without distinct values,
division, cancellation, or domain hypotheses.  They assert no analytic
evaluation or convergence and do not prove the Bell/generalized-harmonic
power-sum presentation.

The separate \nolinkurl{CompleteHomogeneousBell.lean} closes exactly this
finite conversion.  For a finite family \(a_i\), it defines
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousPowerSum} and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousBellInput} by
\[
 p_k=\sum_i a_i^k,\qquad \kappa_0=0,\qquad
 \kappa_{k+1}=k!\,p_{k+1}.
\]
Its strongest theorem is the division-free commutative-semiring identity
\[
 \operatorname{Bell.complete}(\kappa)_n=n!\,h_n(a),
\]
named \nolinkurl{bellComplete_completeHomogeneousBellInput}.  Only after
passing to a commutative \(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra does
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_eq_factorialNormalize_completeBellPolynomial}
state the report form
\[
 h_n(a)=\operatorname{factorialNormalize}
   \bigl(\operatorname{completeBellPolynomial}(\kappa)\bigr)_n.
\]
Thus the generic theorem retains positive characteristic, zero divisors,
nilpotents, repeated values, and the zero ring; the normalized report theorem
uses the rational-algebra layer explicitly.  Neither declaration is an
analytic generating-series or convergence theorem.

Over every commutative semiring, without division or a hypothesis on \(q\),
\nolinkurl{GeometricCompleteHomogeneous.lean} proves
\[
  h_n(1,q,\ldots,q^p)=\GaussianBinomial{n+p}{p}{q},
  \qquad
  h_n(c,cq,\ldots,cq^p)=c^n\GaussianBinomial{n+p}{p}{q}.
\]
The exact declarations are
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_geometric},
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_scaled_geometric}, and the range-indexed
bridge
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_range_pow_eq_gaussianBinomial}.

The commutative-ring theorems in
\nolinkurl{GeometricResidualMoments.lean} assume polynomial exactness directly
on the displayed scaled nodes:
\[
  \sum_{k=0}^{p}w_k(cq^k)^d=0^d\quad(0\leq d\leq p).
\]
They then give, for \(r\geq0\) and \(m>0\),
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}w_k(cq^k)^{p+1+r}
 =c^{p+1+r}(-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q},
 \qquad
 \sum_{k=0}^{p}w_k(cq^k)^m
 =c^m(-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{m-1}{p}{q}.
\]
The theorem with this direct scaled-node hypothesis is strictly more general
than a result that first assumes exactness before dilation: it remains valid
when \(c\) is zero or a zero divisor.  The unscaled declarations are
\nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_geometric_pow_succ_add} and
\nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_geometric_pow_of_pos}; the maximally general scaled
forms are
\nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_succ_add} and
\nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_of_pos}.

For a field, finite-node injectivity is used only to obtain those low moments
from the geometric Lagrange row.  The four resulting declarations are
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_succ_add},
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_of_pos},
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_of_pos}, and
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_shifted_pow_of_pos}.  In the final
form the nodes are \(q^{start+k}\), and the exponent of \(q\) is exactly
\(start\,m+\binom{p+1}{2}\).  These sixteen declarations are finite algebra:
by themselves they assert no analytic Richardson remainder, convergence rate,
or sign bound for a sampled function.

\subsection{Fixed-order phase-locked Lambert extraction}
The later current-tree phase-extraction tranche supplies the analytic layer
for the actual logarithmic Fabius samples.  At the exact lower-Lambert nodes
\[
  x_{\lambda,j}=(\lambda+j)2^{-(\lambda+j)},
\]
\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertPhaseLockedPullback.lean} pulls back the full
small-argument expansion one fixed node at a time.  Periodicity freezes
\nolinkurl{negativeLaplacePsi} and every saddle-log coefficient at the
common base phase \(\lambda\), while the powers retain the shifted scale
\((\lambda+j)^{-1}\).

Before any estimate is used, \nolinkurl{LambertPhaseLockedBell.lean}
specializes the finite Bell algebra to the shifted reciprocal alphabet.  Its
definitions \nolinkurl{shiftedReciprocalPowerSum} and
\nolinkurl{shiftedReciprocalBellInput} encode
\[
 p_k^{(r)}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}(\lambda+j)^{-k},
 \qquad \kappa_{k+1}^{(r,\lambda)}=k!\,p_{k+1}^{(r)}(\lambda).
\]
The exact declaration
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_shiftedReciprocal_eq_bell} converts the
complete homogeneous evaluation to the factorially normalized complete Bell
polynomial.  The all-offset and \(s\ge1\) Lagrange-moment forms are
\nolinkurl{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_card_add_eq_bell}
and \nolinkurl{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_eq_bell}.
These normalized identities are stated over characteristic-zero fields (the
direct complete-homogeneous rewrite uses the rational-algebra structure).
Their shifted-reciprocal definitions use total field inversion, so they impose
no positivity or nonvanishing assumption on \(\lambda\).
They remain finite algebra and provide no asymptotic control.

Two independent asymptotic tools make the finite extrapolation legitimate.
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_isBigO_pow} in
\nolinkurl{CompleteHomogeneousAsymptotics.lean} carries coordinatewise
\(\BigO(g)\) estimates through a fixed degree-\(n\) complete homogeneous
evaluation, including \(n=0\).  In
\nolinkurl{LambertReciprocalAsymptotics.lean}, the reciprocal nodes are
\(\Theta\)-equivalent to \(\lambda^{-1}\), each fixed row coefficient and the
row's total variation are \(\BigO(\lambda^r)\), and
\nolinkurl{shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_isBigO_atTop}
absorbs that growth into a sufficiently high inverse power.

Let \(Y_j(\lambda)\) be the logarithmic sample with the nonperiodic
Wikipedia/Lambert main term removed, and let \(E_r(\lambda)\) be its
order-\(r\) reciprocal-Lagrange combination.  These are
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedReducedSample} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator}; the exact higher moments and
their finite sum are \nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum}.  Write \(\Psi\) for
\nolinkurl{negativeLaplacePsi}.  The leaf module
\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertPhaseExtractionBell.lean} leaves both definitions
unchanged and proves the exact declaration
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 R_{r,n}(\lambda)
   &=(-1)^r\left(\prod_{j=0}^{r}(\lambda+j)^{-1}\right) \\
   &\quad{}\times \operatorname{factorialNormalize}
     \bigl(\operatorname{completeBellPolynomial}
       (\kappa^{(r,\lambda)})\bigr)_n \\
   &\quad{}\times c_{r+1+n}(\lambda).
 \end{aligned}
\]
as \nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm_eq_bell};
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum_eq_sum_bell} rewrites each
term in the exact finite partial sum.  These are algebraic wrappers, not new
analytic claims.

For every fixed \(r,S\in\NaturalNumbers\),
\[
 E_r(\lambda)-\Psi(\lambda)
   -\sum_{n=0}^{S-1}R_{r,n}(\lambda)
 =\BigO\bigl(\lambda^{-(r+1+S)}\bigr)
 \qquad(\lambda\to+\infty).
\]
This is
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_residual_isBigO} in
\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertPhaseExtraction.lean}; its proof requests the
single-node expansion at order \(2r+S+1\), exactly compensating for the
\(\BigO(\lambda^r)\) row.  The same module gives the
\(\BigO(\lambda^{-(r+1)})\) estimator bound without residual subtraction,
the one-order improvement after subtracting the first omitted moment, and
convergence to \nolinkurl{negativeLaplacePsi} at a fixed phase along
\(\lambda=a+n\).  The result is a fixed-\(r,S\) Poincaré statement: it does
not assert growing-order uniformity, convergence of the formal
complete-homogeneous generating series or of an infinite residual series, a
multiplicative or exponentiated Bell relative-error hierarchy, a higher
extractor, or a Lambert sign/bracketing theorem.  The three Bell modules close
only the finite complete-homogeneous/Bell/generalized-harmonic conversion and
the displayed exact residual-term and partial-sum rewrites.

\subsection{Unconditionally summable analytic filters}
At compiled full-facade checkpoint \nolinkurl{22f801337},
\nolinkurl{AnalyticSeriesFilter.lean} supplies the pointwise convergent-series
counterpart of that finite algebra.  For a finite set of nodes \(\xi_i\),
weights \(w_i\), coefficients \(a_m\), and a scale \(z\), it defines
\[
  \mathcal L_z(a)
  =\sum_i\sum_{m\geq0}w_i\bigl((\xi_i z)^m a_m\bigr).
\]
Here the displayed products are scalar actions in Lean.  The generic
declarations \nolinkurl{summable_finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter_diagonal} and
\nolinkurl{finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_tsum} work over a commutative
semiring acting on a normed additive group.  If every \emph{weighted} sampled
series is unconditionally summable, they prove the exact diagonalization
\[
  \mathcal L_z(a)
  =\sum_{m\geq0}\left(\sum_iw_i\xi_i^m\right)z^m a_m.
\]
A zero weight therefore creates no convergence obligation at its node.

When the row reproduces a target through degree \(p\),
\nolinkurl{finiteAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_head_add_tail_of_exact} splits off
the exact finite head and leaves an explicit infinite tail.  Its target-zero
specialization preserves \(a_0\).  Over a commutative ring with continuous constant
scalar multiplication (each fixed scalar acts continuously), the geometric theorem
\nolinkurl{geometricAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_constant_add_gaussian_tsum}
identifies the finite row-moment factor in every residual tail term as
\[
  (-1)^p q^{\binom{p+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{p+r}{p}{q}.
\]
Assuming \(j\mapsto q^j\) is injective on \(\{0,\ldots,p\}\), the geometric-Lagrange
wrapper over a scalar field and its shifted-scale form are
\nolinkurl{geometricLagrangeAnalyticSeriesFilter_eq_constant_add_gaussian_tsum}
and \nolinkurl{geometricLagrangeAnalyticSeriesFilter_shifted}.  Their
summability hypotheses are imposed only at nodes whose Lagrange weight is
nonzero.

This is an exact pointwise \nolinkurl{tsum} theorem, using Mathlib's
unconditional summation semantics.  The generic module itself does not cover
conditionally convergent boundary series and proves no uniform convergence,
error bound, residual sign, or asymptotic acceleration rate.  Separately,
\nolinkurl{RvachevQBinomialFilter.lean} uses the named modes from
\nolinkurl{AnalyticMoments.lean} to instantiate the filter for the actual
infinite Rvachev sinc product, with only finite-power injectivity in the
generic complex theorem and no hypothesis at \(c=1/2\).  That theorem does
not identify the finite prefixes \(P_{b,N}\) or their quotient and Bell
coefficients; conditional-boundary, sign/bound, uniform/derivative, and
asymptotic claims remain separate.  A general bridge from an arbitrary formal
\nolinkurl{PowerSeries} evaluation to these coefficient hypotheses is also
still separate work.

\subsection{Uniform central remainders}
Formal coefficients alone do not prove an asymptotic expansion.  \nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleCentralAllOrders.lean} bounds the difference between the exact central integrand and its truncated polynomial approximation uniformly on the growing central window.

The window grows slowly enough that the normalized Taylor remainder tends to zero at the requested inverse-phase rate, but fast enough that the omitted Gaussian tail is negligible.  Choosing this window is a balancing act.  The proof records explicit inequalities among its powers of \(\lambda\), so later estimates reduce to comparisons of monomials.

\subsection{All-order tails}
\nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleTailAllOrders.lean}, \nolinkurl{GaussianPolynomialTailAllOrders.lean}, and \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertAllOrderRemainder.lean} show that tail errors are smaller than every prescribed inverse power.  Exponential decay is converted into polynomial decay using standard eventual inequalities:
\[
  e^{-c\lambda^\alpha}=O(\lambda^{-N})
  \quad\text{for every fixed }N.
\]
The source proves such comparisons with explicit thresholds and reuses them through Big-O transitivity.

\subsection{Assembling the normalized mass expansion}
\nolinkurl{FabiusSaddleMassAllOrders.lean} combines central coefficient extraction and tail flatness.  For each \(N\), it proves
\[
  \mathcal M(x)-\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{c_j(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^j}
  =O(\lambda(x)^{-N}).
\]
The theorem is formulated through a structure or predicate such as \nolinkurl{HasAsymptoticExpansion}, allowing generic lemmas for truncation, addition, multiplication, and logarithms.

Two generic interfaces make the all-order statement reusable.
\nolinkurl{SaddleExpansionFlat.lean} proves that, once one full expansion is
known, another function has the same coefficient family exactly when their
difference is \(O(\operatorname{scale}^N)\) for every \(N\); this is the
natural uniqueness statement modulo flat remainders without assuming a
nondegenerate scale.  \nolinkurl{SaddleExpansionSmallArgument.lean} transfers
full expansions in both directions between the exact logarithmic coordinate
and \(x\to0^+\), for scales in arbitrary normed fields and functions valued
in arbitrary normed vector spaces.

\subsection{From mass to logarithm}
Since \(c_0=1\), the normalized mass is eventually close to one.  A formal logarithm of the coefficient series gives
\[
  \log\mathcal M(x)
  \sim\sum_{j\geq1}\frac{d_j(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^j},
\]
where the \(d_j\) are again bounded period-one functions.  The analytic proof uses a Taylor theorem for \(\log(1+u)\) with a uniform remainder on a small neighborhood.  Positivity of the exact mass, inherited from the original integral representation, ensures the logarithm is well-defined.

At the formal level, \nolinkurl{SaddleLogExpansionAlgebra.lean} proves that
the recursive logarithm and exponential coefficient transforms are exact
inverses in every commutative \(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra.  Unit and zero constant-term
hypotheses give identities at all indices; at every positive index the
inverse identities need no normalization because the recurrences do not read
the zeroth input coefficient.  \nolinkurl{SaddleLogExpansionPowerSeries.lean}
then identifies the recursive logarithm with Mathlib's
\nolinkurl{PowerSeries.logOf}.

\nolinkurl{FabiusSecondSaddleCorrection.lean} computes \(d_2\) explicitly as a
finite polynomial in the first four bounded exponent jets, and
\nolinkurl{FabiusSecondSaddleExpansion.lean} substitutes it into the public
three-term statement:
\[
  \log F(x)=\operatorname{Main}(x)
   +\frac{A_1(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)}
   +\frac{A_2(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^2}
   +O\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-3}\right).
\]
Both corrections are explicit continuous period-one functions; later
regularity results may provide stronger smoothness where stated.  The theorem
does not assert that either correction is nowhere zero.

\subsection{Transfer to \texorpdfstring{\(F(x)\)}{F(x)}}
\nolinkurl{FabiusFullAsymptoticExpansion.lean} inserts the logarithmic mass expansion into the exact saddle reduction, adds the periodic and explicit main terms, and absorbs the exponentially small forward tail.  The result is an all-order logarithmic expansion for \(F(x)\) at zero.

The primary remainder is \(O(\lambda^{-N})\).  A weaker but simpler corollary uses \(\lambda(x)\asymp-\log x\) to obtain
\[
  O\bigl((-\log x)^{-N}\bigr).
\]

\subsection{First-order and Wikipedia-facing corollaries}
Modules with names such as \nolinkurl{FabiusWikipediaMain.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusWikipediaExpansion.lean} package concise formulas suitable for comparison with public summaries.  They separate what is correct at leading order from what is false at the claimed next order.  The obstruction module demonstrates that a nonconstant dyadic-periodic correction cannot be absorbed into a vanishing error.

\subsection{The proof-audit role of the asymptotic aggregator}
\nolinkurl{PaperFabiusAsymptotic.lean} is not just a theorem list.  Its header records which informal claims are supported, which need corrected domains or periodic terms, and which are disproved.  The formal development therefore functions as an audit trail:
\begin{itemize}
  \item coarse log-squared behavior is validated;
  \item the exact delay relation is restricted to its true domain;
  \item a claimed overly small residual is refuted;
  \item the periodic Lambert-phase formula is proved independently through transforms and saddles; and
  \item arbitrary-order corrections are established at the exact phase.
\end{itemize}

\section{Legendre expansions and polynomial approximation}\label{ch:legendre}

\subsection{Why Legendre polynomials are a natural basis}
The up-function is supported on \([-1,1]\), is even, and is infinitely differentiable.  Legendre polynomials are orthogonal on exactly this interval with respect to Lebesgue measure, so they provide a canonical spectral expansion:
\[
  \RvachevUp(x)=\sum_{n\geq0}a_nP_n(x).
\]
Evenness forces every odd coefficient to vanish.  The repository does not stop at a formal \(L^2\) expansion: it evaluates the even coefficients exactly through moments, proves absolute and uniform convergence, translates the series back to the global Fabius function, and proves least-squares optimality of finite truncations.

\subsection{Building the polynomial basis}
\nolinkurl{LegendrePolynomial.lean} develops the required Legendre facts in the normalization used by the project.  The polynomials are defined through Rodrigues' formula or an equivalent derivative representation:
\[
  P_n(x)=\frac{1}{2^n n!}
          \frac{\Differential^n}{\Differential x^n}(x^2-1)^n.
\]
The module proves degree, parity, endpoint values, differential equation, and derivative bounds.

A bespoke development is useful because later coefficient formulas require explicit monomial coefficients.  A black-box orthogonal-polynomial theorem would provide orthogonality but not necessarily the exact finite sum compatible with the moment sequence.

\subsection{Sturm--Liouville orthogonality}
The Legendre differential equation is
\[
  \bigl((1-x^2)P_n'(x)\bigr)'+n(n+1)P_n(x)=0.
\]
Multiplying the equation for \(P_n\) by \(P_m\), the equation for \(P_m\) by \(P_n\), subtracting, and integrating gives
\[
  \bigl(n(n+1)-m(m+1)\bigr)
  \int_{-1}^{1}P_n(x)P_m(x)\,\Differential x=0.
\]
Boundary terms vanish because of the factor \(1-x^2\).  For \(m\ne n\), the eigenvalues differ, proving orthogonality.

\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreSeries.lean} carries out this argument using interval integration by parts.  The proof explicitly verifies differentiability of the polynomial expressions and endpoint vanishing.  The eigenvalue inequality is discharged in natural arithmetic and cast to reals only at the final multiplication step.

\subsection{Norms and pointwise bounds}
The exact norm is
\[
  \int_{-1}^{1}P_n(x)^2\,\Differential x=\frac{2}{2n+1}.
\]
The source derives it from Rodrigues' formula and repeated integration by parts, or from a recurrence plus normalization depending on the helper theorem used.  This norm fixes the Fourier--Legendre coefficient:
\[
  a_n=\frac{2n+1}{2}
      \int_{-1}^{1}\RvachevUp(x)P_n(x)\,\Differential x.
\]

A bound \(|P_n(x)|\leq1\) on \([-1,1]\) is proved from the differential equation and an extremum argument.  This simple uniform bound is the last ingredient needed to convert absolute convergence of coefficients into uniform convergence of the function series.

\subsection{Vanishing of odd coefficients}
The parity theorem states
\[
  P_n(-x)=(-1)^nP_n(x).
\]
Since \(\RvachevUp\) is even, the integrand \(\RvachevUp(x)P_n(x)\) is odd when \(n\) is odd.  A symmetric-interval integral of an odd integrable function is zero.  In Lean, the proof either invokes a generic odd-integral lemma or performs the substitution \(x\mapsto-x\) and adds the two integral identities.

The resulting series is indexed naturally by \(P_{2n}\).  This is not only cosmetic: the exact coefficient formula then uses the even moment \(M_{2k}\), for which the arithmetic normalization and denominator theory are available.

\subsection{Exact coefficient evaluation}
\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreCoefficients.lean} expands \(P_{2n}\) in monomials:
\[
  P_{2n}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}c_{n,k}x^{2k}.
\]
Finite linearity of the integral gives
\[
  a_{2n}=\frac{4n+1}{2}
          \sum_{k=0}^{n}c_{n,k}M_{2k}.
\]
Every \(M_{2k}\) is the exact rational \nolinkurl{moment} value, so \(a_{2n}\) is rational.  The source also substitutes formulas expressing moments through inverse-power Fabius values, producing a coefficient formula entirely in terms of the exact dyadic table.

The proof is layered:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item establish the explicit polynomial coefficient theorem;
  \item turn polynomial evaluation into a finite sum;
  \item commute the finite sum with the integral;
  \item replace each monomial integral by the analytic moment;
  \item identify the moment with the exact rational sequence; and
  \item simplify casts and normalizations.
\end{enumerate}
At no point is an infinite series exchanged with the integral during coefficient evaluation.

\subsection{Generic convergence infrastructure}
\nolinkurl{LegendreSeriesConvergence.lean} develops a theorem for smooth functions whose repeated derivatives satisfy suitable endpoint or norm bounds.  Integration by parts transfers derivatives from the function to the Legendre eigenstructure, yielding decay of coefficients faster than any fixed power of \(n\).

For the Fabius up-function, compact support and smoothness supply these hypotheses to every order.  Consequently the coefficient sequence is absolutely summable.  Since \(|P_n(x)|\leq1\), the Weierstrass M-test gives uniform convergence on \([-1,1]\).

The generic theorem is separated from Fabius-specific coefficient arithmetic.  This permits future reuse for other compactly supported smooth functions and keeps the main convergence proof independent of exact moment formulas.

\subsection{Identifying the uniform limit}
Orthogonality first shows that the partial sums are the \(L^2\) projections of \(\RvachevUp\).  Absolute/uniform convergence produces a continuous limit \(S\).  To prove \(S=\RvachevUp\), the source may use one of two equivalent routes:
\begin{itemize}
  \item completeness of Legendre polynomials in \(L^2[-1,1]\), followed by equality of continuous representatives; or
  \item a generic convergence theorem already identifying the pointwise limit for sufficiently smooth functions.
\end{itemize}
The final theorem states a \nolinkurl{HasSum} at each point, then upgrades to uniform convergence of the function series.

Endpoints are included.  This matters because pointwise convergence theorems for orthogonal series often state only interior convergence.  The absolute coefficient bound and \(|P_n(\pm1)|=1\) handle endpoints directly.

\subsection{Translated series for the global Fabius function}
\nolinkurl{FabiusTranslatedLegendreSeries.lean} transports the up-function expansion to an interval such as \([0,2]\) where a translate agrees with the signed global Fabius function.  The transformation replaces \(P_{2n}(x)\) by \(P_{2n}(x-1)\) or the corresponding affine normalization.

The module also expands translated Legendre polynomials into ordinary monomials.  This produces an explicit power-polynomial series in the translated coordinate, with exact rational coefficients.  Uniform convergence follows from the original series under composition with the affine map.

\subsection{Least-squares optimality}
\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreLeastSquares.lean} proves that the partial Legendre sum is the unique least-squares approximant among polynomials of the corresponding degree.  Evenness gives a useful strengthening: the visible even sum through \(P_{2N}\) is the full orthogonal projection through degree \(2N+1\), because the next odd coefficient vanishes.  It is therefore uniquely least-squares optimal among \emph{all} real polynomials of degree at most \(2N+1\), one degree beyond its own visible degree.  Let
\[
  S_N^{\mathrm{even}}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}a_{2n}P_{2n}.
\]
For any polynomial \(p\) with \(\deg p\leq2N+1\),
\[
  \lVert\RvachevUp-p\rVert_2^2
  =\lVert\RvachevUp-S_N^{\mathrm{even}}\rVert_2^2
   +\lVert S_N^{\mathrm{even}}-p\rVert_2^2.
\]
This Pythagorean identity follows because
\(\RvachevUp-S_N^{\mathrm{even}}\) is orthogonal to every polynomial in that
degree class.

The formal proof represents the finite span either as a submodule of \(L^2\)-type functions or directly through polynomial coefficients.  It proves orthogonality term by term and then expands the square of the norm.  Uniqueness follows because equality of errors forces
\(\lVert S_N^{\mathrm{even}}-p\rVert_2=0\), hence equality almost everywhere;
polynomial continuity upgrades this to pointwise polynomial equality.

The later module \nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreEnergy.lean} closes the coefficient
energy side of the same expansion.  It defines the polynomial-form blocks
\(B_n=u_nP_{2n}\) and proves their complete orthogonality, including the
diagonal value \(2u_n^2/(4n+1)\).  It then proves Parseval for \(\RvachevUp\), the
exact shifted coefficient tail for every partial-sum squared error in both
\nolinkurl{HasSum} and \nolinkurl{tsum} form, and
\[
  \int_0^1 F(t)^2\,dt
  =\sum_{n\geq0}\frac{u_n^2}{4n+1}.
\]
The block is defined directly as a polynomial.  These declarations do not
formalize its finite up-translate realization, the separate Fourier-product
or infinite-sinc integral for this energy, or rationality of its partial sums.

\subsection{Executable Gaunt coefficients and finite up-law Gram sums}
\label{ch:legendre-gaunt}

The post-snapshot module \nolinkurl{LegendreGaunt.lean} closes the finite
triple-product calculation over the rationals.  Its exhaustive public API consists of
four definitions,
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat}, and twelve theorems,
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat_even},
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat_odd},
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat_cast},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_momentFunctional},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_cast},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_swap_left},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_swap_right},
\nolinkurl{legendrePolynomial_mul_eq_sum_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{legendrePolynomialRat_mul_eq_sum_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_odd_sum},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_add_lt}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_triangle_violation}.
Writing \(\mu_n^{\mathrm L}\) for
\nolinkurl{legendreLebesgueMomentRat}\((n)\), the definition and its first three
theorems say exactly
\[
 \mu_n^{\mathrm L}=
 \begin{cases}
   2/(n+1),&n\ \text{even},\\
   0,&n\ \text{odd},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 (\mu_n^{\mathrm L}:\RealNumbers)=\int_{-1}^{1}x^n\,\Differential x.
\]
Thus \(\mu_{2n}^{\mathrm L}=2/(2n+1)\) and
\(\mu_{2n+1}^{\mathrm L}=0\).  If \(p_{n,a}=[X^a]P_n\) denotes the executable
rational Legendre coefficient, the rational Gaunt definition is the bounded sum
\[
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}
 =\sum_{a=0}^{i}\sum_{b=0}^{j}\sum_{c=0}^{k}
   p_{i,a}p_{j,b}p_{k,c}\,\mu_{a+b+c}^{\mathrm L},
\]
while \nolinkurl{legendreGaunt}\((i,j,k)\) is
\[
 G_{ijk}=\int_{-1}^{1}P_i(x)P_j(x)P_k(x)\,\Differential x,
 \qquad
 L_{ijk}=\frac{2k+1}{2}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}
\]
is \nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat}\((i,j,k)\).
The moment-functional theorem identifies the bounded sum algebraically, and the cast
theorem gives \((G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}:\RealNumbers)=G_{ijk}\).  The two swap
theorems exchange the first pair or the last pair of indices.  The real and rational
product theorems then give, without extra hypotheses,
\[
 P_iP_j=\sum_{k=0}^{i+j}L_{ijk}P_k
\]
in \(\RealNumbers[X]\) and \(\RationalNumbers[X]\), respectively.

All four definitions are total at every \(i,j,k\in\NaturalNumbers\).  The only
hypotheses in the public theorem surface occur in the three support results:
\(\operatorname{Odd}(i+j+k)\) implies \(G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}=0\);
\(i+j<k\) implies the same; and any strict triangle violation
\[
 i+j<k\ \lor\ i+k<j\ \lor\ j+k<i
\]
implies the same.  These are necessary support zeros only.  There is no converse
support theorem, Wigner \(3j\) identification, factorial closed form, positivity
theorem, or asymptotic assertion in \nolinkurl{LegendreGaunt.lean} itself.  The
following focused module closes the first four of those gaps at the integer-index,
zero-magnetic-row square level.

The exhaustive public API of \nolinkurl{LegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} consists of two
definitions,
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible} and
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and twenty-five theorems,
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_iff_exists_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add_factorial},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_factorial_of_halfSum},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pairwise_add_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_eq_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_pos_iff_admissible}.

Admissibility means even total degree and the three weak triangle inequalities; it is
equivalent to
\[
 (i,j,k)=(b+c,a+c,a+b)
 \qquad(a,b,c\in\NaturalNumbers).
\]
The total rational datum \(W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers}\) is zero outside this support.
In pairwise-sum coordinates it is
\[
 W_{b+c,a+c,a+b}^{2,\RationalNumbers}
 =\frac{\binom{2a}{a}\binom{2b}{b}\binom{2c}{c}}
 {(2(a+b+c)+1)\binom{2(a+b+c)}{a+b+c}},
\]
with both pairwise-coordinate and half-sum factorial forms.  The half-sum theorem assumes
exactly \(i+j+k=2s\) and \(i,j,k\leq s\).  The global identities are
\[
 G_{ijk}^{\RationalNumbers}=2W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers},
 \qquad
 G_{ijk}=2\bigl(W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers}:\RealNumbers\bigr),
 \qquad
 L_{ijk}=(2k+1)W_{ijk}^{2,\RationalNumbers}.
\]
The square datum and both Gaunt coefficients are positive exactly on the admissible
support, vanish exactly off it, and are nonnegative everywhere; \(L_{ijk}\) has the
same sharp positivity support.  Separate theorems cover \(k=i+j\) and \(i=0\).
This remains deliberately narrower than a Wigner-symbol library: there is no signed
symbol or phase convention, half-integer or nonzero-magnetic-index theory, general
\(3j/6j/9j\) symbol, Wigner orthogonality, or recoupling identity.

The companion \nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreGaunt.lean} converts those product
linearizations into finite formulas for the Rvachev Legendre Gram entries.  Its
exhaustive public API has one definition,
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat}, and eight theorems,
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_even},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_odd},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_cast},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_eq_normalized_moment},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_sum_full_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_sum_gaunt},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat_apply_eq_sum_gaunt}, and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_sum_gaunt}.
If \(c_r^{\RationalNumbers}\) is
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat}\((r)\), its full extension is
\[
 \FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}=
 \begin{cases}
   c_{k/2}^{\RationalNumbers},&2\mid k,\\
   0,&2\nmid k.
 \end{cases}
\]
The even and odd theorems give
\(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{2n}=c_n^{\RationalNumbers}\) and
\(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{2n+1}=0\).  Unconditionally in \(k\), the
normalized-moment theorem says that \(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}\) is
\((2k+1)/2\) times \nolinkurl{momentFunctional} with moment sequence
\nolinkurl{rvachevRawMomentRat}, evaluated at
\nolinkurl{legendrePolynomialRat}\((k)\).  In abbreviated notation,
\[
 \FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}
 =\frac{2k+1}{2}\,\mathcal M_{\mathrm{Rv}}(P_k^{\RationalNumbers}).
\]
For arbitrary natural \(i,j\), write \(R_{ij}^{\RationalNumbers}\) for
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat}\((i,j)\).  The two rational entry
theorems state exactly
\[
 R_{ij}^{\RationalNumbers}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{i+j}\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}
    G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}c_r^{\RationalNumbers}
    G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ij,2r}.
\]
The rational matrix theorem supplies the second equality entrywise for every
\(i,j\in\operatorname{Fin} n\).

Exactly two public theorems in this companion require analytic hypotheses.
Given \(F\) of type \nolinkurl{BoundedFabius} and a proof \(h_F\) of
\nolinkurl{IsFabius F},
\nolinkurl{canonicalRvachevFullLegendreCoefficientRat_cast} identifies the real cast
of \(\FullRvachevLegendreCoefficientRat{k}\) with
\nolinkurl{rvachevFullLegendreCoefficient}\((F,k)\), and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_sum_gaunt} states, writing
\(D^F_{n,ij}\) for \nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix}\((F,n,i,j)\),
\[
 D^F_{n,ij}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}c_r(F)\,G_{ij,2r},
\]
where \(c_r(F)\) is \nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreCoefficient}\((F,r)\), for
\(i,j\in\operatorname{Fin} n\).  The other six theorems are unconditional finite
identities.  No infinite Legendre series is interchanged with integration, and the
module itself proves no Wigner-square or factorial formula, Christoffel reconstruction,
or new positivity or asymptotic result.  The preceding
\nolinkurl{LegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} supplies the integer zero-row Wigner-square,
factorial, sharp-support, and positivity results independently of this companion.

The final finite composition leaf
\nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} has no public definitions and exactly
three public theorems:
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}.
For arbitrary natural \(i,j\), the first is the unconditional rational identity
\[
 R_{ij}^{\RationalNumbers}
 =2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}
 c_r^{\RationalNumbers}W_{ij,2r}^{2,\RationalNumbers}.
\]
The second packages the same formula entrywise for the executable rational Gram matrix.
The third assumes exactly \(F\) of type \nolinkurl{BoundedFabius} and
\(h_F\) of type \nolinkurl{IsFabius F}, replacing the rational coefficient by
\(c_r(F)\) and the square datum by its real cast in the genuine up-law Gram matrix.
The construction is a finite substitution only.  It chooses no signed phase and adds no
half-integer, nonzero-magnetic-index, general recoupling, orthogonality, or
infinite-series result.

\subsubsection{Expanded zero-row Wigner-square formula and proof ledger}
\label{ch:legendre-gaunt-closed-form}

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Source/PDF version notice.}\space
The retained 126-page walkthrough PDF in this directory was rebuilt from the incoming
source and already contains the exhaustive two-definition, twenty-eight-theorem
closed-form inventory.  The merged TeX source retains that material and adds the expanded
formula-and-proof ledger in this subsubsection.  Because the PDF was not regenerated after
this merge, exact source/PDF synchronization is not claimed at this checkpoint.
\end{boxedremark}

The limitations just stated remain exact descriptions of the two predecessor modules.
The downstream core \nolinkurl{LegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} closes precisely the
integer-index, zero-row \emph{squared} problem.  It has exactly two public definitions:
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible} and
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat}.  Write
\[
 \mathsf A(i,j,k)\Longleftrightarrow
 (i+j+k)\bmod 2=0\ \land\
 i\le j+k\ \land\ j\le i+k\ \land\ k\le i+j,
\]
put \(C_n=\binom{2n}{n}\), and let \(s=\Floor{(i+j+k)/2}\).  The second definition
is the total rational datum
\[
 W^2_{ijk}=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac{C_{s-i}C_{s-j}C_{s-k}}{(2s+1)C_s},
     &\mathsf A(i,j,k),\\[0.8em]
 0,&\neg\mathsf A(i,j,k).
 \end{cases}
\]
The notation \(W^2\) is expository: Lean defines this square datum directly, not as the
square of a separately defined signed symbol.

Its exhaustive public API then has exactly twenty-five theorems, in source order:
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_iff_exists_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntAdmissible_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pairwise_add_factorial},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_factorial_of_halfSum},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_add_boundary_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_zero_left_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pairwise_add_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_of_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_two_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreWignerThreeJZeroSqRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_pos_iff_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_eq_zero_iff_not_admissible},
\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreGaunt_nonneg},
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_eq_mul_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat_pos_iff_admissible}.

The first structural theorem gives the exact triangle-coordinate parametrization
\[
 \mathsf A(i,j,k)\Longleftrightarrow
 \exists a,b,c\in\NaturalNumbers,\qquad
 i=b+c,\quad j=a+c,\quad k=a+b.
\]
If \(S=a+b+c\), the two closed forms on this support are
\[
 W^2_{b+c,a+c,a+b}
 =\frac{C_aC_bC_c}{(2S+1)C_S}
 =\frac{(S!)^2(2a)!(2b)!(2c)!}
 {(2S+1)!(a!)^2(b!)^2(c!)^2}.
\]
The separately named half-sum theorem says that, under the explicit hypotheses
\(i+j+k=2s\) and \(i,j,k\le s\),
\[
 W^2_{ijk}
 =\frac{(s!)^2(2(s-i))!(2(s-j))!(2(s-k))!}
 {(2s+1)!((s-i)!)^2((s-j)!)^2((s-k)!)^2}.
\]
Those dominance hypotheses matter because all indices and subtractions in the Lean
statement are natural-number operations.

For \(G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}=\nolinkurl{legendreGauntRat}(i,j,k)\), the public
boundary formulas are
\[
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j,i+j}
 =\frac{2C_iC_j}{(2(i+j)+1)C_{i+j}}
 =2W^2_{i,j,i+j},
\qquad
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{0,j,k}
 =\begin{cases}2/(2j+1),&j=k,\\0,&j\ne k,\end{cases}
 =2W^2_{0,j,k}.
\]
The identification is global, not merely a necessary support statement:
\[
 G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}=2W^2_{ijk},\qquad
 \nolinkurl{legendreGaunt}(i,j,k)=2(W^2_{ijk}:\RealNumbers).
\]
For each of \(W^2_{ijk}\), \(G^{\RationalNumbers}_{ijk}\), and the real Gaunt integral,
the API separately proves positivity exactly on \(\mathsf A\), zero exactly off
\(\mathsf A\), and global nonnegativity.  Finally,
\[
 \nolinkurl{legendreProductLinearizationCoeffRat}(i,j,k)
 =(2k+1)W^2_{ijk},
\]
and that coefficient is positive exactly on \(\mathsf A(i,j,k)\).

\begin{proofidea}
The proof stays inside the executable rational Legendre layer.  Let
\(\lambda_n=[X^n]P_n=2^{-n}C_n\).  In the finite product linearization of \(P_iP_j\),
only its \(P_{i+j}\) term reaches degree \(i+j\), so
\[
 \lambda_i\lambda_j
 =\frac{2(i+j)+1}{2}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j,i+j}\lambda_{i+j}.
\]
Solving this identity gives the degenerate-triangle boundary above.  The zero-left law
uses the diagonal boundary together with the already proved triangle zeros.

The private engine next derives the rational Bonnet recurrence
\[
 XP_n=\frac{n+1}{2n+1}P_{n+1}+\frac n{2n+1}P_{n-1}.
\]
Applying the rational moment functional to
\((XP_i)P_jP_k=P_i(XP_j)P_k\) gives
\[
 \frac{i+1}{2i+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i+1,j,k}
 +\frac{i}{2i+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i-1,j,k}
 =\frac{j+1}{2j+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j+1,k}
 +\frac{j}{2j+1}G^{\RationalNumbers}_{i,j-1,k}.
\]
The proposed central-binomial quotient obeys the corresponding recurrence by
\((n+1)C_{n+1}=2(2n+1)C_n\).  A nested induction in the pairwise coordinates uses the
degenerate-triangle values on its two boundary faces, the recurrence in the interior,
and cancellation of a strictly positive rational leading coefficient.  Thus
\(G^{\RationalNumbers}=2W^2\) for all pairwise-sum triples.  The coordinate equivalence
covers the admissible support; the predecessor parity and strict-triangle zeros settle
its complement.  Replacing \(C_n\) by \((2n)!/(n!)^2\), and using positivity of central
binomials, yields the factorial, exact-support, and sign packages.  The real result is
then the existing cast theorem.  In particular, the proof imports no external Wigner
integral evaluation or representation-theory theorem.
\end{proofidea}

The wrapper \nolinkurl{FabiusLegendreGauntClosedForm.lean} has no public definition and
exactly three public theorems:
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat},
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}, and
\nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix_apply_eq_two_mul_sum_wignerThreeJZeroSqRat}.
The first two are unconditional rational identities:
\[
 R^{\RationalNumbers}_{ij}
 =2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{(i+j)/2}}
 c_r^{\RationalNumbers}W^2_{i,j,2r},
\]
first for \nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramEntryRat} and then entrywise for
\nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreGramMatrixRat}.  The third requires
\(F:\nolinkurl{BoundedFabius}\) and \(h_F:\nolinkurl{IsFabius F}\), and for
\(i,j:\operatorname{Fin}n\) states
\[
 \nolinkurl{upLegendreGramMatrix}(F,n,i,j)
 =2\sum_{r=0}^{\Floor{((i:\NaturalNumbers)+(j:\NaturalNumbers))/2}}
 \nolinkurl{rvachevLegendreCoefficient}(F,r)
 \left(W^2_{(i:\NaturalNumbers),(j:\NaturalNumbers),2r}:\RealNumbers\right).
\]
All three sums are finite, with Lean's actual index set
\(\nolinkurl{range}(\Floor{(i+j)/2}+1)\).  Their proofs simply substitute the global
Gaunt identity into the three predecessor sums and move the scalar \(2\) outside.

The boundary of this API is deliberate and exact.  It defines only a total rational
square datum for natural angular indices and a zero magnetic row.  There is no signed
symbol and no Condon--Shortley or other phase convention; in particular, there is no Lean
theorem whose left side is the square of a formal signed Wigner term.  There are no
half-integer or nonzero-magnetic-index symbols, no general \(3j\), \(6j\), or \(9j\)
theory, and no Wigner orthogonality or recoupling result.  The wrapper is a finite-sum
transport only and asserts no infinite Legendre-series interchange.

\subsection{Computational significance}
The exact coefficient theorem gives a certified way to produce rational polynomial approximants.  The least-squares theorem certifies optimality in \(L^2\), while uniform convergence certifies pointwise approximation.  This is a rare combination:
\begin{itemize}
  \item exact arithmetic coefficients;
  \item kernel-checked analytic convergence; and
  \item an optimality theorem for every finite truncation.
\end{itemize}
The evaluator and moment recurrences can be used to compute coefficients without numerical integration.

\section{The k-fold Thue--Morse draft audit}\label{ch:kfold}

\subsection{Purpose of the audit}
\nolinkurl{PaperKFoldThueMorse.lean} collects a separate line of work concerning k-fold Thue--Morse constructions and a related informal draft.  Its role is both constructive and diagnostic: it proves exact identities and corrected approximation statements, but it also formalizes counterexamples to several literal claims in the draft.

This part of the repository illustrates a mature use of formalization.  A failed proof is not treated as an obstacle to be papered over; the surrounding hypotheses are tested, false versions are refuted, and repaired versions are isolated.

\subsection{Exact finite identities}
The Thue--Morse modules prove prefix-sum cancellation, long zero runs of finite differences, convolution formulas, and generating-series identities.  Repeated finite difference annihilates polynomials below a threshold degree, which explains the high-order cancellation of the sign sequence.

The full row-level symmetry is sharper than the endpoint cancellation.  If
$k,r,d\in\NaturalNumbers$, $k\le r$, and $k+d<2^r$, then
\[
 \sigma_k(2^r-k-1-d)=(-1)^{r-k}\sigma_k(d),
 \qquad
 \sigma_k(2^r-k-1-d)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \sigma_k(d)=0.
\]
These are exactly
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_reverse_window} and
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_reverse_window_eq_zero_iff} in
\nolinkurl{ThueMorsePrefix.lean}.  At $k=0$, the identity is the binary
complement theorem \nolinkurl{thueMorseSign_dyadic_complement} from
\nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean}; at $d=0$, the statement specializes, using
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_at_zero}, namely $\sigma_k(0)=1$, to the sharp left
boundary \nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_before_dyadic_run} of the final dyadic zero run.  The
terminal zero run and the value $-1$ at its right boundary are separate
theorems, exposed by \nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_endpoint},
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_zero_reverse},
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_dyadic_zero_run}, and
\nolinkurl{iteratedPrefix_at_dyadic}.  In particular, the reflected-zero
equivalence pairs zeros in the pre-run window but does not assert that the
window is zero-free.

The proof mirrors the finite-difference geometry.  Dyadic bit complementation
settles order zero, the left boundary seeds offset zero, and a nested induction
uses
$\sigma_{k+1}(n+1)-\sigma_{k+1}(n)=\sigma_k(n+1)$ to propagate the same signed
reflection in both prefix order and offset.  Since the sign is always
$\pm1$, the zero-locus equivalence follows without any cancellation
hypothesis.

The formal proofs use induction on binary depth and repeated even/odd splitting.  Convolution associativity is kept finite, so all sums are over \nolinkurl{Finset.range} and can be reindexed explicitly.  Generating polynomials turn convolution into multiplication, allowing coefficient comparison to replace nested sum manipulations.

\nolinkurl{ThueMorseBinomialLog.lean} also makes the zero--one convention
exact.  For the integer sum
\(X(n)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{\binom nk}\), it proves
\(n+1-X(n)=2^{\operatorname{wt}(n)+1}\), so the ensuing base-two logarithm
is exact.  If \(t_n=\operatorname{wt}(n)\bmod2\), the resulting rational
identity is
\[
 t_n=\frac{1+(-1)^{\log_2(n+1-X(n))}}2.
\]
The module also identifies, in every ring,
\((-1)^{t_n}\) with the cast of the signed Thue--Morse convention used by the
real and complex \(q\)-series.

\subsection{The failed polygon and the corrected grid samples}
The literal grid of the draft uses
\(S^k_j/2^{\binom{k}{2}}\) at \(j/2^k\).  At \(x=1\) its value is
\(-2^{-\binom{k}{2}}\), so neither the grid nor either intended polygonal
interpolation can converge there to a function with value one.  This is
formalized by \nolinkurl{paperPrefixGridValue_endpoint_not_tendsto_one},
\nolinkurl{paperPrefixPolygon_endpoint_not_tendsto_one}, and
\nolinkurl{paperPrefixPolygonReal_endpoint_not_tendsto_one} in
\nolinkurl{ThueMorseGenerating.lean}.

The repaired theorem uses the shifted, dyadic-floor samples
\nolinkurl{correctedPrefixGridSample}, not a continuous polygon.  Away from the
right endpoint each sample is exactly \nolinkurl{stepApproximant n} at
\nolinkurl{correctedPrefixCellCenter n x}; those centers tend to \(2x-1\).  The
endpoint is handled separately, giving pointwise convergence on \([0,1]\) to
\(\RvachevUp(2x-1)\), and sampling at \(x/2\) recovers \(F(x)\).  No quantitative
uniform rate or continuity of the sample function is asserted.

\subsection{Formal counterexamples}
\nolinkurl{DraftCounterexamples.lean} gives exact witnesses for three distinct
failures.  At \(k=2,j=1\), the proposed slope is \(-2\), whereas
\(F'(1/4)=1\); the error is therefore \(3>4h=1\), contradicting the claimed
local bound.  For every \(x>0\), the proxy ratio from equation~(7) is
\[
  \frac{x\,2^{n+1}}{n+1}\longrightarrow+\infty,
\]
so the terms are unbounded and the displayed maximum does not exist.
Finally, under the draft's equation~(8), the Stirling proxy retains an
additive \(+n\) term; equation~(10) cannot absorb it into \(O(\log n)\).

These are arithmetic or elementary asymptotic counterexamples, not
subsequence arguments imported from the coarse Fabius endpoint theorem.  Each
negative result is stated independently of the prose whose claim it refutes.

\subsection{Repairing the lower Lambert branch}
The draft uses a lower branch of a Lambert-type inverse.  The repository
develops both real branches with explicit domains, order, and inversion
theorems; no appeal is made to an ambiguous multivalued inverse.  The raw
endpoint continuity layer is now closed: in \nolinkurl{LowerLambertW.lean},
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_continuousWithinAt_branchPoint} and
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_continuousOn_Ico} give continuity of \(W_{-1}\) on
\([-\exp(-1),0)\), while \nolinkurl{PrincipalLambertW.lean} supplies
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_continuousWithinAt_branchPoint} and
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_continuousOn_Ici} on
\([-\exp(-1),\infty)\).  At the ordinary point zero,
\nolinkurl{deriv_principalLambertW_zero} states
\(\operatorname{deriv}W_0(0)=1\), and
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_isEquivalent_zero} packages \(W_0(z)\sim z\).
The later module \nolinkurl{LambertWCurvature.lean} closes the regular-domain
second-derivative and shape layer.  On either smooth branch it uses the
nonsingular exponential form
\[
 W''(z)=-\frac{e^{-2W(z)}(W(z)+2)}{(W(z)+1)^3}.
\]
For the principal branch, \nolinkurl{deriv_principalLambertW},
\nolinkurl{deriv_principalLambertW_hasDerivAt}, and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW} give the first derivative and the
derivative-of-derivative formula throughout
\((-e^{-1},\infty)\), including \(z=0\).  The simplification
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW_zero} gives \(W_0''(0)=-2\),
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW_neg} proves strict negativity on the
open domain, and \nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_principalLambertW} extends strict
concavity to the closed natural domain \([-e^{-1},\infty)\).

For \(W_{-1}\), \nolinkurl{deriv_lowerLambertW_hasDerivAt} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW} give the same formula on
\((-e^{-1},0)\).  The three exact sign declarations
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_pos_iff},
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_neg_iff}, and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW_eq_zero_iff} locate the unique change at
\(z=-2e^{-2}\): the second derivative is positive to its left, negative to
its right, and zero exactly there.  Consequently
\nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_lowerLambertW_left} proves strict convexity on
\([-e^{-1},-2e^{-2}]\), while
\nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_lowerLambertW_right} proves strict concavity on
\([-2e^{-2},0)\).  These are regular-interior derivative results plus shape
theorems that include the finite branch point and shared inflection endpoint;
the lower domain remains open at zero.

The next two raw-branch leaves resolve the formerly open branch-point
geometry.  Write
\[
 b=-\exp(-1),\qquad \Delta(z)=z-b=z+\exp(-1),\qquad
 \mathcal S(z)=\sqrt{2\exp(1)\bigl(z+\exp(-1)\bigr)}.
\]
All limits here use the right-hand filter \(z\downarrow b\).  The compiled
statements are
\[
 \operatorname{deriv}W_0(z)\longrightarrow+\infty,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{deriv}W_{-1}(z)\longrightarrow-\infty,
\]
and, for the secants drawn from the common endpoint \((b,-1)\),
\[
 \frac{W_0(z)+1}{z+\exp(-1)}\longrightarrow+\infty,
 \qquad
 \frac{W_{-1}(z)+1}{z+\exp(-1)}\longrightarrow-\infty.
\]
In particular neither branch has a finite right derivative: both fail

\begin{center}
\(\operatorname{DifferentiableWithinAt}_{\RealNumbers}
  (W_j,\operatorname{Ioi}(b),b)\).
\end{center}

The two totalized all-real branch functions also fail

\begin{center}
\(\operatorname{DifferentiableAt}_{\RealNumbers}(W_j,b)\).
\end{center}

The human proof follows the source layering.  Endpoint continuity gives
\(W_j(z)\to-1\), and the inverse derivative is
\[
 \frac{1}{\exp(W_j(z))(W_j(z)+1)}.
\]
