Its denominator tends to zero from above on the principal branch and from
below on the lower branch, giving the two signed infinite derivative limits.
Principal concavity bounds its endpoint secant from below by the moving
derivative; lower convexity on the left shape interval bounds its endpoint
secant from above by the moving derivative.  Those inequalities force the
same signed limits for the secants.  A standard one-sided derivative-divergence
lemma rules out a finite within-set derivative, and ordinary differentiability
would imply that prohibited right-hand differentiability.

The complete public surface of
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointGeometry.lean} is exactly these eight theorems:
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.51\textwidth}
                         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.43\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endhead
\nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_principalLambertW_branchPoint_atTop} & Principal derivative tends to \(+\infty\) from the right. \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_lowerLambertW_branchPoint_atBot} & Lower derivative tends to \(-\infty\) from the right. \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_principalLambertW_secantSlope_branchPoint_atTop} & Principal endpoint secant slope tends to \(+\infty\). \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_lowerLambertW_secantSlope_branchPoint_atBot} & Lower endpoint secant slope tends to \(-\infty\). \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_not_differentiableWithinAt_branchPoint} & No finite principal right derivative on \(\operatorname{Ioi}(b)\). \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_not_differentiableWithinAt_branchPoint} & No finite lower right derivative on \(\operatorname{Ioi}(b)\). \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_not_differentiableAt_branchPoint} & The totalized principal branch is not differentiable at \(b\). \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_not_differentiableAt_branchPoint} & The totalized lower branch is not differentiable at \(b\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The asymptotic leaf proves the first square-root term.  For the forward map
\(\phi(w)=w\exp w\), one application of L'H\^opital's rule gives
\[
 \frac{\phi(w)+\exp(-1)}{(w+1)^2}
 \longrightarrow\frac{\exp(-1)}2
 \qquad(w\to-1,\ w\ne-1),
\]
because the differentiated quotient cancels to \(\exp(w)/2\) away from
\(-1\).  Composing with each branch, rewriting
\(W_j(z)\exp(W_j(z))=z\), and inverting the nonzero limit yields
\[
 \frac{(W_j(z)+1)^2}{z+\exp(-1)}\longrightarrow2\exp(1),
 \qquad j\in\{0,-1\}.
\]
Thus both squared displacements are asymptotic to
\(2\exp(1)(z+\exp(-1))\).  The branch signs select the square root, with no
sign ambiguity:
\[
 W_0(z)+1\sim+\mathcal S(z),
 \qquad
 W_{-1}(z)+1\sim-\mathcal S(z)
 \qquad(z\downarrow b).
\]

The complete public inventory of
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointAsymptotics.lean} is one noncomputable
definition and eight theorems:
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.51\textwidth}
                         >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.43\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endhead
\nolinkurl{lambertWBranchPointScale} & Definition of \(\sqrt{2\exp(1)(z+\exp(-1))}\). \\
\nolinkurl{lambertWBranchPointScale_pos} & Positivity for \(b<z\). \\
\nolinkurl{lambertWBranchPointScale_sq} & Exact scale-square identity for \(b<z\). \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_principalLambertW_add_one_sq_div_branchPoint} & Principal squared-ratio limit \(2\exp(1)\). \\
\nolinkurl{tendsto_lowerLambertW_add_one_sq_div_branchPoint} & Lower squared-ratio limit \(2\exp(1)\). \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_one_sq_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Principal squared-displacement equivalence. \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_add_one_sq_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Lower squared-displacement equivalence. \\
\nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_one_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Signed principal equivalence to \(+\mathcal S\). \\
\nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_add_one_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & Signed lower equivalence to \(-\mathcal S\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

This closes only the leading square-root (first Puiseux) equivalence.  No
\(O(\Delta)\) remainder, higher coefficient, or convergent/full Puiseux
expansion has been proved.  Named endpoint derivative, secant, and
square-root wrappers for the generic power--exponential phases or their
Fabius specializations also remain open, as does the generic
\(m\mathbin{\pm}\sqrt m\) threshold/sign/shape layer.
These two leaves are a current-tree tranche compiled on 30 August 2026:
the inventory is exactly eight public geometry theorems and one public
asymptotic definition plus eight public asymptotic theorems, excluding
private proof helpers.  They are recorded without inventing an aggregate
commit hash and are not attributed to the older title-page snapshot.

The reusable layer treats
\[
  S_{m,A,\beta}(\lambda)=A\lambda^m e^{-\beta\lambda},
  \qquad
  P_{m,A,\beta}
    =A\left(\frac m\beta e^{-1}\right)^m,
\]
under \(m\in\PositiveIntegers\), \(A>0\), and \(\beta>0\).
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCalculus.lean} proves
\nolinkurl{principalPowerExponentialPhase_continuousOn_Icc} on
\([0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\) and
\nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_continuousOn_Ioc} on
\((0,P_{m,A,\beta}]\).  Both proofs compose the raw endpoint theorems with
the globally continuous normalized argument
\nolinkurl{powerExponentialLambertArgument_continuous}.  For \(x\) in the
latter interval and
\(\lambda\ge0\),
\nolinkurl{powerExponentialSaddle_eq_iff_eq_principal_or_eq_lower} is the
exact equivalence
\[
 S_{m,A,\beta}(\lambda)=x
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \lambda=\lambda_0(x)\ \text{or}\ \lambda=\lambda_{-1}(x).
\]
The two values are unequal when \(0<x<P_{m,A,\beta}\), by
\nolinkurl{principalPowerExponentialPhase_ne_lowerPowerExponentialPhase}.
Both classification results live in
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertInverse.lean}.  The hypothesis
\(\lambda\ge0\) matters when \(m\) is even, because negative inputs can
produce further positive profile values.

The next import,
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertAsymptotics.lean}, defines the intrinsic
scale
\[
 \epsilon(x)=\frac{\beta}{m}\left(\frac{x}{A}\right)^{1/m}
\]
and proves exactly
\[
 \lambda_0(x)\sim\left(\frac{x}{A}\right)^{1/m},\qquad
 \lambda_{-1}(x)\longrightarrow+\infty,\qquad
 \lambda_{-1}(x)-M(x)\longrightarrow0
 \quad(x\downarrow0),
\]
where
\[
 M(x)=-\frac m\beta
 \bigl(\log\epsilon(x)-\log|\log\epsilon(x)|\bigr).
\]
The declarations are
\nolinkurl{powerExponentialLambertEpsilon},
\nolinkurl{principalPowerExponentialPhase_isEquivalent_rpow},
\nolinkurl{tendsto_lowerPowerExponentialPhase_nhdsGT_zero_atTop},
\nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhaseIntrinsicMain}, and
\nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhase_sub_intrinsicMain_tendsto_zero}.
This generic result is intentionally only the intrinsic two-term
\(\epsilon\)-expansion with \(o(1)\) remainder.  A cleaned
\(L=\log(A/x)\) formula, additional terms or a complete series, a
named generic-phase branch-point vertical-tangent/secant wrapper, and a
transported leading square-root law remain outside this tranche.  The raw
branch-point statements above do not by themselves create those scaled
phase wrappers.

The separate \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCurvature.lean} does supply
the generic regular-interior second derivative.  If \(\lambda\) denotes
either scaled branch and
\(0<x<P_{m,A,\beta}\), then, under the same hypotheses
\(m\in\PositiveIntegers\), \(A>0\), and \(\beta>0\),
\[
 \lambda''(x)=
 \frac{\lambda(x)\bigl(m-(m-\beta\lambda(x))^2\bigr)}
      {x^2\bigl(m-\beta\lambda(x)\bigr)^3}.
\]
The complete public surface of that module consists of the two
derivative-of-derivative witnesses
\nolinkurl{deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt}, together with their
value corollaries
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase} and
\nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase}.  The factorization
suggests candidate thresholds
\(\beta\lambda=m\mathbin{\pm}\sqrt m\), but generic sign, zero-set,
threshold, inflection, and strict-convexity/concavity packages have not been
formalized and are not claimed here.

Finally \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabius.lean} specializes
\((m,A,\beta)=(1,1,\log2)\).  It defines
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase}, identifies the other branch with
\nolinkurl{fabiusLambertPhase}, and exports
\nolinkurl{fabiusSaddle_eq_iff_eq_principal_or_eq_lower},
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_ne_fabiusLambertPhase},
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_continuousOn_Icc}, and
\nolinkurl{fabiusLambertPhase_continuousOn_Ioc}.
\nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_isEquivalent_id} gives the
specialized principal asymptotic \(\lambda_0(x)\sim x\); the generic lower
divergence and intrinsic expansion specialize to the existing lower Fabius
phase.

The curvature specialization is isolated in
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabiusCurvature.lean}.  Write
\[
 x_*:=\frac{2e^{-2}}{\log2},
 \qquad P:=\frac{e^{-1}}{\log2}.
\]
For the lower phase it proves \(x_*\in(0,P)\),
\(\lambda_{-1}(x_*)=2/\log2\), and, for \(0<x<P\),
\[
 \lambda_{-1}''(x)=
 \frac{(\log2)\lambda_{-1}(x)^2
       \bigl(2-(\log2)\lambda_{-1}(x)\bigr)}
      {x^2\bigl(1-(\log2)\lambda_{-1}(x)\bigr)^3}.
\]
Its second derivative is positive exactly when \(x<x_*\), negative exactly
when \(x_*<x\), and zero exactly when \(x=x_*\).  Thus \(x_*\) is the exact
formalized inflection input: the phase is strictly convex on
\((0,x_*]\) and strictly concave on \([x_*,P]\).

The principal specialization deliberately uses the raw-\(W_0\) curvature
API rather than restricting the generic positive-input formula.  Globally,
\[
 \lambda_0(x)=-\frac{W_0(-x\log2)}{\log2};
\]
for every \(x<P\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \lambda_0'(x)
   &=\frac{1}{e^{W_0(-x\log2)}(W_0(-x\log2)+1)},\\
 \lambda_0''(x)
   &=\frac{(\log2)e^{-2W_0(-x\log2)}(W_0(-x\log2)+2)}
           {(W_0(-x\log2)+1)^3}>0.
\end{aligned}
\]
In particular \(\lambda_0''(0)=2\log2\), and the phase is strictly convex
on the full endpoint-inclusive half-line \((-\infty,P]\), including all
negative inputs and zero.  The leaf's complete public surface is one
noncomputable definition and eighteen theorems:

\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.08\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.47\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
No. & Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
No. & Declaration & Formal content \\
\midrule
\endhead
1 & \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertInflectionInput} & Definition of \(x_*=2e^{-2}/\log2\). \\
2 & \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertInflectionInput_mem_Ioo} & \(x_*\in(0,P)\). \\
3 & \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertPhase_inflectionInput} & \(\lambda_{-1}(x_*)=2/\log2\). \\
4 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase} & Exact lower-phase second derivative on \((0,P)\). \\
5 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_pos_iff} & For \(0<x<P\), \(0<\lambda_{-1}''(x)\iff x<x_*\). \\
6 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_neg_iff} & For \(0<x<P\), \(\lambda_{-1}''(x)<0\iff x_*<x\). \\
7 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_eq_zero_iff} & For \(0<x<P\), \(\lambda_{-1}''(x)=0\iff x=x_*\). \\
8 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_inflectionInput} & The simplified equality \(\lambda_{-1}''(x_*)=0\). \\
9 & \nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_fabiusLambertPhase_left} & Strict convexity on \(\operatorname{Ioc}(0,x_*)\). \\
10 & \nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_fabiusLambertPhase_right} & Strict concavity on \(\operatorname{Icc}(x_*,P)\). \\
11 & \nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_eq_principalLambertW} & Global affine-\(W_0\) coordinate formula. \\
12 & \nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_continuousOn_Iic} & Continuity on \(\operatorname{Iic}(P)\). \\
13 & \nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_hasDerivAt} & First-derivative witness for every \(x<P\). \\
14 & \nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} & Exact first-derivative value for every \(x<P\). \\
15 & \nolinkurl{deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_hasDerivAt} & Derivative-of-derivative witness for every \(x<P\). \\
16 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} & Exact principal second-derivative value for every \(x<P\). \\
17 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_pos} & Strict positivity for every \(x<P\). \\
18 & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase_zero} & The simplified equality \(\lambda_0''(0)=2\log2\). \\
19 & \nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} & Strict convexity on \(\operatorname{Iic}(P)\). \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The import ladder reflects the proof structure:
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambert.lean} imports the raw lower and principal
branches, then
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCalculus.lean},
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertInverse.lean}, and
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertAsymptotics.lean} add continuity,
classification, and asymptotics in that order.
\nolinkurl{PrincipalLambertW.lean} itself imports
\nolinkurl{LowerLambertW.lean};
\nolinkurl{LambertBranchDichotomy.lean} imports the principal leaf; and
\nolinkurl{LambertWCurvature.lean} imports that pair layer.
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointGeometry.lean} then imports exactly the raw
curvature leaf, and
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointAsymptotics.lean} imports the geometry leaf
together with Mathlib's specific-asymptotics and L'H\^opital modules.  Thus
the raw dependency order is
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{lower}\longrightarrow\text{principal}\longrightarrow
 \text{branch dichotomy}\longrightarrow\text{curvature}\\
 {}\longrightarrow\text{branch-point geometry}\longrightarrow
 \text{branch-point asymptotics}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCurvature.lean} imports the generic
calculus layer and contains only the four branch-generic second-derivative
declarations above.  The non-curvature Fabius bridge imports the asymptotic
layer together with the older generalized-coordinate and Fabius saddle
modules, while
\nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabiusCurvature.lean} imports that bridge
and both curvature leaves.  The umbrella \nolinkurl{FabiusFunction.lean}
lists \nolinkurl{LambertWCurvature.lean},
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointGeometry.lean}, and
\nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointAsymptotics.lean} consecutively in dependency
order before the generic power--exponential leaves; it also exposes the two
scaled curvature modules.  These lemmas support the corrected
comparisons in both the k-fold project and the principal Fabius saddle
analysis.

\subsection{Decay comparisons}
\nolinkurl{FabiusFlatness.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusDecayComparison.lean} convert the log-squared law into two useful endpoint comparisons.  For every \(m>0\),
\[
  F(x)=o(x^m)
  \qquad(x\downarrow0),
\]
so the function is smaller than every power.  On the other hand, for every \(c>0\),
\[
  e^{-c/x}=o(F(x)),
\]
so it is much larger than an \(e^{-c/x}\) flat function.

Taking logarithms reduces these statements to comparing \((\log(1/x))^2\) with \(\log(1/x)\) and with \(1/x\).  The code packages positivity and eventual monotonicity so the exponential comparison can use standard filter lemmas.

\section{Recurring proof-engineering patterns}\label{ch:engineering}

\subsection{Strong induction as the default arithmetic engine}
Many sequences in the project depend on all smaller indices, not only on the predecessor.  The idiomatic proof begins with strong induction:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
refine Nat.strong_induction_on n ?_
intro n ih
-- ih : forall m < n, P m
\end{lstlisting}
A finite sum over \nolinkurl{Finset.range n} then supplies the inequality needed to invoke \nolinkurl{ih}.  It is often worth proving a helper lemma that converts membership in the range directly into the strict inequality expected by the hypothesis.

Strong induction also matches the highest-bit evaluator, whose recursive remainder is smaller but not necessarily one less.  Using the same well-founded order for definition and correctness proof keeps the program and theorem structurally aligned.

\subsection{Finite sums and index transport}
The repository moves frequently among:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \nolinkurl{Finset.range n};
  \item subtype indices \nolinkurl{Fin n};
  \item integer intervals;
  \item list positions; and
  \item antidiagonals describing coefficient convolution.
\end{itemize}
Rather than rewriting all indices inside a large expression, the code usually proves an equivalence between finite index types and invokes a reindexing theorem.  A clean bijection proof has four parts: the forward map, inverse map, membership preservation, and left/right inverse equalities.

For even/odd splits, a specialized theorem is especially valuable:
\[
  \sum_{j<2N}f(j)
  =\sum_{k<N}f(2k)+\sum_{k<N}f(2k+1).
\]
Once established, it drives Thue--Morse identities, convolution recurrences, and parity counts.

\subsection{Casts as explicit interfaces}
Exact theorems live in \(\NaturalNumbers\), \(\IntegerNumbers\), or \(\RationalNumbers\); analytic theorems live in \(\RealNumbers\) or \(\ComplexNumbers\).  Lean will insert canonical casts, but it will not silently prove that a finite sum commutes with the cast or that a natural exponent has the intended target type.

The code therefore proves and reuses lemmas of the forms
\begin{align*}
  \left(\sum_{k\in s}q_k:\RationalNumbers\right)\!\uparrow^{\RealNumbers}
    &=\sum_{k\in s}(q_k:\RealNumbers),\\
  (2^n:\RationalNumbers)\!\uparrow^{\RealNumbers}&=(2:\RealNumbers)^n,\\
  \left(\binom nk:\NaturalNumbers\right)\!\uparrow^{\RationalNumbers}
    &=\binom nk \text{ interpreted in }\RationalNumbers.
\end{align*}
In actual Lean syntax the upward arrows are ordinary coercions.  Tactics such as \nolinkurl{norm_cast}, \nolinkurl{exact_mod_cast}, and \nolinkurl{push_cast} are used after the expression is put in a form they recognize.

\begin{boxedremark}\textbf{Lean pattern.}\space
Do not ask \nolinkurl{norm_cast} to solve a goal containing unreduced divisions, nested sums, and conditionals.  First rewrite the exact recurrence, move casts through finite sums, simplify powers, and only then invoke cast normalization.  The repository's helper lemmas reflect this staged approach.
\end{boxedremark}

\subsection{Pointwise derivatives instead of raw \texttt{deriv}}
A theorem of type \nolinkurl{HasDerivAt f f' x} carries both differentiability and the derivative value.  It composes under addition, multiplication, affine maps, logarithms, and exponentials.  By contrast, an equality involving \nolinkurl{deriv f x} often creates a side goal proving \nolinkurl{DifferentiableAt f x} before it can be rewritten.

Accordingly, the calculus modules first prove \nolinkurl{HasDerivAt} identities and derive \nolinkurl{deriv} corollaries only when a source theorem is stated in that notation.  Iterated derivatives are handled through \nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv} or \nolinkurl{ContDiff} lemmas with explicit order parameters.

\subsection{Piecewise functions and boundary glue}
The bounded function, fixed-point transform, splines, and endpoint representatives are all piecewise.  The source follows a stable pattern:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item define each branch as a total function;
  \item prove branch continuity or differentiability;
  \item prove equality of branch values at the boundary;
  \item apply a piecewise glue theorem; and
  \item derive simplified branch lemmas for later rewriting.
\end{enumerate}
The simplified lemmas are often tagged for the simplifier only when their conditions are syntactically obvious.  Overly aggressive \nolinkurl{simp} tags on overlapping branches can create loops or choose an unintended normal form.

\subsection{Local finiteness before infinite-sum algebra}
For translate sums, the code first proves finite support at a point or on a neighborhood.  Only then does it reindex or differentiate a \nolinkurl{tsum}.  This is safer than invoking general unconditional summability theorems on a series whose convergence is really trivial by support.

A typical theorem states that the summand is zero outside a finite integer interval.  From this it derives summability, rewrites the \nolinkurl{tsum} as a \nolinkurl{Finset.sum}, performs the algebra, and converts back only if the public theorem uses \nolinkurl{tsum} notation.

\subsection{Integrability from support and bounds}
The most common integrability proof has three ingredients:
\begin{itemize}
  \item continuity or measurability of the integrand;
  \item compact support; and
  \item a uniform bound on that support.
\end{itemize}
The repository builds reusable support lemmas for \(F\), \(\RvachevUp\), their derivatives, and affine transforms.  Polynomial or exponential multipliers remain bounded on a compact support, so moment and transform integrability follows from a generic compact-support theorem.

This style avoids global estimates that are stronger than needed.  For instance, \(x^n\RvachevUp(x)\) is integrable not because \(x^n\) is globally bounded, but because the product vanishes outside \([-1,1]\).

\subsection{Interchanging sums, limits, derivatives, and integrals}
Every interchange in the project is supported by a named convergence theorem:
\begin{itemize}
  \item finite sums commute with everything by algebra;
  \item infinite sums commute with integrals under a summable integrable majorant;
  \item limits pass under integrals by dominated or bounded convergence;
  \item derivatives pass through series under local uniform convergence of derivative series; and
  \item contour limits use explicit uniform tail bounds.
\end{itemize}
The proof normally constructs the majorant first, proves its summability or integrability, and only then invokes the interchange theorem.  This order makes failures local: if an informal series is not uniformly controlled, the missing hypothesis appears before the main identity is attempted.

\subsection{Big-O, little-o, and eventual domains}
Asymptotic modules use filters rather than epsilon notation.  A theorem of the form
\[
  f(x)=O(g(x))\quad(x\to0^+)
\]
is represented through \nolinkurl{IsBigO} at a within-neighborhood filter.  The proof often begins with
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
filter_upwards [eventually_pos, eventually_small] with x hxpos hxsmall
\end{lstlisting}
and establishes a pointwise norm inequality.

Eventual positivity is particularly important for logarithms, divisions, and Lambert phases.  The source proves threshold lemmas once and then includes them in filter-upwards blocks.  Transitivity of Big-O and equivalence under asymptotically equal scales are used heavily to transfer results from the exact phase \(\lambda(x)\) to \(-\log x\).

\subsection{Polynomial identities}
Exact finite identities are proved at the polynomial level whenever possible.  The usual techniques are:
\begin{itemize}
  \item extensionality by coefficients;
  \item evaluation at an arbitrary point followed by \nolinkurl{ring};
  \item induction using polynomial addition and multiplication; and
  \item degree bounds plus a roots theorem.
\end{itemize}
For translation-invariant Thue--Morse sums, a degree bound and finite-difference theorem often give a cleaner proof than expanding every binomial coefficient.

\subsection{Choosing automation deliberately}
The development uses automation, but proofs are structured so each tactic sees an appropriate goal:
\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=2.5em]
  \item[\texttt{simp}] unfolds branch lemmas, finite-range membership, and standard algebraic identities.
  \item[\texttt{norm\_num}] closes concrete rational and natural computations, especially counterexamples and base cases.
  \item[\texttt{ring} / \texttt{ring\_nf}] handles polynomial identities after divisions and casts have been normalized.
  \item[\texttt{linarith} / \texttt{nlinarith}] combines real inequalities once transcendental terms have been abstracted as variables with known bounds.
  \item[\texttt{positivity}] proves positivity of products, powers, factorials, exponentials, and denominators.
  \item[\texttt{omega}] handles Presburger arithmetic for indices, ranges, and exponents.
  \item[\texttt{field\_simp}] clears rational or field denominators after nonzero hypotheses are established.
  \item[\texttt{aesop}] resolves local structural goals, but is not used as a substitute for choosing the main induction or analytic theorem.
\end{description}

\subsection{Public wrappers and internal strength}
Theorem-producing modules tend to prove results in a strong reusable form: unrestricted numerators, arbitrary candidate functions, general coefficient rings, or explicit error constants.  Paper-facing wrappers then add notation and specialize hypotheses.

When extending the library, search for the strongest internal theorem before reproving a source-shaped special case.  Names ending in \nolinkurl{_all}, \nolinkurl{_global}, \nolinkurl{_scalar}, \nolinkurl{_correct}, \nolinkurl{_transfer}, or \nolinkurl{_of_isFabius} often signal the reusable layer.

\subsection{No placeholders and explicit corrections}
The repository states that the development builds without \nolinkurl{sorry}.  More importantly, corrected claims are not represented by axioms that merely assume the missing fact.  When a source formula is false, the project either adds the necessary hypothesis, changes the object to the signed extension, weakens the error term, or proves a counterexample.

This makes the dependency graph epistemically meaningful.  A theorem about the Fabius function ultimately reduces to mathlib, Lean's kernel, explicit definitions, and proved lemmas---not to a paper citation embedded as an axiom.

\section{How to read and extend the code}\label{ch:extending}

\subsection{Five recommended reading paths}
Different goals call for different entry points.

\subsubsection{Path A: existence and uniqueness}
Read, in order:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{Basic.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{Differential.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{Existence.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
This path explains the abstract specification, generic derivative consequences, dyadic rigidity, and fixed-point implementation.

\subsubsection{Path B: exact evaluation}
Read:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{Arithmetic.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicCorrectness.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{GlobalDyadic.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
Keep an editor evaluation buffer open and inspect the types of table, Horner, and wrapper correctness theorems.

\subsubsection{Path C: the historical Rvachev theory}
Read:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FourierProduct.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FourierAnalytic.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{OriginalUniqueness.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{ProbabilityRepresentation.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{StepApproximationLimit.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
This path emphasizes compact support, transform refinement, convolutions, and pointwise approximants.

\subsubsection{Path D (post-snapshot): integer and fractional Volterra calculus}
The fractional modules in this path were included in the compiled full-facade
checkpoint \nolinkurl{22f801337}.
Read:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{NormalizedVolterra.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterra.lean} and
        \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraSemigroup.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraCalculus.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean} and
        \nolinkurl{FabiusAntiderivatives.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusFractionalVolterra.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
The first module is the reusable natural-order layer: arbitrary base points and oriented
endpoints, real normed-space-valued kernels and algebra, exact affine covariance
for every real scale, arbitrary base-point shifts, and the zero-local-tail Taylor
jet.  For continuous Banach-valued inputs it additionally identifies literal
iteration with the direct kernel and supplies the semigroup's strict FTC,
derivative cancellation, smoothness, and Taylor reconstruction.
\nolinkurl{FractionalVolterra.lean} and its semigroup companion give the
Gamma-normalized real-order operator and positive-order composition on ordered
intervals.  \nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraCalculus.lean} adds positive affine
covariance for arbitrary real order and, for \(\alpha>0\), the exact
integration-by-parts order-raising law with its left-endpoint boundary term.
The two Fabius-specific modules are used only after this generic work:
\nolinkurl{FabiusAntiderivatives.lean} gives the signed-global and bounded
natural-order ladders and finite monomial weights, while
\nolinkurl{FabiusFractionalVolterra.lean} gives the positive-real one-step
fractional shifts for the signed extension on \(x\geq0\), the bounded function
on \([0,1]\), and the bridge from \(\RvachevUp\) based at \(-1\) to \(F\).

\subsubsection{Path E: sharp asymptotics}
Read:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusLogScale.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusLogSquaredAsymptotic.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{NegativeLaplace.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{PeriodicCorrection.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{MellinBose.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertPhase.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{BromwichSaddle.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusSharpExactReduction.lean};
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}; and
  \item \nolinkurl{FabiusFullAsymptoticExpansion.lean}.
\end{enumerate}
Use the specialized central/tail and all-order modules as dependencies are encountered.

\subsection{Adding a new exact identity}
Suppose a new conjectured formula concerns \(F(a/2^n)\).  A productive workflow is:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item state and test the identity entirely in \(\RationalNumbers\) using \nolinkurl{fabiusDyadicValue};
  \item normalize representations and determine whether the bounded or global extension is intended;
  \item prove the finite arithmetic identity, preferably through existing closed-form or q-binomial lemmas;
  \item only then transport it to the analytic function using dyadic correctness; and
  \item add a paper-facing wrapper if the notation comes from an external source.
\end{enumerate}
This approach keeps exploratory computation cheap and avoids starting with real analysis when the content is combinatorial.

\subsection{Adding a new moment theorem}
For a new moment recurrence:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item derive the analytic recurrence in a generic \nolinkurl{IsFabius} context;
  \item define an exact rational sequence only if computation or arithmetic consequences justify it;
  \item prove equality by strong induction;
  \item design a division-free numerator recurrence before attempting parity or divisibility; and
  \item separate reduced-denominator conclusions into a later module.
\end{enumerate}
The existing normalized moment files are templates for common-denominator induction.

\subsection{Adding an asymptotic correction}
A proposed correction term should be attached to the exact saddle reduction rather than inferred from numerical fitting.  The robust route is:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item identify which cumulant or periodic Fourier coefficient contributes at the desired order;
  \item extend the formal coefficient recurrence;
  \item prove a uniform central remainder at that order;
  \item prove the tail is smaller than the target scale;
  \item update the normalized mass expansion; and
  \item transfer through logarithm and the exact phase.
\end{enumerate}
If the desired final formula is in elementary logarithms, derive it as a corollary of the exact-phase theorem using \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertAllOrderSmallArgument.lean}.

\subsection{Testing a suspected source error}
Formalization is most efficient when the claim is stress-tested early.
\begin{itemize}
  \item Check endpoint and smallest-index cases by exact evaluation.
  \item Verify domains of every differentiated functional equation.
  \item Compare bounded and signed-global versions under translation.
  \item For asymptotic claims, sample subsequences of fixed dyadic phase.
  \item For uniform errors, examine knots and support boundaries.
  \item For q-dependent finite rows, test nondyadic locations before claiming parameter independence.
\end{itemize}
A small counterexample theorem is often more informative than a long stalled proof attempt.

\subsection{Maintaining dependency hygiene}
Before importing the umbrella module into a new file, ask which interface is actually needed.  An exact lemma should normally depend on \nolinkurl{Arithmetic.lean} or a narrow q-binomial module, not on \nolinkurl{Existence.lean}.  A generic analytic lemma should depend on \nolinkurl{Basic.lean} and the relevant calculus modules, not on the canonical instance unless the theorem truly uses it.

A useful check is to run the import-graph tooling included through mathlib's dependencies.  Unexpected paths from asymptotic modules into paper aggregators or from arithmetic modules into analysis are signs that a helper theorem belongs in a lower layer.

\subsection{Naming and theorem granularity}
The repository's long module names encode the proof stage: \nolinkurl{Raw}, \nolinkurl{Scalar}, \nolinkurl{ExactReduction}, \nolinkurl{Transfer}, \nolinkurl{Central}, \nolinkurl{Tail}, \nolinkurl{AllOrders}, and so on.  New declarations should preserve this vocabulary where possible.

A theorem should expose one conceptual transition.  Good boundaries include:
\begin{itemize}
  \item closed formula equals Horner program;
  \item analytic recurrence equals exact recurrence;
  \item central integral equals Gaussian main term plus error;
  \item exact phase equals truncated elementary expansion plus error; and
  \item source statement follows from the stronger internal theorem.
\end{itemize}
Large proofs become navigable when these transitions have names.

\subsection{A minimal downstream example}
A downstream file that only needs exact evaluation and the analytic correctness theorem can begin as follows:
\begin{lstlisting}[style=pseudo]
import FabiusFunction.PaperStatements

open Fabius

example (n : Nat) (a : Int) :
    (fabiusDyadicValue n a : Real) =
      fabiusReal fabius ((a : Real) / (2 : Real) ^ n) := by
  exact fabiusDyadicValue_cast fabius fabius_spec n a
\end{lstlisting}
This statement accepts every signed numerator, so no reduced-fraction or
unit-interval side condition remains.  Importing \nolinkurl{FabiusFunction.lean}
would also work, but would hide which layer supplies the public wrapper.

\subsection{What to trust when prose and code differ}
The kernel-checked theorem type is the final authority.  Module headers and README tables explain intent and source correspondence, while the proof term certifies the exact statement.  When a paper formula differs from a Lean wrapper, inspect:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item whether the paper silently assumes an index is positive;
  \item whether a removable singularity has been totalized;
  \item whether a global extension replaces a bounded function;
  \item whether endpoint representatives differ only almost everywhere; and
  \item whether an asymptotic periodic term has been omitted.
\end{enumerate}
The repository usually records the reason in the aggregator header or a nearby correction theorem.

\section{Concluding perspective}
The ProveIt development is best understood not as a very long proof of a single theorem, but as a small mathematical library centered on one function.  It has several independent foundations:
\begin{itemize}
  \item an exact arithmetic language of moments, binary signs, products, and dyadic evaluators;
  \item an abstract analytic interface capturing the Fabius differential and symmetry laws;
  \item a Banach fixed-point construction of the bounded solution;
  \item a compact-support/Fourier theory of the Rvachev twin; and
  \item a transform-and-saddle theory for the flat endpoint.
\end{itemize}
The strongest parts of the architecture are the bridges.  The dyadic bridge identifies executable rationals with analytic values.  The moment bridge identifies finite recurrences with integrals.  The Fourier bridge identifies a refinement equation with an infinite product.  The saddle bridge identifies that product with a corrected sharp asymptotic.  The Legendre bridge turns exact moments into optimal polynomial approximants.

Formalization also changes the mathematical product.  It forces endpoint conventions, domains of delay equations, transform normalizations, and bounded-versus-global distinctions to be stated.  It exposes false residual estimates and missing terms, while often supplying a corrected theorem stronger than the source statement.  The result is not merely a verification of known facts: it is a coherent, reusable account of how the facts fit together.

For a reader entering the source, the single most useful habit is to ask which side of the project a declaration belongs to: exact arithmetic, generic analysis, canonical construction, or a bridge.  Once that question is answered, the long file list resolves into a small number of repeated proof patterns and a remarkably systematic dependency graph.

\appendix

\section{Detailed dependency map}\label{app:dependency}

\subsection{Core spine}
The shortest conceptual spine from definitions to the canonical function is:
\[
\begin{gathered}
\boxed{\text{\texttt{Arithmetic.lean}}}
\longrightarrow
\boxed{\text{\texttt{Basic.lean}}}
\longrightarrow
\boxed{\text{\texttt{Differential.lean}}}\\[1mm]
\longrightarrow
\boxed{\text{\texttt{DyadicAnalytic.lean}}}
\longrightarrow
\boxed{\text{\texttt{Existence.lean}}}\\[1mm]
\longrightarrow
\boxed{\text{\texttt{PaperStatements.lean}}}.
\end{gathered}
\]
This display omits two important side inputs.  \nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean} supplies the exact finite polynomial matched by \nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean}, while \nolinkurl{DyadicCorrectness.lean} supplies evaluator correctness and representation invariance.  \nolinkurl{Existence.lean} proves unique existence; \nolinkurl{PaperStatements.lean} makes the canonical choice and publishes \nolinkurl{fabius_spec}.  The resulting canonical function is then consumed by moment, Fourier, probability, global-extension, and asymptotic modules.

\subsection{Arithmetic spine}
The main arithmetic chain is:
\[
\begin{gathered}
\text{\texttt{Arithmetic.lean}}
\longrightarrow
\left\{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{\texttt{NormalizedMoments.lean}}\\
\text{\texttt{NormalizedEvenMoments.lean}}\\
\text{\texttt{Parity.lean}}
\end{array}
\right.\\[2mm]
\longrightarrow
\left\{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{\texttt{HalfMomentDenominator.lean}}\\
\text{\texttt{TwoAdic.lean}}\\
\text{\texttt{DenominatorBound.lean}}
\end{array}
\right.
\longrightarrow
\text{\texttt{PaperStatements.lean}}.
\end{gathered}
\]
\nolinkurl{AnalyticMoments.lean} and \nolinkurl{ExactInversePower.lean} connect this chain to the canonical function before the source-facing wrappers are assembled.

\subsection{Original-paper spine}
The original Rvachev paper is represented by two parallel paths:
\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{differential/refinement law}
\to\text{Fourier recurrence}
\to\text{sinc product}
\to\text{uniqueness},\\[1mm]
\text{finite elementary laws}
\to\text{convolutions}
\to\text{weak convergence}
\to\text{step approximants}.
\end{array}
\]
Their principal modules are \nolinkurl{OriginalUniqueness.lean}, \nolinkurl{FourierProduct.lean}, \nolinkurl{ProbabilityRepresentation.lean}, \nolinkurl{WeakConvergence.lean}, and \nolinkurl{StepApproximationLimit.lean}.  \nolinkurl{OriginalPaperSupplement.lean} adds the remaining numbered theorems and prose claims.

\subsection{Activation spine}
The activation development consists of one local Taylor branch and a small
square-summable fork-and-join graph:
\[
\begin{gathered}
\text{\texttt{HyperbolicActivation}}
\longrightarrow
\text{\texttt{ActivationTaylor}},\\[2mm]
\text{\texttt{HyperbolicActivation}}
\longrightarrow
\left\{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{\texttt{ActivationSeries}}\\
\text{\texttt{ActivationAsymptotics}}
\end{array}
\right.\\[2mm]
\left\{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{\texttt{ActivationSeries}}\\
\text{\texttt{ActivationAsymptotics}}
\end{array}
\right.
\longrightarrow
\text{\texttt{ActivationSeriesAsymptotics}},\\[2mm]
\text{\texttt{ActivationSeries}}
\longrightarrow
\text{\texttt{GeometricActivationDimension}},\\[2mm]
\left\{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{\texttt{ActivationSeriesAsymptotics}}\\
\text{\texttt{GeometricActivationDimension}}
\end{array}
\right.
\longrightarrow
\text{\texttt{GeometricActivationAsymptotics}}.
\end{gathered}
\]
The Taylor branch is a local analytic development and terminates on its own.
Of the remaining two branches, the first contains global comparison and
summability and the second contains the one-coordinate punctured limit.
Tannery joins those two interfaces only after both are available.  Geometric
algebra stays in the structural module, so the final asymptotic specialization
contains almost no repeated series computation.

\subsection{Asymptotic spine}
The sharp endpoint proof has the following high-level flow:
\[
\begin{gathered}
\text{\texttt{AnalyticMoments.lean}}
\to\text{\texttt{NegativeLaplace.lean}}
\to\left\{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{\texttt{PeriodicCorrection.lean}}\\
\text{\texttt{MellinBose.lean}}
\end{array}
\right.\\
\to\text{\texttt{BromwichSaddle.lean}}
\to\text{\texttt{FabiusLambertPhase.lean}}\\
\to\left\{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{central modules}\\
\text{tail modules}
\end{array}
\right.
\to\text{\texttt{FabiusSharpExactReduction.lean}}\\
\to\text{\texttt{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean}}
\to\text{\texttt{FabiusFullAsymptoticExpansion.lean}}.
\end{gathered}
\]
The actual source graph is finer because the all-order proof separates formal coefficient algebra, small-argument Lambert expansion, Gaussian contraction, central uniformity, tail flatness, and transfer lemmas.

\subsection{How to use the map}
The declaration named after a source theorem is normally the best citation target in downstream formal work.  The principal proof location is the best learning target.  When the two differ, the wrapper is preserving source notation while the internal file proves a stronger or more reusable theorem.

Exact inverse modulus & \nolinkurl{fabiusIntervalMass}, \nolinkurl{strictMonoOn_fabiusIntervalMass_firstHalf}, \nolinkurl{fabiusIntervalMass_eq_fabiusReal_iff}, \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_sub_eq_sub_iff_of_mem_Icc}, \nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_le}, \nolinkurl{abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq_iff_of_mem_Icc}, \nolinkurl{sSup_abs_fabiusInv_sub_eq}, \nolinkurl{forall_abs_fabiusInv_sub_lt_iff} & \nolinkurl{InverseModulus.lean}; strict and equality refinements are unit-interval qualified where clamping requires it \\

Logarithmic reciprocal inverse modulus & \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicOrder}, \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicFactorialDenominator}, \nolinkurl{inverseFabiusLogarithmicDeltaDenominator}, \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous_logarithmic}, \nolinkurl{fabiusInv_effectivelyUniformContinuous_logarithmicDelta} & \nolinkurl{FabiusInverseLogarithmicModulus.lean}; least binary-length selector and two primitive-recursive witnesses, not an inverse realizer \\
Actual quarter inverse jet & \nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv_centeredFabiusInv_quarter_eq_quadraticInverse}, \nolinkurl{iteratedDeriv_fabiusInv_five_seventy_two_succ} & \nolinkurl{FabiusInverseQuarterJet.lean}; equality of jets, not local analytic equality \\
Centered Thue--Morse mixed differences & \nolinkurl{symmetricMixedDifference}, \nolinkurl{symmetricMixedDifference_polynomial_eq_coeff_card}, \nolinkurl{symmetricDyadicMixedDifference_eq_sum_thueMorseSign_smul} & \nolinkurl{ThueMorseSymmetricDifference.lean}; finite algebraic identities, with no analytic limit claim \\
Uniform spline \(S_p\) & \nolinkurl{fabiusUniformSpline} & \nolinkurl{FabiusUniformSpline.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusComputability.lean} \\
Logarithmic profile & \nolinkurl{fabiusLogProfile} & \nolinkurl{FabiusLogScale.lean} \\
Real Lambert-branch curvature & \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalLambertW}, \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerLambertW}, the lower sign iff family, \nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_principalLambertW}, \nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_lowerLambertW_left}, \nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_lowerLambertW_right} & \nolinkurl{LambertWCurvature.lean}; exact regular-domain second derivatives, with shape theorems including the branch point and inflection while the lower domain stays open at zero \\
Real Lambert branch-point vertical geometry & \nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_principalLambertW_branchPoint_atTop}, \nolinkurl{tendsto_deriv_lowerLambertW_branchPoint_atBot}, the two \nolinkurl{secantSlope_branchPoint} limits, and the four \nolinkurl{not_differentiable} endpoint theorems & \nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointGeometry.lean}; exactly eight theorems giving signed right-hand derivative/secant blow-up, failure of finite right derivatives, and failure of full differentiability \\
Real Lambert leading branch-point asymptotics & \nolinkurl{lambertWBranchPointScale}, the two squared-ratio limits, the two squared equivalences, and \nolinkurl{principalLambertW_add_one_isEquivalent_branchPoint}, \nolinkurl{lowerLambertW_add_one_isEquivalent_branchPoint} & \nolinkurl{LambertWBranchPointAsymptotics.lean}; one definition and eight theorems, with signed \(+\sqrt{2e\Delta}\) and \(-\sqrt{2e\Delta}\) laws only \\
Generic scaled power--exponential phases & \nolinkurl{principalPowerExponentialPhase}, \nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhase}, \nolinkurl{powerExponentialLambertEpsilon}, \nolinkurl{lowerPowerExponentialPhaseIntrinsicMain}, \nolinkurl{deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt}, \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_principalPowerExponentialPhase}, \nolinkurl{deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase_hasDerivAt}, \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_lowerPowerExponentialPhase} & \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambert.lean}, \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCalculus.lean}, \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertInverse.lean}, \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertAsymptotics.lean}, and \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertCurvature.lean}; generic curvature sign/threshold/shape packages remain open \\
Classical principal/lower Lambert reference coordinates & \nolinkurl{fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase}, \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertPhase} & \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabius.lean} and \nolinkurl{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean} \\
Fabius Lambert-phase curvature & \nolinkurl{fabiusLambertInflectionInput}, \nolinkurl{deriv_deriv_fabiusLambertPhase_eq_zero_iff}, \nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_fabiusLambertPhase_left}, \nolinkurl{strictConcaveOn_fabiusLambertPhase_right}, \nolinkurl{strictConvexOn_fabiusPrincipalLambertPhase} & \nolinkurl{PowerExponentialLambertFabiusCurvature.lean}; exact lower inflection and principal global-\nolinkurl{Iic} convexity \\

\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\begin{boxedremark}
The table maps notation to declarations; it does not assert that every
informal formula in either source paper is a proved theorem.  In particular,
\nolinkurl{reshetnikovQuestion} and \nolinkurl{conjecture_sixteen} record open
assertions, the lower-Lambert coordinate is not claimed to solve the full
residual phase's exact critical equation, the periodic correction is not
constant, finite complex-\(q\) approximants need not be translation
independent, and the literal k-fold polygonal limit is formally refuted.
\end{boxedremark}

\section{Annotated module guide}\label{app:modules}

The following guide groups the principal files by role.  The development contains many fine-grained helper modules; the descriptions emphasize the transition each module contributes rather than every local lemma.

\begin{longtable}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.31\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.63\textwidth}}
\toprule
Module & Role in the development \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Module & Role in the development \\
\midrule
\endhead
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Core interfaces and construction}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{Arithmetic.lean} & Exact natural/rational sequences, binary weight, Thue--Morse signs, product normalizations, inverse-power values, and executable dyadic evaluators. \\
\nolinkurl{Basic.lean} & The unit-interval codomain, bounded-function type, abstract IsFabius specification, up-function, signed extension names, transforms, moments, and generating functions. \\
\nolinkurl{Differential.lean} & Derivative identities for any IsFabius candidate, including iterated scale formulas and reflected branches. \\
\nolinkurl{Existence.lean} & Banach fixed-point construction of \nolinkurl{Existence.boundedCandidate}, calculus bootstrap, unique existence, and generic equality of all \nolinkurl{IsFabius} candidates.  The canonical choice itself is made in \nolinkurl{PaperStatements.lean}. \\
\nolinkurl{ScaleTranslation.lean} & Reusable chain-rule formulas for affine rescaling and translation of Fabius and up-functions. \\
\nolinkurl{GlobalExtension.lean} & Local finiteness, smoothness, unit-interval agreement, and translation laws of the signed global extension. \\
\nolinkurl{NormalizedVolterra.lean} & Base-point and endpoint-order independent repeated integration.  Over arbitrary real normed targets it provides direct normalized kernels, division-free affine covariance for every scale (and an inverse form at nonzero scale), arbitrary base-point shifts into a local tail plus finite jet, the zero-local-tail specialization, and finite Hasse-polynomial and monomial commutators.  Completeness is confined to the continuous literal-iterator/strict-FTC bridge, derivative cancellation and smoothness, and Taylor reconstruction. \\
\nolinkurl{FractionalVolterra.lean} & Total Gamma-normalized real-order Volterra operator, unconditional endpoint/locality/scalar laws, the order-one and positive-natural-order bridges, and positive-order kernel integrability and input additivity on ordered intervals. \\
\nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraSemigroup.lean} & Shifted beta-kernel integrability and evaluation, and addition of two positive real orders for continuous Banach-valued inputs on ordered compact intervals. \\
\nolinkurl{FractionalVolterraCalculus.lean} & Positive-scale affine covariance at arbitrary real order on ordered endpoints, its inverse-smul form, and the positive-order integration-by-parts law raising the order of a right interior derivative, including the exact boundary-free specialization. \\
\nolinkurl{GlobalDyadic.lean} & Exact dyadic identities for the signed extension with unrestricted and nonreduced representatives. \\
\addlinespace[0.45em]
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Global shape, inversion, and computability}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{ScalingRecurrence.lean} & Mathlib-only chain rules for arbitrary positive dilations, in vector and scalar forms, with caller-normalized outgoing weight; inert-parameter variants and dyadic wrappers used by negative-Laplace derivatives. \\
\nolinkurl{Monotonicity.lean} & Strict increase of \(F\) on \([0,1]\), endpoint range, and order consequences. \\
\nolinkurl{Regularity.lean} & Optimal global Lipschitz constants for \(F\) and \(\RvachevUp\). \\
\nolinkurl{Convexity.lean} & Exact second-derivative formula and sign/zero loci, convexity before the midpoint, and concavity afterward. \\
\nolinkurl{GlobalBounds.lean} & Sharp value and iterated-derivative bounds for the signed global extension and \(\RvachevUp\), including attainment and derivative-support control. \\
\nolinkurl{BoundedDerivatives.lean} & Exact global sup-norm bounds for every derivative, including attainment. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusAntiderivatives.lean} & Complete natural-order primitive ladder for the signed extension at every real endpoint, its bounded specialization on \(x\le1\), and finite natural-monomial formulas for both. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusFractionalVolterra.lean} & Positive-real one-step fractional order shift for the signed extension on \(x\ge0\), its bounded specialization on \([0,1]\), and the affine bridge from \(\RvachevUp\) based at \(-1\) to the bounded Fabius function for \(x\ge-1\). \\
\nolinkurl{EffectiveFlatness.lean} & Explicit power bounds at the flat endpoint. \\
\nolinkurl{SharpFlatness.lean} & Factorial-strengthened endpoint power bounds for \(F\) and \(\RvachevUp\). \\
\nolinkurl{NowhereAnalytic.lean} & Exact real-analytic loci: off the closed transition supports and nowhere on them. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusInverse.lean} & Totalized inverse, order isomorphism on the unit interval, exact positive reciprocal derivative, exact global smoothness locus (continuity everywhere, but differentiability and every positive finite or \(C^\infty\) order exactly off the clamping endpoints), reciprocal-cubic second derivative, midpoint jet and interior curvature loci, strict closed-half curvature, and endpoint quotient/derivative blow-up. \\
\nolinkurl{InverseModulus.lean} & Exhaustive one-definition/thirty-one-theorem structural inverse-modulus layer: global weak and maximal strict fixed-length increment shape, exact endpoint minimizers, constrained-superadditivity equality, unit-interval inverse-gap rigidity, global sharp modulus bounds and attained suprema, and effective injectivity.  The inverse equality loci are deliberately not global because of the clamped tails. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusInverseEffectiveContinuity.lean} & Exhaustive two-definition/twelve-theorem effective inverse-continuity layer: primitive-recursive factorial and report denominators, recurrence and endpoint-mass lower bounds, strict and closed global inverse moduli, and \nolinkurl{EffectivelyUniformContinuous}; the inverse remains noncomputable and no sequential realizer or combined computable-function theorem is supplied. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusInverseLogarithmicModulus.lean} & Three definitions and fifteen theorems: the primitive-recursive least binary-length order, its exact \nolinkurl{Nat.log2} formula and minimality, factorial and Delta denominators at that order, strict/closed-input reciprocal moduli, and an effective-uniform-continuity theorem for either witness; no exact endpoint-mass ceiling, tolerant-bisection realizer, sequential computability, combined computable-real-function theorem, or input-bit asymptotic. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusInverseQuarterJet.lean} & Full smooth derivative jet of the centered inverse at \(5/72\), identified with the factorial-scaled coefficients of the quadratic compositional inverse; this is not a local analytic identity. \\
\nolinkurl{ImplicitPowerSeries.lean} & Nonmonic complete-adic lifting and residue-class uniqueness, with arbitrary-base and zero-constant formal implicit-root specializations over power-series rings; it does not construct or analytically realize a Fabius inverse germ. \\
\nolinkurl{QuarterCatalanGerm.lean} & The explicit scaled quarter Catalan formal germ, its functional equation and uniqueness, and the forward/reverse rescaling bridge from \nolinkurl{dyadicGermTwo} to \nolinkurl{QuadraticInverse.inverse}. \\
\nolinkurl{InverseBranch.lean} & Analytic inverse theorem, analytic-density preservation for continuous right-inverse branches, and the complement-interior analyticity condition and conditional Lambert-branch criterion for \nolinkurl{IsElementaryOrInverse}. \\
\nolinkurl{AlgebraicBranch.lean} & Algebraic-function branch interface used in non-elementarity arguments. \\
\nolinkurl{ElementaryFunction.lean} & Formal expression language and semantics for elementary real functions. \\
\nolinkurl{NotElementary.lean} & Non-elementarity theorem for the bounded Fabius function on its transition interval. \\
\nolinkurl{InverseNotElementary.lean} & Interior noncriticality derived from the positive derivative API in \nolinkurl{FabiusInverse.lean}, the exact analytic locus of \nolinkurl{fabiusInv}, and local analytic-density/non-elementarity obstructions. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusComputability.lean} & Global uniform spline error \(\leq2^{-p}\), moving-input stability and diagonal convergence, an effective uniform-continuity modulus, and the computable-real-function bridge. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusComputableSpline.lean} & Primitive-recursive bounded and signed-global dyadic spline evaluators, each with an explicit error certificate, and their computable-real interfaces. \\
\addlinespace[0.45em]
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Dyadic formulas and verified computation}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{DyadicClosedForm.lean} & Closed finite Taylor formulas, highest-bit decompositions, and equivalence among exact dyadic expressions. \\
\nolinkurl{DyadicCorrectness.lean} & Termination-facing invariants, table-prefix stability, Horner correctness, refinement invariance, and evaluator representation independence. \\
\nolinkurl{DyadicAnalytic.lean} & Finite Taylor recurrence for an abstract IsFabius function and proof that analytic dyadic values equal the exact evaluator. \\
\nolinkurl{ExactInversePower.lean} & Links inverse-power Fabius values with exact moments and table entries. \\
\nolinkurl{TaylorReduction.lean} & Finite Taylor-reduction identities used to move between dyadic cells. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDyadicLogBounds.lean} & Explicit logarithmic bounds for inverse-dyadic values, used in endpoint asymptotics. \\
\nolinkurl{DyadicSharpConditional.lean} & Conditional sharp statements at dyadic points under specified recurrence estimates. \\
\nolinkurl{DyadicSharpDecomposition.lean} & Exact decomposition of dyadic logarithms into main and correction pieces. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDyadicSharpCumulant.lean} & Cumulant organization specialized to inverse-dyadic asymptotics. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDyadicSharpAsymptotic.lean} & Sharp asymptotic statements along the inverse-power sequence. \\
\addlinespace[0.45em]
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Moments, arithmetic, and denominators}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{AnalyticMoments.lean} & Compact-support integration, total mass, moment and half-moment recurrences, identification with exact rational sequences, and the named even Fourier modes \nolinkurl{rvachevFourierMomentTerm} with their zero term, scaling law, and genuine \nolinkurl{HasSum} to the Rvachev Fourier transform. \\
\nolinkurl{MomentPowerSeries.lean} & Exact \nolinkurl{PowerSeries} algebra over \(\RationalNumbers\), including \(M(4X)=M(X)S(X)\); analytic evaluation is deferred to later modules. \\
\nolinkurl{NormalizedMoments.lean} & Division-free normalization theorem for half moments using factorial and Mersenne products. \\
\nolinkurl{NormalizedEvenMoments.lean} & Natural numerator and common-denominator formula for even centered moments. \\
\nolinkurl{Parity.lean} & Lucas-theorem parity analysis and oddness of natural moment numerators. \\
\nolinkurl{TwoAdic.lean} & Reduced numerator/denominator parity and exact two-adic valuation consequences. \\
\nolinkurl{PrimePowerBinomialValuation.lean} & Three exhaustive theorems for prime-power Pascal-row valuations: the additive and subtraction forms for \(0<j\le p^m\), including \(m=0\) and \(j=p^m\), and the strict-interior dyadic specialization; no endpoint-flat or histogram result. \\
\nolinkurl{DenominatorBound.lean} & Common-denominator divisibility and explicit denominator bounds for source-facing arithmetic results. \\
\nolinkurl{HalfMomentDenominator.lean} & Denominator control specialized to half moments and inverse-power values. \\
\nolinkurl{BernoulliRecurrences.lean} & Translation of moment recurrences into Bernoulli-number identities used by the arithmetic paper. \\
\nolinkurl{LaplaceMoments.lean} & Moment identities in the Laplace-transform normalization. \\
\nolinkurl{LaplaceMomentBounds.lean} & Uniform factorial/exponential bounds needed for transform series and differentiation. \\
\nolinkurl{ProbabilityLaplaceMoments.lean} & Moment and Laplace identities connecting the weighted-sum probability model with the analytic generating function. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusRecurrenceSequence.lean} & Auxiliary exact recurrence sequence used in asymptotic and dyadic estimates. \\
\addlinespace[0.45em]
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Fourier, probability, and the first paper}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{FourierProduct.lean} & Entire sinc, pointwise multipliability, finite-product recurrence, and equality of the product with the analytic Fourier transform. \\
\nolinkurl{FourierAnalytic.lean} & Entire Fourier transform of \(\RvachevUp\), Fourier inversion consequences, and its imaginary-axis relation to the half-moment generating function. \\
\nolinkurl{OriginalCharacterization.lean} & Rvachev's original compact-support predicate, derived scale positivity, the forced scale and normalization, and the general fold from bounded Fabius solutions. \\
\nolinkurl{OriginalUniqueness.lean} & Fourier-refinement uniqueness, the exact kernel of the fold, and the equivalences with fixed candidates or unique bounded Fabius witnesses. \\
\nolinkurl{WeightedUniformSeries.lean} & Absolutely summable uniform-coordinate series, their canonical pushforward laws, pointwise and law-level head--tail splitting, reflection, and carried-interval results. \\
\nolinkurl{WeightedUniformDistribution.lean} & Compatibility and naturality API for weighted laws, including scalar transport, affine recursion, sharp absolute continuity from any nonzero real coefficient, and null singletons. \\
\nolinkurl{WeightedUniformSupport.lean} & Support equal to the continuous series range, exact signed-real min/max support intervals, and nonnegative unit-mass specializations. \\
\nolinkurl{ContinuousCDF.lean} & Generic continuity of atomless probability CDFs, reflection symmetry from measure invariance, and the theorem identifying any everywhere pointwise CDF derivative as the exact Lebesgue density without prior regularity or absolute continuity assumptions. \\
\nolinkurl{AffineIndependentCopy.lean} & The affine independent-copy convolution operator, its probability and product-map forms, characteristic-function factorization and finite fixed-point iteration, and contractive uniqueness.  The digit space is arbitrary measurable, the target is second-countable Borel real inner-product, and completeness is confined to the three uniqueness declarations; \(\lvert q\rvert<1\) includes zero and negative scales without support, density, or moment hypotheses. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricUniformLaw.lean} & Named weights \((1-q)q^n\), their series and probability law, affine self-similarity, reflection, absolute continuity, null singletons, and exact range/support statements. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricUniformMultisection.lean} & Fixed normalized even/odd split of the half-ratio coordinate series into two independent quarter-ratio copies, with pointwise, product-map, and convolution forms. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricUniformUniqueness.lean} & Specializes the generic product-map theorem to prove \nolinkurl{eq_geometricUniformDistribution_of_selfSimilar}: every probability solution of the geometric affine head--tail equation is \(\mu_q\) when \(\lvert q\rvert<1\), including \(q=0\) and negative \(q\), with no support, density, or moment assumption. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricSincFactorization.lean} & Closed characteristic function of the scaled uniform digit, exact closed-factor and phase-collapsed finite residual formulas for \(\lvert q\rvert<1\), pointwise convergence of the phase-bearing sinc prefixes, and the two dyadic weighted-sum wrappers. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricReciprocalGamma.lean} & Locally uniform convergence, pointwise multipliability and \nolinkurl{HasProd}, and entireness of the geometric sinc product, together with its reciprocal-Gamma reflection factorization. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricSincCharacteristicFunction.lean} & The four-theorem, zero-definition surface: two exact phase-bearing bridges from the geometric-uniform characteristic function to the rescaled geometric sinc product and paired reciprocal-Gamma factors, plus locally uniform convergence of the full phase-bearing prefixes on the real frequency line and uniform convergence on every compact real frequency set, for every \(\lvert q\rvert<1\), including zero and negative ratios. \\
\nolinkurl{RvachevPochhammerFactorization.lean} & One definition and nine theorems: the total complex infinite Pochhammer product and its strict-contraction convergence API, absolute summability and paired scale--zero product, the global \(S_q(z)=\prod_{k\ge0}(z^2/(k+1)^2;q^2)_\infty\) factorization for complex \(\lVert q\rVert<1\), and its two dyadic Rvachev/Fabius specializations. \\
\nolinkurl{QPochhammerEntire.lean} & No definitions and five theorems: for each fixed complex \(\lVert q\rVert<1\), locally uniform convergence in \(a\), entireness of \(a\mapsto(a;q)_\infty\), the total factor-zero criterion, the reciprocal-power lattice when \(q\ne0\), and analytic order one at every zero.  The \(q=0\)-safe theorem remains division-free; no outer-product normal convergence, joint holomorphy, or centered/MGF wrapper is asserted. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricUniformCDF.lean} & The named \(F_q\) and explicit \(f_q\), conditioning and interval-integral equations, pointwise derivative and exact positive-ratio \nolinkurl{withDensity} law, continuity, reflection, exterior zeros, ordinary/topological support containments, compact support, and \(C^\infty\) regularity under their exact parameter hypotheses.  It also proves that the selected total formula is zero at \(q=0\), nonpositive for \(q<0\), yields the zero clamped measure for \(q\le0\), and cannot represent \(\mu_q\) on the nonpositive contraction range. \\
\nolinkurl{HyperbolicActivation.lean} & Four definitions and 58 theorems forming the totalized real hyperbolic-sinc, \nolinkurl{tanhDiv}, activation-odds, and activation-probability dictionary, with continuity, parity, positivity, exact odds bridges, global elementary bounds, and the canonical identification \nolinkurl{tanhDiv_eq_dslope}. \\
\nolinkurl{ActivationTaylor.lean} & Three theorems: the exact degree-nine Taylor polynomial of \(\tanh\) with \(O(\lVert x\rVert^{11})\) remainder, the induced degree-eight activation-probability polynomial with \(O(\lVert x\rVert^{10})\) remainder, and its equivalent \(O(x^{10})\) form. \\
\nolinkurl{ActivationSeries.lean} & Three theorems: the rescaled pointwise quadratic budget, genuine summability for arbitrary-index square-summable signed real weights, and the corresponding global \nolinkurl{tsum} budget. \\
\nolinkurl{ActivationAsymptotics.lean} & Two punctured-neighborhood theorems: the sharp coefficient \(1/3\) proved by one l'Hopital step, and its all-real scaled form \(a^2/3\), including \(a=0\). \\
\nolinkurl{ActivationSeriesAsymptotics.lean} & One arbitrary-index Tannery theorem identifying the exact quadratic coefficient of every square-summable activation series; the global majorant equals the limiting \(\ell^2\) coefficient, so no Taylor remainder is used. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricActivationDimension.lean} & Two definitions and 30 theorems: geometric/dyadic activation sums, genuine summability, global budgets, positivity and parity, refinement and finite-prefix/tail certificates, and the exact \nolinkurl{HasSum} of the squared normalized geometric weights. \\
\nolinkurl{GeometricActivationAsymptotics.lean} & Two thin specializations of the generic Tannery theorem, giving coefficient \((1-q)/(3(1+q))\) for \(\lvert q\rvert<1\) and the dyadic value \(1/9\). \\
\nolinkurl{ProbabilityRepresentation.lean} & Product measure on \([0,1]^{\NaturalNumbers}\), weighted coordinate sum, self-similarity, and exact CDF/\(\RvachevUp\) identifications.  Its dyadic CDF smoothing, continuity, exterior-value, and reflection proofs are half-base specializations of \nolinkurl{GeometricUniformCDF.lean}; it also identifies \(F_{1/2}\) with every bounded Fabius function and \(f_{1/2}(x)\) with \(2\RvachevUp(2x-1)\). \\
\nolinkurl{WeakConvergence.lean} & Characteristic-function convergence, Lévy continuity, bounded-continuous test convergence, positivity on every open set meeting \((-1,1)\), and exact limiting-measure support \([-1,1]\). \\
\nolinkurl{StepMeasureBridge.lean} & Relates discrete/step approximants to their associated finite measures. \\
\nolinkurl{EarlyMeasureBridge.lean} & Foundational bridge between early convolution approximants and measure formulations. \\
\nolinkurl{StepApproximationLimit.lean} & Coefficient monotonicity, endpoint-corrected step functions, and pointwise convergence by an interval-average squeeze. \\
\nolinkurl{EarlyApproximants.lean} & Definitions and elementary properties of the finite approximants used before the limiting theorem. \\
\nolinkurl{PoissonSummation.lean} & Poisson summation, rapid decay of every real-axis Fourier derivative, and upper-bound-free support specializations for \(a\geq\tfrac12\). \\
\nolinkurl{OriginalPaperSupplement.lean} & Remaining numbered and prose results of the first paper, including exact Taylor formulas and nonanalyticity. \\
\nolinkurl{Paper05442.lean} & Aggregator and coverage audit for arXiv:1702.05442. \\
\addlinespace[0.45em]
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{The arithmetic paper}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{PaperStatements.lean} & Source-numbered propositions, theorems, question, definition, and conjecture for arXiv:1702.06487. \\
\nolinkurl{Paper06487Supplement.lean} & Prose-level and strengthened arithmetic-paper consequences, with corrected hypotheses and bounded/global distinctions. \\
\nolinkurl{Paper06487.lean} & Compact aggregator for the complete arithmetic-paper formalization. \\
\addlinespace[0.45em]
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Finite q-binomial and Thue--Morse algebra}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{HalfQBinomial.lean} & Exact q-binomial coefficients at q=1/2, q-Pascal recurrence, Mersenne normalization, and a finite q-binomial theorem. \\
\nolinkurl{QBinomialCauchy.lean} & One q-Bernstein definition and five denominator-free finite q-Cauchy/q-Bernstein theorems over arbitrary commutative rings, including the compatibility alias \nolinkurl{finiteQPochhammerIn_mul_eq_sum_gaussianBinomial}. \\
\nolinkurl{ThueMorseMixedDifference.lean} & Order-independent translation differences, Boolean-cube expansion, polynomial coefficient extraction, and dyadic Thue--Morse specialization. \\
\nolinkurl{ThueMorseSymmetricDifference.lean} & Centered half-step recurrence and Boolean-cube expansion, sign-free sharp polynomial extraction, strict-degree cancellation, and centered/increasing-grid dyadic Thue--Morse block formulas. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusRawQBinomialFormula.lean} & Raw coefficient-valued finite q-binomial identity before scalar or analytic specialization. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusRawQBinomialScalar.lean} & Scalar evaluation of the raw finite identity. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusQBinomialFormula.lean} & Principal exact Thue--Morse/q-binomial formula for unrestricted natural parameters. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusQBinomialScalarFormula.lean} & Scalar-valued form of the principal q-binomial theorem. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDyadicQBinomialScalar.lean} & Specialization using exact dyadic Fabius values. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusQBinomialTaylor.lean} & Constancy in the common translation indeterminate and identification, after normalization, with \nolinkurl{fabiusReductionSum}. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusGlobalQBinomialSeries.lean} & Real and complex all-real q-series for the signed extension, with pointwise q-independent summands and zero-series behavior on the nonpositive side. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusBinaryReductionSeries.lean} & Exact binary telescope, the scale-zero term distinguishing even and odd unit blocks, a global \(2^{1-N}\) residual bound, uniform convergence on \(\RealNumbers\), and all-real sum identities. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusParityPowerSeries.lean} & All-real parity-power form of the signed extension, including the integer-valued scale-zero exponent, termwise vanishing on the nonpositive side, and the restricted older \(m=1\) form. \\

\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Discrete-limit family}}\\[0.25em]
\nolinkurl{FabiusUniformSpline.lean} & Centered finite Thue--Morse splines; empty-prefix support, block translation, finite-CDF identification, monotonicity/range bounds, and pointwise convergence. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusComplexShiftSpline.lean} & Taylor control of complex shifts of finite splines. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDiscreteLimitToeplitz.lean} & Toeplitz-type averaging principle for normalized finite rows. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDiscreteLimitComplexShift.lean} & Vanishing of complex-shift corrections under the limit scaling. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDiscreteLimitIntegration.lean} & Exact outer q-binomial reindexing, Toeplitz passage for complex-shift splines, identification with the binary/literal global series, and all-real extension by empty-prefix vanishing. \\

\nolinkurl{FabiusSmallArgumentScale.lean} & Filter and scale conversions between x tending to zero and the large logarithmic variable. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusFlatness.lean} & Proof that the Fabius function is smaller than every positive power at zero. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusDecayComparison.lean} & Comparison with exponential-flat functions such as exp(-c/x). \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusLogMainDefect.lean} & Definition and first bounds for the defect after subtracting the quadratic logarithmic main term. \\

\nolinkurl{NegativeLaplaceVerticalOrdinaryJets.lean} & Exact zero-axis vertical logarithmic jets in terms of ordinary negative-Laplace jets, including the exceptional order-zero normalization. \\
\nolinkurl{PeriodicCorrection.lean} & Continuity of the multiplicatively periodic correction by locally uniform convergence. \\
\nolinkurl{PeriodicMean.lean} & Mean value and normalization of the period-one logarithmic correction. \\
\nolinkurl{PeriodicRegularity.lean} & Differentiability and higher regularity of the periodic correction. \\
\nolinkurl{PeriodicFourier.lean} & Fourier coefficients and nonconstancy of the periodic correction. \\
\nolinkurl{MellinBose.lean} & Mellin transform of the Bose kernel and Gamma/zeta factorization. \\
\nolinkurl{MellinFinitePart.lean} & Finite-part continuation after subtraction of singular Laurent terms. \\
\nolinkurl{BoseFinitePartIntegral.lean} & Convergent integral representation for the regularized Bose/Mellin contribution. \\
\nolinkurl{StieltjesConstant.lean} & Sign-fixed first Stieltjes constant, exact real and complex derivatives at one, and the local zeta Taylor/Laurent interface. \\
\nolinkurl{GammaSecondOrder.lean} & Exact \(\Gamma''(1)\), analytic cubic factorization of the quadratic Taylor remainder, and the sharp complex \(O(z^3)\) estimate at zero. \\
\nolinkurl{LaplaceCumulantAsymptotics.lean} & Bell-polynomial inversion of normalized Laplace moments through order four and the conditional corrected \(O(1/n)\) endpoint comparison. \\
\nolinkurl{LaplacePeriodicSecondOrder.lean} & Second-order transform asymptotics retaining the periodic correction. \\

\nolinkurl{BromwichSaddle.lean} & Generic Fourier--Bromwich inversion and exact change to dimensionless saddle coordinates, without contour shifting. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertPhase.lean} & Exact lower-Lambert reference coordinate and its domain/inversion identities. \\

\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertSaddle.lean} & Exact algebraic identities at the lower-Lambert reference radius, without claiming it solves the full residual phase's critical equation. \\

\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertMinorArc.lean} & Modulus loss away from the central saddle window. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusLambertTailFlat.lean} & Exponential tail bounds upgraded to every inverse-power scale. \\

\nolinkurl{FabiusSharpExactReduction.lean} & Exact decomposition into explicit main term, normalized saddle mass, and forward-tail error. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusExplicitSharpTransfer.lean} & Conversion between exact sharp expressions and source-facing elementary forms. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusSharpAsymptoticTransfer.lean} & General transfer lemmas for logarithmic and exponential asymptotic equivalence. \\
\nolinkurl{FabiusSharpAsymptotic.lean} & Correct sharp formula with periodic phase, first error bound, and asymptotic equivalence. \\
